# Zonaras, Epitome Historiarum: our English translation

Translated from Dindorf's Greek (Teubner, 1868–75), one section per chapter, with his chapter numbers. Made with Claude Opus 5.5 from 2 to 8 November 2026 (docs/adr/0003). The Preface and Books 1 to 4, 12 and 13 so far. Where a chapter begins or ends in the middle of a sentence, the English does too, following the Greek.

## praef.1

Someone might fairly say, mocking me: "Your by-work is bigger than your work." For truly I ought, having long since given up public affairs and kept away from their turmoil, having left the world and chosen to live by myself and condemned myself to lifelong exile (for so He who is above us ordered my life, when He broke my bonds by depriving me of those dearest to me, for reasons He knows, painfully for me but surely for my good), to pursue nothing else than what fits the soul for its end and cleanses it of the stains that have sunk into it through wicked deeds, and whatever appeases God for the anger I have roused in Him by transgressing His commandments and ordinances, and so to seek pardon for my failings. But I, through sloth towards what is good, have treated the work carelessly and have spent my zeal on the by-work.

Yet, to say something in my own defence, I did not set out on the undertaking of my own accord. Friends urged me to it, seeing me at leisure and saying: "Use your leisure for a work useful to all, and from this too a reward will be laid up for you with God." For they went on to say that those who have laboured over histories and written up what happened long ago have done it in different ways. Some have set out at length the deeds and stratagems of the men of old, describing battle lines and clashes of armies, camps and palisades and whatever goes with these, and besides this descriptions of places, rough roads, steep and pathless mountains, narrow valleys, strong cities and the towering heights of fortifications, reaching, one might say, to the sky. Others have composed their writings for display, to show what power of writing they had, and so they insert speeches and use a style more digressive, or more rhetorical. With some the ambition has ended in dialogues: when they write about men of other beliefs who have strayed from the right doctrines, they hold disputations with them as though they were present, refute their false belief and bring their proofs from Holy Scripture; or they argue against the Jews and show them to be wilfully perverse if they will not accept our mystery, and use the sayings of the prophets; or again they take a stand against the Greeks, bring their nonsense into the open, mock their myths and produce their own writings to expose their false belief; and here and there they make maxims and moralize.

"These things," they said, "most of those who read the writings of those histories, not to say all, find tiresome and superfluous, both because they need more leisure, and because, even if some who go through the histories have it, the labour over them proves vain, since the long accounts of battles and wars and the marshalling of armies and the like escape the memory, and the speeches and disputations come to nothing for those who read the narrative. For what profit," they said, "will anyone have from knowing what this demagogue said to the people, what the general said to the soldiers, or what that emperor said to the envoys from the Persians, or another to those from the Celts or Scythians, or perhaps to those from Egypt or from the Dacians and Triballi, and what someone else said when he addressed the senate or the common people?"

Some, then, they said, had published their histories in this way, spread out at length and leaning towards display; but others had gone about writing history in the opposite way, using too few words, and so had cheated those who studied their writings of what matters, since they had left out even the more important deeds of the men whose history they told, some of which deserved to be celebrated, saying very little about them, and that little showing neither their character nor their nature and purpose, nor how each of the emperors gained his rule, nor who he was before it, nor from whom he was born. Some of these writings, they added, were also published in a style too plain, and expressed in common words, sometimes even foreign ones, and put together with solecisms, so that those familiar with literature take no pleasure in them.

Saying such things, and finding fault with the historical writings as has been told, they often urged me to take the books in hand, to pass over the many things that either by their number will not settle in the memory or lead to nothing useful, and, cutting down the drawn-out narrative, to publish a short history which would teach those who read it, in summary, the more important things that were done by, or otherwise happened to, the people the history speaks of. Those men, then, kept urging me to strip for such a labour and take up such a work.

## praef.2

But I, partly because I am easy-going in temper (the truth shall be told) and live in idleness, and partly because I saw that the task needed hard work and many books, hesitated and shrank from the undertaking. They, however, kept prodding me and would not let up, and in the end by the persistence of their prodding they roused me to the work. For if the steady fall of a drop can hollow out hard and stubborn rock, all the more will a persistent word knocking at the ears be able to rouse an idle mind and a slack will. Then it also came into my mind that the labour would not be without gain for the good of my soul, and that being busy with the writing would serve it. For when the mind is idle, the spirits of wickedness are more apt to blow on it, to stir up little waves of base thoughts and reckonings, now of one kind and now of another, to swamp it by the persistence of their attacks, and either to make it slip into sin, if not in deeds, then at least in consent, or for the while to raise great surge and turmoil over it. But when the mind is busy with something, it usually escapes the high seas of many reckonings and wicked thoughts. So, both because my friends urged me and to drive off foul or vain thoughts, I applied myself to the task.

That is how I came to undertake the present work. If I do not make the history of each thing recorded fully exact, I ask those who will read it to grant me pardon. For perhaps I shall not be able to obtain all the books I need for the work, since I am now living in this remote place; nor have all the writers of histories written the same things about the same matters, but they disagree in many things, if not in most. And if I should wish to be exact about each thing recorded, and to show what this writer says about it and what another says about the same thing, I too would make my treatment of each matter run to many lines. For these reasons I have decided to pass over the points on which those who have written about the same things contradict one another, unless it is something of great importance whose omission would do harm to what matters in the history.

If the character of the style varies and is not like itself throughout, let no one be surprised, nor blame the work or me, its father. For since I have gathered the histories from many books, in many places I shall use the arrangement and phrasing of those writers; and wherever I paraphrase or rework them myself, I shall fit the form of my language to their character, so that the writing may not seem out of tune with itself.

## praef.3

But before the history, let me say in summary what will be recorded, so that those who read the work may know that they will gain knowledge of many histories, and those the most necessary.

The epitome, then, contains the Octateuch and everything told in it; and the books of Kingdoms are included in it, and with them the Chronicles, and whatever the Hebrew Josephus, in his Antiquities, said beyond the older writers or told in a more digressive or somewhat different way from them; and the history of Ezra, and of the captivities of the Hebrews: the first, that of the ten tribes, which was brought about by the Assyrian Shalmaneser when he took Samaria by siege, carried the nation captive, led them away beyond the Euphrates and settled them there, and moved into Samaria certain nations who were called Cuthaeans; and then the captivity brought on Jerusalem by Nebuchadnezzar, and how the city was left desolate, the temple burned and the whole nation enslaved; and how after seventy years, according to the predictions of the prophets, the people were allowed by Cyrus, who had overthrown the kingdom of the Assyrians, to return to Jerusalem, raise up the city again and renew the temple; who Cyrus was, how he destroyed the kingdom of the Assyrians, and who held the kingdom after him; how, and by whom, the building of the city was hindered, and by whom its rebuilding was permitted again.

It tells of Daniel the prophet, and how he interpreted the dreams of Nebuchadnezzar and the vision of Belshazzar, when that barbarian saw the knuckle of the hand writing on the wall; and of some of the prophet's visions, all of which are recounted with a brief explanation; and of the three children and the wonders done by God for them or through them; of Esther, and how she saved the race of the Hebrews from utter destruction; of Judith, who outwitted Holofernes, killed him and gave his army over to destruction; and of Tobit, and how, struck with blindness and driven from wealth into utter poverty, he by God's providence, because of his good works, recovered his sight and came to have abundant wealth.

The history of Alexander the Macedonian is also summarized here, since the history necessarily mentions him too, among other reasons because after his first defeat of Darius at Issus he visited Jerusalem and gave the high priest special honour; and how he destroyed the kingdom of the Persians and brought it under himself, how long he reigned, and how his kingdom was divided into four realms after his death. It tells what the Jews suffered from Antiochus Epiphanes, who was a descendant of one of Alexander's successors; how the Hasmonaeans stood against him and freed their countrymen from his tyranny, who these were, and how and for how long they led their people; how, after the return to Jerusalem from the captivity under the Assyrians, the Jewish nation was not ruled by kings but governed by the high priests; and that the descendants of the Hasmonaeans already mentioned, holding the honour of the high priesthood and steering the nation, also put the diadem on themselves.

It tells how, when the brothers Hyrcanus and Aristobulus quarrelled over the Jewish kingship, Pompey the Great, then a Roman general, was called in to arbitrate between the brothers, took the city of Jerusalem and made the nation subject to the Romans; how Herod the son of Antipater afterwards held the kingship of the Jews, who he was and where his family came from, all that befell him in his household, and how long his descendants held the kingship; in what way and from what time governors were sent from Rome to Judaea; and what Josephus wrote about our Saviour Jesus Christ, and also about John the Baptist; for what reasons the Jews revolted from obedience to the Romans, how their nation was warred upon by the Romans, by whom Jerusalem was sacked, and how it suffered its last sack, from which there was no recovery.

## praef.4

Since the history has mentioned the Romans and Rome, I thought it necessary to write about them too and to hand down where the Roman nation came from and how it had its beginning, and by whom the land of Italy was settled in earlier times; from where Romulus, who became the founder of Rome, was brought into the light, how his brother Remus was killed, and how Romulus himself then disappeared; how this city was first ruled by kings, and what customs and laws it used; how Tarquin the Proud turned the kingship into a tyranny and was deposed, and how many wars, and of what kind, Rome endured because of his deposition; how the Roman state changed to aristocracy and then to democracy, with consuls and dictators, and then tribunes, managing public affairs; what the consulship was in old times, and what the dictatorship, what the work of the censors was, and how long a term was fixed for each of these offices; what the triumph was like among them, and where the name came from; and what happened in the times of the consuls, though not everything, for want of books that tell of it.

It tells how later the Roman government passed from these into monarchy; how Gaius Julius Caesar first laid claim to it, though not openly, and then, when he had been killed at the tribunal by those who clung to liberty, Augustus Octavius Caesar, who was the murdered Caesar's nephew and had been adopted by him, pursued the murderers of his adoptive father, with Antony helping him in the work; how afterwards he quarrelled with Antony too, defeated him in the sea battle at Actium, then caught him when he fled to Alexandria with Cleopatra, and brought the man to such straits that he killed himself; how great a slaughter of Romans took place in these civil wars, first when Octavius and Antony stood against Brutus and Cassius and the other murderers of Caesar, and then when they fought each other; how Cleopatra, the queen of Egypt, a descendant of the Ptolemies, was taken alive, but killed herself too, as was supposed, by the bite of an asp; and that Octavius, returning to Rome in this way with brilliant triumphs, claimed sole rule and turned the government of the Romans into an exact monarchy.

It tells who ruled as monarchs after him, how each came to power, how and for how long he ruled, and what end of life he met; who in their times, after the revered apostles, adorned the thrones of the four great churches, I mean Rome, Alexandria, Antioch and Jerusalem, and how many of these were found worthy of a martyr's end; how Diocletian and Maximian Herculius raged against the Christians more than the others, and how, laying down their rule, they appointed other Caesars in their place, one of whom was Constantius Chlorus, the father of Constantine the Great, to whom the rule of the Gauls and Britain was assigned; how, when he was dying, he made his first-born son, that Constantine equal to the apostles, successor to his own rule; how Constantine became sole ruler, having overcome the rest, after the sign of the cross had appeared to him in the sky in stars; how he came over to Christ and spread the faith, giving freedom to the preaching; how he built in Byzantium a city named after himself, calling it New Rome, and moved the seat of empire from the elder Rome to it; who reigned in it after him, what each was like in his ways and in his faith, how long he held power, and how he departed this life; who presided over the church in Constantinople, and for how long each, which of them held to the right doctrine and which became heterodox, and how each passed from this world; and under which emperors and patriarchs, and against whom, the councils were assembled.

So the account comes down to the emperors of our own time and ends the work, recalling on the way many other histories, some of them little known. I must begin the work by making its beginning the first beginning, the cause of all things, without beginning and without time, and the bringing forth and birth of the things brought by it from not being into existence and being.

## 1.1

God is a nature that lacks nothing, sufficient to itself for glory and for comprehension, needing no glory from others and comprehensible to no other, unless one should say that its infinity and incomprehensibility are what can be comprehended. In saying God I mean the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit, which are the Godhead, as the great father Gregory the Theologian says. Being so, the Divine first brought into being the angelic powers, heavenly ministers and singers of hymns to the brightness on high, not because it needed them, but so that it might not be moved only by the contemplation of itself, but that the good might be poured out and go forth, and the kindness reach more beings; for this belonged to supreme goodness. Then it also established this world, material and visible.

In the beginning He made the heaven and the earth. Since the earth was covered and unseen, for there was darkness and water lay upon it, God brought forth light. He divided the light and the darkness, and called the light day and named the darkness night; to its beginning He gave the name evening, and to the first dawning of the day morning. And there was evening and there was morning, one day.

I know that it is written in the Little Genesis that on the first day the heavenly powers too were brought into being by the Creator of all, before the rest. But since this Little Genesis is not counted among the books of Hebrew wisdom accepted by the holy fathers, I regard nothing written in it as certain, nor do I agree with its account.

Next He established the firmament, stretching it out in the midst of the waters, so that some were enclosed above it and the rest were poured round the earth below. It was called the firmament because its body is solid, and its nature is not thin or loose like the waters from which it took its substance; and it was also named heaven. These things happened on the second day.

On the third, God commanded the water that covered the whole face of the earth to be gathered together and the dry land to appear. The word became deed, and deep hollows were made and received the waters. He named the land dry, and the gatherings of the waters seas. On this day plants and seeds sprang up from the earth; for the earth, being unadorned, had to be adorned with grasses and countless shoots and flowers and every kind of fruit and the graces of the trees.

The fourth day saw the making of the lights, sun and moon and the other stars. With these the Creator made the heaven splendid, and He set in these lights the light brought forth on the first day, ordering for them their motions, risings, courses and settings, so that the region about the earth might be lit by them, time be counted by their risings and settings, and signs be given through them to those who attend simply to their shining and do not study them too curiously, or, as one would say to put it exactly, to those who foolishly believe that all our affairs are carried round with the motion of the heavenly bodies.

On the fifth of the days, again at God's command, the waters brought forth creeping things with living souls, and birds. Of these, the first love to dwell in the waters and swim in them, while the birds cut through the air, creeping, as it were, aloft through it.

On the sixth the earth was ordered to bring forth the living soul, four-footed beasts and creeping things and wild animals; and all were brought forth according to the divine command. Scripture says that the earth was commanded to bring forth the living soul so that it might show by this the difference between the soul of man and that one. For the soul of the other animals grew from the earth at the command, and being earthy it may reasonably be thought perishable too, since Scripture also says elsewhere: "the soul of every beast is its

## 1.2

blood," and blood certainly perishes. But the soul of man the earth did not send up: the Divine breathed it into Adam as a breath. That is why it is believed to be something divine and immortal.

The sixth of the days also saw man fashioned by the hand of God. For the supreme craftsman first established all things and brought forth the world in order and harmony (which is why it is called a *kosmos*), and prepared it beforehand like a palace for the reception of a king; then He brought forth man himself, as a king, ruled from above, of the things on earth. He did not make him too by a command, but fashioned him with His hands, and made his creation not like that of the others but different and beyond compare. For everything else was brought forth by a word, but man was thought worthy of God's own handiwork. For God took dust from the earth, says the book of Genesis, and fashioned man, and breathed into him a living soul; and because of this soul man is said to have been made in the image of God. For some of the things that belong to the divine nature in its essence are imaged in the soul of man, present in it not by nature but by grace. By nature the Divine is invisible and immortal, incomprehensible and imperishable; and these belong to the soul too, by grace and not by nature. For neither its nature nor its essence can be comprehended or seen, and it is imperishable and immortal. Other marks of the divine nature, too, are seen in the soul by grace.

He named the man He had fashioned Adam. In the Hebrew language this means "red", as Josephus says, because he had been fashioned out of red earth; for such is virgin earth. Having brought forth the world in this way in six days, He rested on the seventh. That is why the Hebrews have made it their custom that the seventh of the days is a day of no work, and why it is called the sabbath; for in the language of the Hebrews sabbath means rest.

God placed Adam in the paradise which He Himself had planted, where there were the tree of life and the tree of knowledge, or, according to Josephus, of understanding. He commanded him to enjoy the rest but to keep away from the tree of knowledge, and told him beforehand that death would be the penalty for transgression. The paradise is watered by a river, and this river goes out from it divided into four. The name of one is Pishon, which means "abundance"; the Greeks call it the Ganges, and it passes through India and empties into the open sea. The second is called Gihon; the name means "the one that flows out from the east", and Josephus says the Greeks call it the Nile. The next is the Tigris, which the same writer says is also called Diglath, and that the name signifies swiftness with narrowness. The last is the Euphrates, or Phora, meaning either "flower" or "scattering". These two both flow into the Red Sea.

God brought all the animals before Adam, and he gave a name to each kind of them. And God created the woman, taking one of Adam's ribs while he slept, and brought her to him. He knew that she had come from himself and named her woman; for among the Hebrews woman is called *essa*, and she was named Eve. So the two of them led a blessed life in paradise, naked in their simplicity and in a life without arts. But they were envied by the serpent, or rather by the dragon of the mind. The serpent came to the woman and advised her to taste the plant of understanding; Josephus says that all the animals spoke the same language then. She was persuaded, and, delighted with the food, persuaded her husband too to share in the fruit. And they knew that they were naked, and made themselves girdles of fig leaves; and Adam and the woman hid from God because of their nakedness.

God asked the reason. Adam pleaded his nakedness and laid the blame on the woman, and she said she had been deceived by the serpent. God first cursed the serpent, deprived it of feet and took away its voice, according to Josephus, ordered it to crawl along the ground, and made it an enemy of the seed of those it had deceived. Then He turned to the woman, made her subject to her husband, and punished her with the pains of childbirth. Then He brought Adam too under a penalty: this was his return to the earth from which he had been taken, eating his bread in sweat, and the earth bringing forth thorns and thistles for him. And He moved them out of paradise, putting garments of skin on them, perhaps the thicker, mortal and resistant flesh. From this comes our toilsome and painful life and our proneness to slip into wickedness.

## 1.3

Children were born to them. The first was Cain; the name means "acquisition", because they had acquired a man. The second was Abel; the name signifies "grief", for there was to be grief for them on his account. Abel was a keeper of a flock and cared for virtue; Cain tilled the ground and was most wicked. When they decided to bring God first-fruits of their own labours, Abel offered the best of the first-born of his flock, while Cain brought whatever came to hand of the fruits of the earth. God regarded the gifts of Abel, but did not regard Cain's offering. Cain, envious at the preference, killed his brother, became accursed by God and was condemned to groan and tremble. Cast out from the face of God, he lived in the land of Nod, where he also had children. He invented measures and weights and was the first to fix boundaries on the land, and so proved a teacher of wickedness. He built a city named after his first-born son Enoch. Enoch's son was Irad, his son Mehujael, his Methushael, whose son was Lamech. Lamech took two wives, named Adah and Zillah, and had seventy-seven children by the two of them. Of these Jabal was a keeper of cattle and loved tending sheep; Jubal invented music and devised the lyre and the harp; and Tubal, born of Zillah, was a smith, that is, a worker in iron and bronze.

To Adam, when he was two hundred and thirty years old, after the death of Abel, another son was born, whom he called Seth. Adam died having lived nine hundred and thirty years. Seth, at two hundred and five years old, fathered Enosh, who first hoped to call on the name of the Lord God, that is, to address the Lord as God. Seth died, having lived nine hundred and twelve years in all and leaving sons and daughters. He pursued virtue and left his descendants as imitators of it. They came to understand the wisdom of the heavenly bodies, and inscribed their discoveries on two pillars, one made of stone and the other of brick, so that they might be kept for those who came after; for Adam had foretold that there would be a destruction of the whole world by fire and by water.

From Enosh were born Kenan and other sons and daughters, and he died having lived nine hundred and five years. From Kenan sprang Mahalalel and other children, and he died having lived a span of nine hundred and ten years. The son of Mahalalel was Jared, with others, and Mahalalel ended his life at eight hundred and ninety-five years. Jared was the father of Enoch and of other children besides, and died having lived nine hundred and sixty-two years. The son of Enoch was Methuselah, with others. Enoch pleased the Lord and was taken away and was not found, being three hundred and sixty-five years old. The son of Methuselah was Lamech, with others, and he died having lived nine hundred and sixty-nine years. Noah was born to Lamech, and Lamech died having lived seven hundred and fifty

## 1.4

-three years. Three sons were born to Noah: Shem, Ham and Japheth.

The sons of God saw the daughters of men and took wives for themselves. By sons of God Scripture means the descendants of Seth, because they pursued virtue, just as it calls the virtuous sons of the Most High; and by daughters of men it means the women descended from Cain's offspring. Josephus says that angels took the daughters of men, calling them so, I think, because they pleased God and practised the angels' way of life. When men had multiplied they turned to wickedness, and children were born to them who were insolent and reckless because of their confidence in their strength. God was angered with them and cut short their long life, limiting the life of men to a hundred and twenty years.

But Noah found favour before God. So when the land was turned into sea by a rain lasting many days and violent, the whole human race perished, and Noah alone was saved, by God's providence, in a wooden chest which he built square, three hundred cubits long, fifty broad and thirty deep. He went into it with his sons and their mother and their wives, putting in every kind of seed and animals of every kind to preserve their kinds, two of each of the unclean and seven of each kind of the clean. Noah was the tenth counting from Adam, and the flood was brought on the earth after one thousand six hundred and fifty-six years. For forty days rain poured violently down on the earth, so that the water rose fifteen cubits above the higher mountains. When the rain stopped and the water had been falling for a hundred and fifty days, the chest grounded on a mountain of Armenia in the seventh month. Then Noah opened it and let out a raven, which did not come back. Then he let a dove go, and it returned again. When seven days had passed he let it go once more, and it came back carrying a twig of olive. From this he knew that the water had left the earth. Noah went out, with his wife, his sons and their wives, and the animals that had been with them in the ark, and he sacrificed to God.

Josephus says that the place in Armenia is called the Place of Descent, and that remains of the chest are shown there. He says that many of those who wrote the histories of the barbarians mention the flood and the ark: the Chaldaean Berossus, Hieronymus the Egyptian, who wrote the antiquities of Phoenicia, and Mnaseas of Damascus.

God promised never again to bring such a disaster on the earth. He commanded that men might use animals for food, but must not eat flesh with the blood of its life, and must abstain from the murder of men. As a sign that He would never again flood the earth with rain He set the bow in the cloud, meaning the rainbow by the bow. Noah lived three hundred and fifty years after the flood, and died at nine hundred and fifty years old.

Josephus has said this about the long lives of the men of that time: "Let no one, comparing the present life and the shortness of our years, think false what is recorded of the ancients. For, being dear to God and made by God Himself, and because their food was better suited to a longer time, they reasonably lived so great a number of years; and then also because of the usefulness of what they were discovering, astronomy and geometry, which they could not have understood and taught with certainty had they not lived six hundred years; for the great year is completed in that many."

## 1.5

The sons of Noah, Shem, Ham and Japheth, came down from the mountains and settled in the plains. Noah planted a vineyard, drank of the wine, became drunk and lay uncovered. Ham saw his father's nakedness and told his brothers, as though mocking his father. They put a garment on their backs and covered their father's nakedness, walking backwards and not looking at their father. So when Noah woke from his drink he rewarded them with blessings, and cursed Ham.

The plain where the sons of Noah lived was called Shinar. Many children were born to each, and as they went on through the generations their descendants multiplied. A certain Nimrod, a grandson of Ham, who was the first giant seen on the earth, bold and strong of hand, took the lead over the others and turned affairs into a tyranny. He proposed that they build a tower, built of baked brick and bitumen. God, seeing them so mad, made them speak different languages and unable to understand one another's speech, by dividing the tongues. The place where they built the tower is now called Babylon because of the confusion of their speech; for the Hebrews call confusion *babel*. From then on they were scattered by their difference of language, so that every mainland and island was filled with them.

When the descendants of Noah were scattered, the nations were formed from them and named after them. Japheth fathered seven sons, who went out from the mountains Taurus and Amanus, in Asia as far as the river Tanais, and in Europe as far as Gadeira; and nations were named after them. From Gomer came the Gomarians, who are now called Galatians. Magog named those descended from him Magogians; they are now called Scythians. From Javan the Ionians and all the Greeks were named, and from Madai the Madaeans, who are now called Medes. Tubal named those descended from him Thobelians; these are the Iberians. Meshech began the race of the Meschenians, who are thought to be the Cappadocians. Tiras called those descended from him Tirasians, who were later renamed Thracians. Of the descendants of Japheth, the people called Rhegines came from Ashkenaz as Ashkenazians; the Riphathaeans, as the Paphlagonians were formerly called, are believed to draw their descent from Riphath; and the Thorgamaeans, who were later renamed Phrygians, claim Togarmah as their forefather. Elishah became the founder of the Elishaeans, who are the Aeolians. Tarshish was the forefather of the Tarshians, for in old times the Cilicians were called Tarshians; that is why the most notable of their cities is called Tarsus, the *th* having been changed to *t*. So Josephus has written about Tarsus. Others say that Perseus, when he visited the land of the Cilicians and wished to found a city, struck the ground with the flat (*tarsos*) of his foot as he showed the builders the site of the city, and from this the city was named Tarsus. Kittim settled the island of Kittima: this is Cyprus, so named by the Greeks after their goddess Aphrodite, whom they call Cypris.

The sons of Ham held the land from Syria and the mountains Amanus and Lebanon, and seized all the parts of it that faced the sea, as far as the Ocean. From Cush came the Cushites, who are the Ethiopians. Mizraim became the forefather of the Mestraeans; so the Egyptians are called, and the land of Egypt is named Mestre. Put settled Libya and called the people of the land Phutians and the land itself Phute; its name was afterwards changed to Libya from Libys, a son of Mizraim. Canaan settled the land later called Judaea, named it Canaan, and its people Canaanites. The sons of Mizraim held the land from Gaza to Egypt; only the portion of Philistim kept his name, which the Greeks have called Palestine. Sidonius, the first-born son of Canaan, built a city in Phoenicia and called it Sidon. Amathi founded the city of Amathe, which the Macedonians renamed Epiphania after one of the Ptolemies called Epiphanes.

To Shem the son of Noah five sons were born, who live in Asia from the Euphrates as far as the Ocean by India. Elam left the Elymaeans, who were the founders of the Persians. Asshur founded the city of Ninus and named those under him Assyrians. Arphaxad was the forefather of the Chaldaeans. Aram named after himself the Aramaeans, who were later called Syrians. Lud produced the Ludians, who afterwards were called Lydians. Of the four sons of Aram, one settled Trachonitis and Damascus, which lies between Palestine and Coele Syria; another settled Armenia; and the third the Bactrians.

The son of Arphaxad was Kenan, and Kenan's son was Shelah. He was the father of Eber. Eber fathered Peleg, so called because of the division of the earth; for when the division of the men of that time took place, then too the dividing of the earth was born, and the Hebrews call division *peleg*. The nation was called Hebrews from the beginning after Eber. The son of Peleg was Reu, Reu was the father of Serug, Serug fathered Nahor, from Nahor came Terah, and from Terah Abraham.

## 1.6

This great Abraham was the forefather of the Hebrew race and the patriarch of its clans. He was a Chaldaean by race, the tenth from Noah, two hundred and ninety-two years after God had flooded the whole earth with rain. Though his ancestors were impious, he was the first to know one God, the creator of all things, understanding the maker from the things made, and he said that honour must be given to Him alone. When the Chaldaeans rose against him for this, he moved away from there at the command of the God he had come to know, and took possession of the land of Canaan. When famine pressed on Canaan he moved to Egypt; and, admired for his understanding, he handed on arithmetic and astronomy to the Egyptians, who were ignorant of them. It is said that these went from the Chaldaeans to Egypt, and from there to the Greeks.

When the Assyrians attacked the people of Sodom, among whom Lot, Abraham's nephew and Sarah's brother, was living, many of the Sodomites fell and the rest were taken captive, and Lot was taken captive with the Sodomites. When Abraham learned this, he pursued the Assyrians with three hundred and eighteen of his servants and three friends, and rescued the Sodomites and Lot. As he returned from the pursuit the king of the city of Solyma received him; he was called Melchizedek, which means, put into the Greek language, "righteous king". For Solyma was then the name of the city of Jerusalem; so ancient was the city, and it was allotted precedence in age over all others.

God, pleased with Abraham's virtue, promised him a reward for it. When Abraham said that every kindness seemed to him without joy since he had no one to inherit it (for he still had no lawful offspring, because Sarah's womb was barren), God promised to give him a son as well, and that from him a great nation would come, equal in number to the stars. And He commanded that he and the males under him should cut the foreskin, so that his race might not mix with the other nations; and they were circumcised.

So a son was born to Abraham by Sarah when he was a hundred years old, and God ordered him to call the boy Isaac. The name means "laughter", either because Sarah smiled when God declared that she would bear a child, since the promise was hard to hope for, as she was past the age and did not expect to bear (she was ninety years old when she bore Isaac), or because the child was a cause of laughter to his parents, that is, of gladness and joy. When the boy was born his father circumcised him on the eighth day, and that is why the Jews have made it their custom to be circumcised so. The Arabs are accustomed to circumcise their children after the thirteenth year, because Ishmael their forefather was circumcised at that age; for he was thirteen years old when God commanded circumcision to Abraham. His name means "heard by God", since God listened to Abraham's prayer and gave him a son. Abraham fathered him by a maidservant whom Sarah put to bed with him. For since she then had no offspring of her own, she asked her husband to lie with her own maidservant, called Hagar, an Egyptian by race; and when she bore Ishmael, Sarah took the child as her own. But when she had borne Isaac, out of jealousy she condemned Hagar and her son to exile.

When Isaac had grown to manhood (according to the Hebrew Josephus, who says that Isaac was then twenty-five years old), God put Abraham to the test. He called him and ordered him to bring his son as a sacrifice, to take his only, beloved son to one of the mountains and offer him as a whole burnt offering. Abraham did not refuse, but took the boy, led him up to the mountain appointed to him, shared the divine oracle with him, and urged him to bear the sacrifice nobly, since God commanded it. Isaac obeyed and offered himself willingly to be sacrificed, saying that it was not right to resist the will of God and of his father. So Josephus records it. Then Abraham was commanded to spare his son, and a ram appeared of its own accord in his place for the burnt offering.

After this Sarah died, a hundred and twenty-seven years old, and Abraham married another wife, called Keturah, by whom six sons were born to him. When Isaac was about forty his father betrothed to him Rebekah from Mesopotamia, the daughter of Bethuel, the son of Nahor, Abraham's brother. Then Abraham died, having lived a hundred and seventy-five years. Isaac married Rebekah and had twin sons by her. On the elder, thick hair grew over his whole body, and so he was called Esau because of his hairiness. The younger's hand held his brother's heel as he leapt out first from his mother's womb, and so he was named Jacob; the name means among the Hebrews "the one who grasps the heel".

## 1.7

After this Isaac grew old and lost his sight. He called Esau, for he was devoted to him because of his birthright, and ordered him to hunt and prepare a meal for him, "so that," he said, "when I have eaten I may bless you before I die." Esau went out to hunt. But Rebekah, who loved Jacob more, called him, told him what his father had ordered Esau, and urged him to hurry to the flock and bring her two tender kids, so that from them she might prepare for the old man such dishes as she knew he liked, and he might eat and bless him. He did as his mother told him. When the dishes were ready, she wrapped the boy's arms in the skins of the kids and gave him the meal to take to his father. Isaac perceived by the voice that the one coming to him was Jacob, called him very close and felt his hands. Since God had surely judged Esau unworthy of the blessing, Isaac thought that his first-born stood beside him (perhaps he was deceived by the thickness of the hair), and he ate and blessed Jacob.

Esau came back too, brought his father a meal and asked for the blessing. Isaac recognized the trick and said: "Your brother came and took your blessing." Esau lamented and begged to be blessed, for, he said, surely his father had more than one blessing. Isaac, so entreated, set out to bless him; but since it did not seem good to the divine Spirit that spoke in him that Esau should obtain a blessing, as he was not worthy of it, Isaac's voice was shaped into a curse for him instead of a blessing. Esau was hostile to his brother because of this; and Jacob, fearing his brother, went away to Mesopotamia on his mother's advice.

On the way, in his sleep, he was granted the famous vision of the ladder, one end of which rested on the earth and the other on heaven, and he was taught the future by the Higher Power. He called the place where he saw the vision Bethel, which means in Greek "the hearth of God". Arriving at Haran, he lodged with Laban, the brother of his mother Rebekah, received hospitality from him and was entrusted with the care of the flocks. Laban had two daughters, the elder called Leah and the other Rachel. Jacob was roused to love for the younger, who was more lovely in looks than her sister, and he spoke to Laban about her. Laban agreed to betroth Rachel to Jacob if he would serve him for seven years. The lover consented, the time passed, the father-in-law prepared the wedding; but at night he put the elder of his daughters to bed with Jacob. When Jacob learned in the morning that he had been deceived, he accused his father-in-law of wrongdoing. Laban promised him Rachel too after the service of another seven years. Jacob agreed to serve again, enslaved to love, and after another seven years he lay with Rachel too.

Leah had children by Jacob. She called the first-born Reuben, because he came to her by the mercy of God; for she was stung that her husband loved her sister, and she expected to be held in more honour if she conceived. She named the second Simeon, which means that God had listened to her. After him Levi was born; the name means "the one who confirms". After him she bore Judah; the name signifies thanksgiving. Rachel had no child, and fearing that she would be put in the shade because she was childless, she begged her husband to lie with her maidservant. He was persuaded and lay with Bilhah, and Bilhah bore Jacob a son, and the boy was called Dan; the Greeks would say "judged by God". By the same woman Naphtali was born next, "the well-contriving", as it were, because she had devised a counter to her sister's fruitfulness. Leah was jealous at this, and she too put her own maidservant Zilpah to bed with Jacob. She too bore a son, Gad, whom one might call "lucky". After him she bore another, Asher, who might be called "the one who makes blessed", since he brought Leah renown and blessing through her many children.

When Reuben, Leah's son, brought her mandrake apples, Rachel asked her to share them with her, and offered her sister in return Jacob's bed for that night; for that night had been allotted to Rachel. So Jacob lay with Leah for this reason, and the woman bore two more sons: Issachar, the name meaning "born from hire", and Zebulun, which means "the one pledged by goodwill towards her"; and a daughter called Dinah. A son, Joseph, was born to Rachel too; the name signifies the addition of someone yet to be born.

## 1.8

For twenty years Jacob tended sheep for his father-in-law; then, wishing to leave, he was not allowed. So he ran away secretly with his wives, with the maidservants and the children, taking along his property and the cattle that had been allotted to him as wages for his shepherding. Rachel also carried off her father's idols, not to honour them, but so that she might have a refuge in them if her father pursued and caught them. When Laban overtook them on the seventh day, God ordered him in a dream to make peace with Jacob. In the morning the two talked, and when Jacob showed that he had done no wrong, Laban accused him about the gods of his fathers. Jacob, knowing nothing about them, let him search, and he searched. Rachel put the idols in the saddle of the camel that carried her and sat on it herself, saying that her monthly flow troubled her. Laban, thinking that his daughter would never approach his gods in such a state, passed her by. He went home, and Jacob, fearing his brother, sent ahead to tell him of his return and sent him gifts.

When night came on, he met an apparition, wrestled with it and seemed to prevail. Perceiving that it was an angel of God, he asked it to tell him what his lot would be. It said that he ought to consider his victory over a divine angel a sign of great blessings, and bade him be called Israel. Others interpret this as "the mind that sees God", but Josephus says it means "the one who stood against a divine angel". Jacob called the place Penuel, that is, "the face of God". Since in the struggle with the angel he suffered a pain about the broad sinew, he renounced eating it, and because of him it is forbidden to his whole race.

When Esau met his brother, they came together and embraced. Then Esau went away, and Jacob came to Shechem. While the Shechemites were holding a festival, his daughter Dinah went into the city to look at the sights of the festival. Shechem, the son of the king of the city, saw her and violated her by force. Then his father begged Jacob to give Dinah to Shechem in marriage, but Jacob would not consent. Simeon and Levi, the girl's brothers by the same mother, went in by night while the people of the city were feasting for the festival and heavy-headed, and killed every male of age, and the king and his son. So Josephus says. But the book of Genesis tells of these things more plausibly. It says that when Hamor, the father of Shechem, asked that Dinah be married to his son, Simeon and Levi said: "If all the males in the city are circumcised, we will give our sister to your son"; and that they accepted the proposal and were all circumcised. On the third day after the circumcision, when the circumcised men were in a bad way and in pain from the inflammation, they attacked them and killed them all, without Jacob's knowledge. When he was angry at this, God told him to take heart.

Jacob came to Bethel, where he had seen the dream, and sacrificed. Going on, he buried Rachel, who had died in childbirth, at Ephrath, and he called her child Benjamin, because of the pain his mother had suffered at his birth. Coming to Hebron, a city of the Canaanites, he found his father Isaac still alive, though Rebekah had died before. Isaac died soon after and was buried with his wife in the family tomb, having lived a hundred and eighty-five years.

Esau ruled Idumaea, calling the country after himself. For he was called Edom, because once, coming back hungry from hunting and finding his brother had prepared lentil pottage for his own food, he asked him to give it to him; and Jacob made him sell him his birthright in exchange for the lentils. Esau gave it up to him with oaths because of his hunger. From this he was called Edom, because of the redness of the lentils, for they were very much of that colour; and among the Hebrews red is called *edom*.

## 1.9

Jacob loved Joseph, the son of Rachel, more than his other sons, both for the fine form of his body and for the virtue of his soul. His father's affection and the dreams he saw moved his brothers to envy him. One of the dreams was this: at harvest time he seemed to be reaping with his brothers, binding sheaves and setting them down, and his own stood still, while his brothers' ran up and bowed down to his. In the other the sun and the moon and the rest of the stars seemed to bow down to him. He told these visions to his father, with his brothers present, and asked to learn their meaning. His father foretold good fortune for the boy, and that a time would come when he would be honoured and bowed down to by his parents and brothers. This grieved Joseph's brothers, and they looked askance at the lad.

When Jacob's sons were grazing the flocks at Shechem, their father sent Joseph to see how they were. When they saw him they set out to kill him. But Reuben advised them not to kill their brother with their own hands, but to throw him into the pit nearby, so that he might die there, and so the evil would be less. The young men agreed to this, and Reuben took the lad and gently let him down into the pit. Judah, seeing some Arab merchants, advised his brothers to sell Joseph to them. This was agreed, and they sold him to the merchants for twenty minas, at seventeen years old. Reuben came to the pit at night, to bring Joseph out and save him without his brothers' knowledge; when he did not find him, he mourned and blamed his brothers. When they told him what had been done, he stopped his mourning. They stained the lad's tunic with goat's blood, came to their father and said that they had neither seen Joseph nor knew how he had perished, but had found this tunic bloodied and torn. Jacob, mourning for the lad, sat in sackcloth.

Joseph was bought from the merchants by Potiphar, an Egyptian, in charge of the cooks of King Pharaoh, and he held him in honour. His master's wife fell in love with him for his beauty and his ability and spoke to him of lying with her, but he turned the request aside. She, terribly besieged by love, made a second attempt, and, despairing of persuasion, wished to use force. When the young man left his cloak behind and leapt away, she sat downcast and distraught. When her husband came, she accused Joseph and showed the cloak, as if he had left it when he tried to force her. Potiphar threw Joseph into the prison for criminals, where Pharaoh's cupbearer and his chief baker had also been shut up in his anger. Joseph became familiar with them and was asked to tell them the meaning of their dreams. The cupbearer dreamed he saw a vine, from which three branches grew, with great ripe clusters hanging from them; he squeezed these into a cup and brought it to Pharaoh, and the king took it. Joseph explained that the dream meant good for him, for within the next three days he would be restored to his former service; and he asked him to remember him when he had prospered. The chief baker said that he seemed to carry three baskets on his head, two full of loaves and the third of meat and dishes of all kinds, and these were snatched away by birds swooping down. Joseph told him too that the three baskets signified three days, and that on the third day he would be hanged and become food for the flesh-eating birds. It turned out for both as Joseph had explained.

## 1.10

Then God sent Pharaoh visions in two dreams, and the meaning of both. He remembered the visions but forgot the interpretations. So he called together the wise men of the Egyptians, told them the dreams and demanded their meaning; and when they were at a loss he was angry. Then the cupbearer, though not before, remembered Joseph, and told Pharaoh about him. So he was brought at once. Pharaoh said: "Young man, tell me the judgement of the dreams I have seen. I seemed to see seven well-fed cows come up out of the river and go to the marsh; and seven others, thin and wasted with hunger, came out of the marsh and met them, and the first and fat ones were eaten by the second, and these stayed as thin as before. Again I see seven ears of grain growing from one root, flourishing and bowed down by the weight of the grain. Close by them I seemed to see as many ears, withered for lack of dew and ugly to look at, which consumed the fruitful and ripe ones."

Joseph said: "Though the dreams are two, O king, they have one meaning for the future. The cows, an animal that labours at the plough, eaten by the worse ones, and the flourishing ears consumed by the withered, foretell a famine for as many years in your country, after it has first had abundance of crops for an equal number of years, so that the plenty of those years will be used up by the years after. But if you store up the produce of the first prosperity for the years of barrenness, you will make the misfortune unfelt by the Egyptians."

Pharaoh, amazed at Joseph's wisdom in the interpretation and the advice, said: "You yourself shall be the judge and counsellor of these things, and the steward of what has been planned." And he gave him his own authority, so that he used Pharaoh's seal, wore purple and rode in a chariot. Joseph was then thirty years old. Pharaoh called him Psothomphanech, a name which means "finder of hidden things". He also gave him a wife called Asenath, the daughter of the chief of the priests at Heliopolis. By her children were born to him before the famine. The elder was Manasseh, which signifies "forgetful", because he had found forgetfulness of his misfortunes; the younger Ephraim, which means "restoring", because the freedom of his forefathers had been restored to him.

Then came the time of want. The famine prevailed not only over Egypt but also over the land where Jacob lived. So he sent his sons to Egypt to buy grain, keeping only Benjamin with him, as the youngest. They came before Joseph. He, unrecognized, recognized his brothers, and said they had come as spies. They told him where they had come from, that they were the sons of one father who was still living, and that there was another, younger brother with their father. Joseph said they would give him proof of the truth if they brought their younger brother to him. So he gave them grain, putting the money secretly into each one's sack, and kept Simeon as a hostage for their return.

The sons came back to Jacob bringing the grain and reported what had happened, and the keeping of Simeon grieved him, and he would not consent to give Benjamin to be taken to Egypt. But when the grain had been used up, since they could not go to Egypt otherwise unless they brought Benjamin too, their father yielded to necessity and gave him, and twice the price of the grain, and gifts for Joseph. When they came to Egypt they presented the gifts to him. Seeing Benjamin he was overcome, and went out so as not to be seen weeping. Then he took his brothers to dinner and favoured Benjamin with double portions; and he ordered the steward of the grain to give them grain, to put the price in their sacks, and to put a silver cup into Benjamin's load as well. When this had been done, the brothers left, taking Simeon. As they went, horsemen surrounded them, charging them with the theft of the cup, and searching they found the cup in Benjamin's load. The horsemen led Benjamin to Joseph, and his brothers tore their clothes, weeping, and followed. Joseph reproached them as ungrateful, set the rest free and was content to keep Benjamin alone. They were at a loss. But Judah spoke and said much to move him to pity, and so brought Joseph to betray his feeling. No longer able to feign anger, he made himself known to his brothers, and acknowledged his gratitude to them as having helped bring about what God had planned for him. "Go," he said, "and tell this to our father; take him and all our kin and move here, for there will be famine for five years more." With these words he embraced his brothers and honoured them with gifts.

## 1.11

They went away. When Jacob learned about Joseph, he set out to him at once, bringing all those with him; they were seventy-five. When he was already near Egypt Joseph came out to meet him, and he almost fainted for joy. When Pharaoh heard that Jacob and those with him had come to Egypt, he was glad, and when Jacob came to him he greeted him. Learning that those with him were shepherds, he allowed them to live at Heliopolis.

As the famine grew worse, Joseph sold the grain to the Egyptians for money. When that ran out they bought it with their cattle, and then they gave up their land too so that they might be fed. So Pharaoh became master of all property except that of the priests, who kept their land. When the famine had at last eased, Joseph gathered the Egyptians, each into his own city, gave them the land and urged them to work it diligently, paying a fifth of the crops to Pharaoh.

Jacob, after spending seventeen years in Egypt, fell ill and died, having foretold how those of his line would live in Canaan. He prayed that his other sons might have a store of blessings, and spoke in praise of Joseph, because he had not held a grudge against his brothers; and he commanded his sons to take Joseph's sons Ephraim and Manasseh among themselves and divide Canaan with them. When Joseph set his sons before Jacob to be blessed, he placed the elder at his right hand and the younger at his left. But Jacob crossed his hands as he laid them on the boys' heads, and gave his right hand to the younger and his left to the elder. When Joseph told his father that the hands should not be laid on so, he would not be persuaded to change the position of his hands. For he did not do it, as Joseph supposed, without knowing what he was doing, his mind harmed by deep old age; but as a prophet he was hinting at the future, prefiguring the saving cross, and showing that the new people, that is, the one from the nations, would be reckoned on the right hand by God and preferred to the elder, I mean, of course, the Jewish people.

Jacob died having lived a hundred and forty-seven years. Joseph, with Pharaoh's leave, went to Hebron and buried his father lavishly. When his brothers were afraid to return with him, in case he should bear a grudge and repay them, he persuaded them to suspect nothing. He too died, having lived a hundred and ten years, after charging that when those of his race left Egypt they should carry out his bones too and take them to Canaan.

## 1.12

The Egyptians, seeing the Israelites flourishing and having forgotten Joseph, devised all kinds of hardships against them. They ordered them to cut the river into very many canals, to build walls for the cities and raise embankments, so that by them the river might be kept from turning into marsh, and to build pyramids; and they wore the Hebrews out with these tasks. They spent a long time under these afflictions. Then one of the sacred scribes told Pharaoh that at that time someone would be born to the Israelites who, when he grew up, would humble the Egyptians and raise up his countrymen. Pharaoh, afraid, ordered that every male born to the Hebrews should be thrown into the river and destroyed, that parents of children who did not do this should be put to death with their whole family, and that the Egyptian midwives should watch the births of the Hebrew women.

So things stood. But a man of good family was distressed at this, for his wife was pregnant, and he prayed to God; and God told him in his sleep not to despair. "For the child," He said, "whom the Egyptians suspect will be yours, and he will be saved and brought up without his plotters' knowledge, and will free his countrymen from slavery among the Egyptians, and win everlasting memory. His brother shall be priest to me, with his descendants, for ever." So his wife bore him a male child without the guards' knowledge, and the baby was nursed by his parents for three months. Then, afraid, they entrusted his safety to God: they made a woven basket, smeared it round with bitumen, put the baby in it and let it go down the river. The child's sister Miriam watched what would happen.

Thermuthis, Pharaoh's daughter, playing by the banks of the river, saw the basket, sent for it and took it. Seeing the child, she loved him for his beauty and his size. Egyptian women were brought to nurse him, but he would not take their breast. Miriam, as if she happened to be there, said: "Queen, if a Hebrew woman were called, perhaps the baby would take to one of his own race." "Call her," she said. She brought the mother, and Thermuthis gave her the nursing of the child for pay, and named him from what had happened; for the Egyptians call water *mo*, and those saved from water *yses*, and she put the two words together and used them as the baby's name. Moses was the seventh from Abraham: he was the son of Amram, Amram's father was Kohath, Kohath's Levi, Levi was Jacob's son, Jacob was born to Isaac, and Isaac sprang from Abraham.

When the boy was three years old, his stature and the fineness of his form showed a more divine grace, and his understanding grew far beyond his years. So Thermuthis, having no child of her own, adopted him and brought him to her father. He hugged the boy to his breast and in affection put his diadem on him. The boy, as if in childishness, threw it to the ground and trod on it. Seeing this, the sacred scribe who had foretold that someone would be born among the Hebrews to humble the rule of the Egyptians cried out: "This is that child; kill him and take away the Egyptians' fear." But Thermuthis snatched him away first, and God made Pharaoh slow to murder.

When Moses reached manhood, after such a beginning, it became clear to the Egyptians what his virtue was. The Ethiopians, neighbours of Egypt, were plundering their country, and the Egyptians marched against them; and they were defeated, and the Ethiopians, despising them, harried the whole of Egypt. At the bidding of an oracle Pharaoh asked his daughter for Moses, now grown, to be his general against the Ethiopians. She gave him, after making her father swear to do him no harm. Moses, entrusted with the war, led the army against the enemy not by the usual road but by one untrodden because of the multitude of venomous snakes; and there he showed his understanding. He made baskets and filled them with ibises (Egypt breeds many, and tame), and brought them along that road; and ibises are enemies of these venomous creatures. He used them as champions going ahead of the army against the snakes. He fell on the Ethiopians when they had no warning, defeated them and took not a few of their cities. They were driven together into Saba, the royal city of Ethiopia, which Cambyses later named Meroe after his sister of that name, and were besieged; and the city was hard to take by siege. Tharbis, the daughter of the ruler of the Ethiopians, fell in love with Moses and sent to him about marriage; he consented if she would hand the city over to him. The deed came before the words, and Moses celebrated the marriage and led the Egyptians back to their own land.

But they hated him even though he had saved them, and stirred Pharaoh against him. Pharaoh, persuaded by the sacred scribes, and because of the killing of the Egyptian who was flogging the Hebrew, as told in the book of Genesis (though Josephus passed this over), set about putting Moses to death. Though the roads were guarded, Moses slipped away unseen through the desert, went to the city of Midian, and was received by the priest of the city, who had two names and was called both Reuel and Jethro. He gave his daughter Zipporah to Moses in marriage and made him the shepherd of his flocks.

## 1.13

While Moses was grazing the flocks by Mount Sinai, he was granted the vision of the bush, which fire was burning but did not burn up. Then he also heard a voice from it, which ordered him not to come further, since the place was holy, foretold what would be, and bade him go to Egypt, to free his countrymen from their suffering there and lead them out of Egypt, and commanded him to make sacrifices in that place. These things Moses heard from God out of the fire. He was at a loss how he would either persuade his own people or force Pharaoh to allow their departure. God promised to make these things very easy for him, and as a pledge ordered him to throw his staff on the ground; when he threw it, it became a serpent, and when he took hold of it again it was a staff. Told to put his right hand in his bosom, he brought it out white, like chalk in colour, and then it was restored to its former look. Told to take some of the nearby water and pour it on the ground, he saw that what was poured out had become blood. So he was told to take heart at these things and delay no longer.

Taking his wife and his sons by her, Gershom, which means "into the foreign land", and Eleazar, which signifies that he had had the God of his fathers as his ally, he set out for Egypt. As he drew near, his brother Aaron met him. So the two came to Pharaoh, told him what God had commanded and asked him not to act against the divine will. When he mocked them, they showed the signs in deed, and the rod, thrown down, became a snake. When the Egyptian enchanters did the same and turned their own staffs into serpents, Moses threw his staff on the ground again, and, turned into a snake, it ate up the Egyptians' staffs, which looked like serpents; then he took it back when it had changed into its own shape.

As Pharaoh hardened his heart, the waters were turned into blood. Then frogs covered the whole land of Egypt. After these, gnats came up from the earth. Pharaoh's enchanters seemed to work the other wonders too, but they could not produce gnats. So they said to Pharaoh, "This is the finger of God"; but he was still unyielding. Then came the dog-fly, and after it the destruction of the cattle. Then, when Moses scattered soot from a furnace into the air, sores and blisters broke out on beasts and men; again hail rained down, and after it came the locust; after these, darkness covered the Egyptians for three days; and last the destruction of the first-born was brought on them.

For as Pharaoh hardened his heart, God commanded Moses to prepare a sacrifice for the fourteenth day of the month which the Hebrews call Nisan, the Egyptians Pharmouthi, the Macedonians Xanthicus and the Greeks April. When the fourteenth came, they all sacrificed, smeared the doorposts of their houses with the blood of the victim, dined on the flesh and burned what was left. From then on the Hebrews have held a festival at this season and sacrificed, calling the festival Pascha. Some say that Pascha means "crossing", but Josephus says "passing over", because on that night God passed over the Hebrews and struck the Egyptians with the destruction of the first-born. So the Egyptians gathered and begged Pharaoh to let the Hebrews go, and he ordered them to leave.

They went out. The number of those departing who were of military age alone was six hundred thousand. They went out on the fifteenth day of the month Xanthicus, by the moon, four hundred and thirty years after Abraham came to Canaan, and two hundred and fifteen years after Jacob's settlement in Egypt. Moses was then in the eightieth year of his life, and Aaron was three years older. They also took Joseph's bones with them, fulfilling the man's command.

## 1.14

So they went away, but the Egyptians repented and took up arms and pursued them. They had six hundred chariots, fifty thousand horsemen and two hundred thousand infantry. Their plight, caught in a narrow place, drove the Hebrews to despair, and they were about to stone Moses. He urged them to follow him and led them to the sea; standing by it and calling on God, he struck the sea with his staff, and it was driven back and left the ground bare for the Hebrews, and they crossed in haste. Seeing this, the Egyptians too pursued, putting their cavalry in front. When the Hebrews had already come safe to the land opposite, the sea poured over the Egyptians, as Moses struck the water again with his rod to turn it back and joined its divided parts. All the Egyptians in the waters perished. The Hebrews sang hymns and rejoiced, and Moses composed a song to God in hexameter measure, as Josephus says. The Hebrews took the arms of the Egyptians, which had been thrown up by the sea with their bodies by the wind, and armed themselves with them.

Moses led them to Mount Sinai, to sacrifice there the offerings for their deliverance, as he had been commanded. As they were led there, the scarcity of food and the lack of water distressed them. In this state they came to a place where there was a well whose water could not be drunk because of its bitterness. The book of Exodus names the place Marah, but Josephus writes that it was called Mar by the Hebrews, saying that the Hebrews call what is bitter *mar*. They murmured against their leader; and he, after first praying to God, took a piece of wood, threw it into the well and made the water sweet. Josephus seems not to believe that the sweetening of the water was a miracle, but thinks it was sweetened naturally, for he says that Moses threw the wood into the water and ordered the well to be drawn out; they laboured at it, and the water, worked and cleansed by the constant strokes, was now drinkable.

The lack of food he remedied, after praying to God, with quails. For a multitude of quails flew over the camp of the Hebrews, and they caught and ate them. After these God rained manna on them, like honey in sweetness, in size like coriander seed, and called manna by them, as though they were at a loss what it might be; for in their language *man* also expresses a question. So Josephus says of these things too. They were commanded each to gather an assaron of it each day (the assaron is a measure), and if anyone gathered more, it was not fit to eat the next day, spoiled by worms and bitterness. This was arranged so that the strong might not gather much and leave too little for the weak. They used this food for forty years, as long as they were in the desert.

Again they came to a waterless place called Rephidim, and, thirsty, they were stirred against Moses. He prayed to God and was ordered to strike the rock with his rod; he struck it, and at once water gushed from it, abundant and very clear. The Amalekites advanced against the Hebrews, calling their neighbours in as allies. Moses separated the fighting men of the people from the rest, put Joshua the son of Nun in command of them and left part of the army to guard the water; he himself withdrew to the mountain, and bade Joshua trust in God and lead the army out against the enemy. Battle was joined. As long as Moses held his hands up, the Hebrews handled the Amalekites roughly; but whenever Moses let his hands down, tired from holding them up, the Hebrews then began to be beaten. So he set his brother Aaron and Hur, the husband of his sister Miriam, one on each side, and bade them hold up his hands. And so the Hebrews defeated the Amalekites, and would have destroyed them all had not night stopped them. Not one of the Hebrews died.

## 1.15

From there they went forward, and in the third month after leaving Egypt they reached Mount Sinai, and Moses sacrificed to God there. There his father-in-law Reuel met him. Seeing his son-in-law in a crowd of business, with everyone coming to him, he advised him to entrust the hearing of lawsuits to others and himself to see to greater matters and the safety of the people: to appoint rulers over ten thousands, then over thousands, to set others over five hundreds, after these to make captains of hundreds and of fifties, and to set men over thirties, twenties and tens to keep order. When Reuel had given this advice, Moses did as he suggested.

Then, commanding the people to move their tents near Mount Sinai, he himself went up the mountain. The people moved their tents, keeping themselves pure in other ways and from intercourse with women for three days. On the third day at dawn a cloud encircled the camp of the Hebrews, and there were terrible lightnings and violent winds, which frightened the Hebrews as they waited in their tents. Moses appeared, full of courage, and at the sight of him they were freed from fear. He told them what God had commanded and what laws they were to keep, and brought the people forward, with their wives and children, to hear God. And they heard a voice coming from on high.

Then he went up the mountain again. When he stayed there long, the people gathered before Aaron and said: "Make us gods; for we do not know what has become of Moses." Aaron, under pressure, ordered each to bring the golden earrings and the ornaments their wives and daughters wore. They brought them, and from them they made a molten calf and sacrificed to it. Moses came down from the mountain carrying in his hands the two tablets of the testimony, of stone, written on both sides; as he came near the camp he saw the calf, and in anger he broke the tablets, took the calf, burned it, ground it fine, threw it into water and made the people drink it. He gave orders to the sons of Levi, and they went through the camp and killed many, so that those who fell were counted at as many as three thousand.

Moses returned to the mountain and said: "Lord, your people have sinned. Now, if you forgive them their sin, forgive it; if not, blot me too out of your book." And the Lord said to him: "Go down, lead this people to the place I told you of." And when Moses asked to see Him, the Lord showed him His back. He came down to the people again carrying two tablets with the ten words written on them, and the look of his face was glorified, and the people could not gaze on him; and he put a veil over his face, and so spoke with them. He told them what God had commanded about sacrifices and purifications, about unclean and clean animals, to keep away from the one and feed on the other, about unlawful unions, whom the priests might marry and what sort of women the high priests, and about the sabbath, the seventh year and the fiftieth, which they call the jubilee; and that God had ordered a tabernacle to be made, into which He would come, to be carried with them as they moved; and He showed him the measurements of the tabernacle and the plan by which it should be built. The people, rejoicing at this, brought gold and silver, precious stones, bronze, wood, goats' hair, rams' skins, linen and wool. As master builders of the tabernacle he appointed Bezalel, the son of his sister Miriam, and Oholiab.

## 1.16

When the tabernacle was finished, Moses set it up. The ark was made of wood that does not rot, covered all over with gold, and in it they put the two tablets with the ten words written on them. The first says that there is one God and that He alone must be worshipped; the second, to make no image of any living thing and not to bow down to it; the third, not to swear by God over anything vain; the fourth, to keep the seventh days, resting from all work; the fifth, to honour parents; the sixth, to abstain from murder; the seventh, not to commit adultery; the eighth, not to steal; the ninth, not to bear false witness; the tenth, not to covet anything that belongs to another. This ark they brought into the inner sanctuary. In the temple they placed a table, and on it twelve unleavened loaves, and a golden lampstand, and an altar of incense, of wood inside but covered with gold outside. Before the tabernacle he set up an altar of wood plated with bronze, and opposite it were set bowls and mixing-bowls with censers, and other vessels prepared for the sacred service, all of gold. Robes were made for the priests, and for the high priest too. God chose Aaron as high priest. Moses gathered the people and said: "Men, the work is finished, and God judges Aaron worthy of the honour of the priesthood."

Moses ordered each man to contribute half a shekel, to meet the expenses of the tabernacle. The shekel is a Hebrew coin worth four Attic drachmas. The number of those who contributed was six hundred and five thousand five hundred and fifty. The contribution was made by free men past their twentieth year up to their fiftieth. The tabernacle was made in seven months. God showed that the work had been done as He wished by coming to be present in the tabernacle. He showed His presence in this way: a mist came over the tabernacle alone, neither thick and dense like clouds, nor so very thin and loose that the sight could pass through it; and a sweet dew flowed from it.

Moses sacrificed in the court of the tabernacle for seven days, and on the eighth he proclaimed a festival to the people and bade them sacrifice; and they vied with one another. When the offerings lay on the altar, fire was kindled of itself and consumed everything on the altar. The two elder of Aaron's four sons, Nadab and Abihu, sacrificed against the ordinances and were burned on their chests and faces by fire that broke out of itself and consumed them; their bodies were carried outside the camp and buried. He set apart the tribe of Levi from the others and consecrated it, and handed over to them the tabernacle and the sacred vessels, so that they might serve as the priests directed.

After this he decided to number the people, and those able to bear arms were found to be six hundred and three thousand six hundred and fifty; these were those past their twentieth year up to their fiftieth. In place of the tribe of Levi they enrolled the sons of Joseph, Manasseh and Ephraim, among the heads of tribes, as their forefather Jacob had ordered. The Levites, when numbered, were found to be twenty-three thousand eight hundred and eighty.

## 1.17

Then the multitude rose against Moses, blaming him for having taken them from a good land and made them wander in the desert. He led them from there to the place called the Ravine, near the land of the Canaanites, and taking one man from each tribe he sent them to spy out the strength of the Canaanites and the quality of the land. They went through the land in forty days and came back, bringing fruit from that land; they praised the country as good, but frightened the multitude by saying that it would be impossible to take, because of the size of its rivers, the roughness of its mountains and the strength of its cities. Again the multitude were angry with Moses, and they thought of stoning him and Aaron and returning to Egypt. Of the spies only Joshua the son of Nun and Caleb encouraged the people, insisting that nothing difficult would meet them. Moses and Aaron besought God to stop the multitude's madness. The cloud was present in the tabernacle and signified God's appearance. Moses took heart and spoke to the multitude, and said that God was angry, but would not destroy them all nor blot out the race; He would not give them Canaan, however, but would leave them in the desert for forty years, and give the land to their children. At these words of Moses the multitude were in grief. Then they wished to engage the Canaanites, though God forbade it; and they attacked and were defeated, and some fell and others fled together to the camp.

A revolt seized the multitude for this reason. Korah, who was of the tribe of Levi and most distinguished in birth and wealth and most persuasive in speech, envied Moses and cried out against the man as acting like a tyrant. "On what grounds," he said, "has he given the priesthood to Aaron and his sons?
