If as being of the tribe of Levi, I have a better right to obtain it, being his equal in birth and his superior in wealth and age. If it must be assigned to another tribe, that of Reuben will be preferred for its seniority, and Dathan and Abiram will take it." He said this with malice, and two hundred and fifty of the leading men were ranged with him, and the multitude too was stirred up.

Moses stood up in their midst and said: "To me, Korah, both you and each of those ranged with you seem worthy of honour; but God chose Aaron for the priesthood, and now He Himself will judge again whom He wishes to be priest. At dawn, then, all of you who lay claim to the priesthood, come forward, each bringing a censer from home, and fire and incense, and let Aaron come forward with you likewise. When you burn incense before the people, he whose sacrifice God judges the more pleasing shall be your priest." The multitude approved this, and the next day they gathered in assembly. Moses sent to Dathan and Abiram and bade them come; they would not obey, and threatened Moses.

Moses, bidding the elders follow him, went to their tents; and when he was near he raised his hands to heaven and cried aloud: "Since I am suspected of wrongdoing, Master, come Yourself as judge and show that nothing is ordered by chance, but everything by Your providence. If I gave the priesthood to Aaron at Your command, may the ground they stand on gape around Dathan and Abiram and their family; but if they speak the truth against me, may I fall into the curses I have called down on them." He said this weeping. Suddenly the earth shook, and with a harsh crashing it sank around their tents and carried them and all they had down into the chasm, and then what had parted came together again.

The people shuddered at this, and Moses called those who contended for the priesthood. Two hundred and fifty men of note, and Aaron and Korah, came together before the tabernacle and offered what they had brought in their censers. A great fire blazed out, such as might be kindled at God's command, and by it the two hundred and fifty and Korah were destroyed, so that not even their bodies were found. Aaron alone was found unharmed. Moses ordered the censers of those men to be laid round the bronze altar, so that they might be a reminder of what had happened to those who came after.

Holding an assembly again, he told the heads of the tribes to come bringing the names of the tribes written on staffs, the priesthood to belong to him on whose staff a sign from God appeared. So the others brought theirs, and Aaron too, having written "Levite" on his staff. Moses laid them in the tabernacle of God. The next day he brought them out. The others stayed just as they were, but Aaron's had put forth shoots and fruit. The book of Leviticus calls the fruit nuts, but Josephus almonds. All were astonished, since God had chosen Aaron three times. Since the tribe of the Levites was released from wars and service in the field, so that they might not neglect the service of the temple for want of necessities, Moses ordered that when the Hebrews had gained Canaan they should assign forty-eight cities to the Levites, with land before them, and pay a tenth of the yearly crops to them and to the priests; and he ordered the people to supply them with a great many other things, which anyone who wishes to know each of them will find by opening the book of Leviticus.

## 1.18

Having ordered these things, Moses came with the army to Idumaea and asked to pass through it. The king of the country came out to meet Moses with an armed force, and he went through the desert, since God advised him not to begin a battle. Then too Miriam, Moses' sister, died, in the fortieth year after the departure from Egypt. He led the people away through the desert and Arabia; and there Aaron died, after going up a high mountain and handing over the high priest's robe to his son Eleazar, with the multitude watching, having lived a hundred and twenty-three years.

Moses came to the country of the Moabites and Amorites and asked a passage for the army. Sihon, the king of the country, forbade the Hebrews to pass, and since God had signified victory when Moses asked, the Hebrews took up their full armour and advanced to battle. The Amorites were routed at once and all destroyed, and Sihon their king fell too. They took their cities and seized their land. Og too, the king of Gilead and Gaulanitis, attacked the Israelites, having come as an ally of Sihon, who had already perished. He died in the battle and his army was destroyed; the cities were taken and their inhabitants perished.

From there Moses led the force to the great plain and camped opposite Jericho. This is a prosperous city, bearing palms and growing balsam. Balak, the king of the Moabites, sent for the seer Balaam, so that he might come and lay curses on the Israelites for their destruction. Balaam set out; but on the road a divine angel met him, and the ass on which Balaam rode, perceiving the divine spirit, would not go on and did not yield to blows; speaking with a human voice by the will of God, it called its master unjust for beating it and not understanding that it was held back by God from the service he was hurrying to. As he was troubled by the human voice, the angel appeared and said that it was he who was barring the beast's way; and when Balaam wished to turn back, he urged him to go on, and to say whatever God inspired. He came to Balak, and was taken up a mountain, and offered seven bulls and as many rams as whole burnt offerings; and when he saw a turn of fortune signified, far from cursing the Hebrews, he was seized by the god and blessed them and all their race and sang their praises. When Balak was displeased at this, Balaam said: "It is not in our power to keep silent or to speak about such things, O king; when the Spirit of God takes hold of us, it sends out through us whatever words it wishes. But since I wish to please you, an altar and victims must be prepared again." So he sacrificed again, and falling on his face he foretold what sufferings there would be for kings and what for cities. Balak sent Balaam away in dishonour.

Then, on Balaam's advice, he sent the virgins against the Israelites. The young men of the Hebrews were caught by them and lay with them, so that the lawlessness now spread through the whole army, the young men, out of love for the Midianite women, sacrificing to their gods and tasting unaccustomed foods. Moses brought the people into assembly, said that the young men were acting unworthily of themselves and of their fathers, and tried to set them right. But Zimri, the head of the tribe of Simeon, who was living with a Midianite woman and madly in love with her, defied Moses shamelessly and swore he would never leave the woman. Phinehas, the son of the high priest Eleazar, deeply grieved, did not stop striking the Midianite woman and Zimri with his sword until he had killed them both. Other young men who cared for virtue imitated him and killed those of the young men who had broken the law with foreign women. So no fewer than fourteen thousand men perished.

Provoked by this, Moses sent an army against the Midianites, appointing Phinehas its commander. Battle was joined, and their kings, five in number, fell, and a very great multitude. The Hebrews plundered their country, and, destroying the inhabitants with their wives, they spared only the virgins, numbering thirty-two thousand, whom Moses divided among the priests, the Levites and the people. Being now old, he appointed Joshua the son of Nun his successor, at God's command.

## 1.19

The tribes of Gad and Reuben and the half-tribe of Manasseh begged Moses to allot them the land of the Amorites. He called them cowards, unwilling to share with their kinsmen in the battle against the Canaanites. They answered in their defence that this was not so, but that, being rich in a multitude of cattle, they wished to leave them in that country, which was good for feeding four-footed beasts, so that they themselves might march out unencumbered with their kinsmen to the struggles. Moses approved this and allotted the Amorite land to them on these terms.

When a man of the tribe of Manasseh died leaving only daughters, and Moses was asked whose his inheritance would be, he answered: "If the girls marry men of their own tribe, let them go to them with the inheritance; but if they are betrothed to men of another tribe, let them leave the inheritance in their father's tribe." And so he ordained that each man's inheritance should stay within the tribes.

When forty years less thirty days had passed since the Israelites came out of Egypt, Moses, now a hundred and twenty years old, gathered the people and announced to them his own end. He gave them much advice, gave them laws in books and the ordering of their constitution, commanded them to make war on the Amalekites, required an oath of them to keep the laws, and urged Joshua, their leader, to march against the Canaanites. "See," he said, "I am going to our forefathers; for God has shown me this day for my going to them." The multitude wept, and their lament was beyond comfort and so great that Moses himself was overcome by the feeling and shed tears. As he went to where he was to vanish, they all followed, beating their breasts. He signed to those at a distance with his hand to stay, and only the council of elders escorted him, and the high priest Eleazar and the general Joshua, until he came to Mount Nebo, to the top of Pisgah, which faces Jericho. From there the Lord showed him the land which the sons of Israel were to inherit, saying to him: "See it with your eyes, but you shall not go into it." When he came to this mountain, which is high, he sent away the council of elders, and while he was talking with Eleazar and Joshua a cloud suddenly enveloped him and he vanished. They wrote in the sacred books that he died, so that no one might dare to say, because of the greatness of his virtue, that he had been taken up to God. He lived a hundred and twenty years in all, and ruled for a third of them, less one month; and they mourned him for thirty days. So Josephus says; but the book of Deuteronomy has this at its end, word for word: "And Moses the servant of the Lord died, and no one knows of his death to this day."

When the mourning had ended, Joshua sent spies to Jericho, and summoning the heads of the tribes of Reuben, Gad and Manasseh he reminded them of their agreement with Moses. With fifty thousand of their infantry following, he advanced to the Jordan. The spies who had been sent to Jericho surveyed the whole city, but were recognized and reported to the king of the country, and he ordered them seized at once. When the woman with whom they had taken refuge, called Rahab, learned that men were coming in search of them, she hid them, laying bundles of flax on them, and told the searchers that those strange men had dined with her and left before sunset. Deceived by the woman in this way, they rushed off in all directions after the men. Rahab told the men to go to their own people, to remember her and repay her when they had mastered the land of Canaan. They acknowledged their gratitude, and told her that when the city was taken she should shut up her property and her family in her lodging and hang a scarlet cloth in front of the door, so that the house might be known and spared. They came safe to their own people and reported what they had done.

The Jordan was running high, and Joshua took the Hebrews across it in this way. The priests went first, carrying the ark; then the Levites, bringing the tabernacle; and the people followed them. When the stream of the river was cut off for the priests who stepped in first, they all crossed with confidence. The priests stood in the middle until the multitude had crossed; then they came out too, leaving the stream free to flow. Ten stades before Jericho they

## 1.20

camped. Joshua set up an altar of the stones which each of the heads of the tribes, at his command, had taken from the bed of the Jordan, sacrificed on it and kept the feast of the Passover. Then the manna failed them, after they had used it for forty years; for they now had plenty of all they needed from the land.

On the first day of the feast Joshua began the siege of Jericho, managing it in this way. The priests carrying the ark went round the wall, sounding seven horns, and the council of elders followed; then they returned to the camp. They did this for six days. On the seventh, when they had done the same and gone round seven times, the wall fell, and the people went in and killed everyone and burned the city. The spies saved Rahab alone, with her family, since she had hung a scarlet cloth in front of the house; and Joshua gave her fields and held her in honour. He laid curses on any who should wish to raise the city again: that the man who laid its foundations should lose his first son, and the one who finished it should be punished with the loss of his youngest. A great quantity of silver and gold was collected from the city, and all was dedicated to God as the first-fruits of their successes; for all were forbidden to take any of it for themselves.

A certain Achar of the tribe of Judah, finding a cloth woven with gold and a lump of gold weighing two hundred shekels, kept them for himself, burying them in his tent. After this, when battle was joined, the Hebrews were routed and thirty-six of their men fell. This grieved the multitude greatly, and Joshua fell on his face and prayed to God. Told by an oracle that there had been a theft of the consecrated goods, he left the judgement to the lot. When the lot fell on Achar, he confessed the theft and produced the stolen goods. He was stoned with his family, and Joshua besieged Ai and took it.

The Gibeonites and their neighbours, who were akin to the Canaanites, fearing that they would suffer the same as the people of Jericho and of Ai, sent an embassy to Joshua, saying that they lived very far away and had no kinship with the Canaanites, and asking the Israelites to make a treaty with them and agreements of friendship. So Joshua made friendship with them. But learning afterwards that they were of the race of the Canaanites, he accused them of the deceit. They said they had done it from necessity, fearing destruction. Joshua, so as not to break his oath, made them public slaves. So Josephus; but the book of Joshua says that they made them hewers of wood and drawers of water for the whole congregation and for the altar of God.

When the king of Jerusalem attacked the Gibeonites because of their treaty with the Hebrews, Joshua came to their aid, routed the enemy and pursued them in their rout. Then hail bigger than usual and thunderbolts fell from heaven on the enemy, and the day was lengthened, God stopping the sun in its course at Joshua's prayer, so that the enemy might not escape when night came on. The kings were caught hiding in a cave, and all were killed. Joshua went up again into the hill country of Canaan, made a great slaughter of those in it, took booty and came to the camp at Gilgal. The place of the camp was called Gilgal because the Israelites now knew themselves free from the Egyptians and from the hardship of the desert; for the Hebrews call freedom *galgala*.

The Canaanites about Lebanon and those in the plains marched against them, with the Philistines as their allies. Their army was three hundred thousand infantry, ten thousand horsemen and thirty thousand chariots. God promised victory, and there was a stubborn battle, so that the whole army of the enemy was destroyed except for a few, and their kings fell too.

## 1.21

Five years had now passed, and none of the Canaanites was left any longer. Joshua set up the tabernacle at the city of Shiloh, until it should be possible to build them a temple. He gathered the people at Shiloh and counted up the cities taken, the armies of the enemy destroyed, and that thirty-one of their kings had been killed. He said that the cities still left needed a long siege because of their strength, and thought it right now to send home those from beyond the Jordan who had set out with them. Calling those of the two tribes and the half-tribe, fifty thousand in number, he allowed them to go away to their allotted lands. When they had crossed the Jordan they set up an altar on its bank. This caused alarm among the multitude of the Israelites. So they sent ten men of rank and Phinehas to them, to complain about the altar. They said that they had set up the altar not for rebellion, but as a token to those who came after of their kinship with you; for they knew one God, common to all the Hebrews. When they said this, Phinehas and those with him returned to Joshua.

Joshua divided the lands among the multitude by lot and lived at Shechem. Having grown very old and being now about to die, he gave an exhortation to the multitude, and died having lived a hundred and ten years, of which he spent forty with Moses, and twenty-five as general after Moses' death. The high priest Eleazar died too, leaving the high priesthood to his son Phinehas.

After this the Canaanites, hoping to overcome the race of the Hebrews now that Joshua was dead, marched against them, entrusting the command to their king Adonibezek. The name means "lord of Bezek", for in Hebrew *adoni* means lord. The two tribes of Judah and Simeon engaged the Canaanites, routed them, captured Adonibezek and cut off his hands and feet. They also took many cities and the lower city of Jerusalem, and killed its inhabitants; but the siege of the upper city was difficult, both from the nature of the place and the strength of its walls. They also took Hebron and slaughtered everyone, and gave it to Caleb as his portion, as Moses had commanded. They also allotted land to the descendants of Jethro, who were kinsmen of Moses, because they had left their country and followed the Hebrew people in the desert. The tribes of Judah and Simeon, having prospered greatly, laid down their arms.

The tribe of Ephraim, besieging Bethel, achieved nothing. But they seized a man of the city and gave him pledges that if he handed over his country they would save him and his kin. So he betrayed it and was saved with his family, and they killed the inhabitants and held the city. The Benjamites, to whom the city of Jerusalem had fallen by lot, imposed tribute on its inhabitants and allowed them to live, against the divine commands; and the other tribes did the same.

## 1.22

Now that the Israelites were prospering, luxury corrupted them. They broke the divine laws and were reluctant to work; the aristocracy failed and the council of elders was no longer appointed. So a terrible civil strife seized them, for this reason. A Levite was going home bringing his wife, who was from Bethlehem. When it was already evening he lodged with an old man in Gibeah, a city of the tribe of Benjamin. Some young men of Gibeah saw the woman and came demanding her; when the old man would not give her up, they threatened to kill him. He offered them his own daughter, so that his guests might suffer no violence. But they seized the woman and went off, and after abusing her through the whole night they let her go at daybreak. Overwhelmed by the night's suffering, by shame before her husband and by grief, she died. Her husband cut the dead woman into twelve parts and sent a part to each tribe, telling them of the outrage and of those to blame for the calamity.

So they sent to Gibeah demanding the lawbreakers, but its people would not give them up. The tribes marched against them, and twice the Israelites engaged and were defeated. Then they prayed to God through Phinehas the high priest, and so, engaging the Benjamites, they prevailed, so that all fell except six hundred, who fled to the mountains nearby. The Israelites burned Gibeah, slaughtered the women and children, and did the same to their other cities. They also slaughtered the inhabitants of Jabesh, a city of Gilead, with their wives and children, because it had not fought on their side, except for four hundred virgins, whom they gave to the surviving Benjamites to have as wives, pitying them for their calamity. They considered how the other two hundred might get wives too; for they had earlier sworn, because of the outrage, that no one would give a daughter in marriage to a Benjamite. As they considered, they decided that they themselves would not give their daughters, because of the oath, but that the Benjamites might seize virgins and marry them, with the Israelites neither preventing nor encouraging it. So they resolved this, and the Benjamites carried out what had been resolved.

When the Israelites had long ceased fighting, the Canaanites despised them and took cities from them. The tribe of Dan fled to the mountains, and from there moved inland. Since the Israelites had adopted the customs of the Canaanites and despised their own constitution, God was angered. So the king of the Assyrians, Chusarsathaim, marched against them and imposed heavy tribute on them, under which they were oppressed for eight years. Then an energetic man of the tribe of Judah, called Othniel, gathering a few companions, destroyed the Assyrian garrison among them at the bidding of a divine oracle. When more had gathered afterwards, he defeated the Assyrians in battle and drove them off, and for this he was chosen to rule the multitude and judge the people. He held the rule for fifty years and then departed this life.

Being without a ruler and despising God, the Israelites were made subject by Eglon, the king of the Moabites, and had yearly tribute laid on them. He built a palace at Jericho and afflicted them in many ways for eighteen years. A young Benjamite named Ehud (Josephus says he was called Judas), bold and strong, became familiar with Eglon and visited him constantly, flattering the man and fawning on him. Once, bringing gifts to Eglon with two servants, he secretly carried a dagger. At midday, when the guards had gone off by themselves, he found him alone, went in as a familiar, and taking him aside, as if to tell him something secret, struck him a mortal blow with the dagger and killed him. Then he went off to the people of Jericho and persuaded them to claim their freedom. They armed themselves; and Eglon's men, finding him dead, turned to flight, and were destroyed as they were pursued. So the Hebrews were freed from slavery to the Moabites, and the rule of the multitude was entrusted to Ehud. He led the people for eighty years and ended his life. After him Shamgar the son of Anath ruled, and died in the first year of his rule, having struck down many of the foreigners.

## 1.23

As the people of the Hebrews broke the divine laws, God was angry. Sisera, the commander of Jabin, king of Canaan, attacked them, afflicted them sorely and made them pay tribute; and so they remained for twenty years. A prophetess, Deborah (the name means "bee"), besought God for the people. God consented, and bade them choose Barak as general; in the Hebrew language this means "lightning". The man was of the tribe of Naphtali. Deborah urged Barak to go against the enemy with ten thousand men, since God commanded it. He demanded that she share the command with him. The woman agreed, and when battle was joined between them, with the ten thousand, and Sisera, heavy rain poured down and hail burst out; these were driven by the wind into the faces of the enemy and darkened their sight, and so they turned to flight.

Sisera too fled, and took refuge with a woman, asking her to hide him; the woman was named Jael. She received him and gave him milk to drink, and when he had drunk he fell asleep. As he slept, Jael attacked him, drove a nail through his temple and pinned him to the ground, and showed the dead enemy to Barak's men. Barak also marched against Jabin, the king of Canaan, killed him and razed his city. He held command for thirty years and died, Deborah dying at about the same time.

Then the Midianites, with the Amalekites and Arabs, routed the Israelites in battle. As they attacked them for seven years, there was famine and scarcity of necessities, and they prayed to God. Gideon, of the tribe of Manasseh, was secretly beating out sheaves of grain in the winepress, and he saw someone in the form of a young man standing beside him and calling him a friend of God and fortunate. Gideon, speaking with irony, said that the proof of his good fortune was that he was using the winepress instead of a threshing floor. The young man urged him to take heart and exert himself for the freedom of his countrymen. Gideon begged the one appearing to him not to leave the place, and went in and brought out a sacrifice. The angel bade him put it by the rock; and touching the rock with his rod he kindled a fire of itself and burned up the sacrifice. Then Gideon asked God that dew should come on the fleece, and so it did; and again he asked that dew should come on all the ground, but dryness on the fleece, and so it happened. So Gideon was persuaded, and armed himself against the enemy with ten thousand men.

Then, at God's command, he led them all at midday to the river, and taking with him those who lapped as they drank and drank hastily, as being fearful (these were three hundred), he went off to the war and left the rest behind. At night he took one of the soldiers and went to the enemy's camp. He heard one of the enemy telling his tent-mate that he seemed to see in his sleep a barley loaf rolling through the camp, which knocked down the king's tent and those of the army; and the other judged that the dream signified the destruction of the army, likening Gideon and those with him to a barley loaf because they were few and weak. Hearing this, Gideon took heart, went back and ordered his men to arms. Dividing the force into three hundreds, he ordered each man to carry an empty jar, a lighted torch and a ram's horn, and in the fourth watch of the night he led them out against the enemy. When they were already near them they blew the horns, broke the jars and rushed on with the torches, raising the war-cry. This threw the enemy into panic, and some were killed by their opponents, but more were destroyed by one another, since it was night, and almost all perished, among them two of their kings, Oreb and Zeeb. The rest of the leaders and the army, fleeing, were overtaken by Gideon and those with him, and all fell.

Gideon held the rule for forty years, and died an old man, leaving seventy lawful sons by different wives, and one by a concubine called Abimelech. He killed all his brothers except one, Jotham, who escaped, and made himself tyrant. The people rose against Abimelech and drove him out of the city of Shechem. With the help of one of the Shechemites he took the city by stratagem, killed the Shechemites of age and razed the city. He marched on Thebez, another city, and took it by assault. The multitude ran together into one great tower in the middle of the city. As Abimelech came near its gates, a woman threw a piece of millstone down on his head from above and killed him.

The leadership of the Hebrews was entrusted to Jair, of the tribe of Manasseh; he directed the government for twenty-two years and died. Afterwards the Ammonites, with the Philistines, plundered the country. As the Ammonites were pillaging Gilead, the inhabitants chose Jephthah as their leader. When he was about to engage the enemy, he made a vow that if he won he would sacrifice whatever first met him on his return. He engaged and won; but as he came back his little daughter met him, his only child and still a virgin. He cried out in grief, but so as not to break his promise he sacrificed the girl as a whole burnt offering. When the tribe of Ephraim were angry with him for not taking them with him to the war against the Ammonites, and he could not persuade them by his excuses, he killed many of them in battle. He held the leadership for six years and ended his life. Ibzan, of the tribe of Judah, took over the rule, judged Israel for seven years and died. After him Abdon, sprung from the tribe of Ephraim, was appointed leader, and died an old man after ruling for eight years.

## 1.24

Then the Philistines mastered the Israelites and held them tributary for forty years. At that time Samson was born (the name signifies strength), and he attacked the Philistines. His coming into life happened in this way. Manoah, of the tribe of Dan, lived with a barren wife and asked God that a child be given them. Once, when the woman was alone, a young man appeared to her, tall and handsome, bringing her the good news of the birth of a son. He told her not to drink wine or strong drink, and not to cut the child's hair; for he said the boy would be a Nazirite to the Lord, that is, holy, and Israel would be saved through him from the hand of the foreigners. She told her husband this. He prayed to God that the one who had appeared to his wife might appear to him too. So the angel came again to the woman, when Manoah was again away, and the woman begged him to wait for her husband to come. Manoah came and asked the angel what they should do when the child was born. He said: "He shall not drink wine or strong drink, he shall eat nothing unclean, and his hair shall not be shaved." Manoah asked to learn the name of the one speaking with him, but he said it was wonderful and was silent. Manoah brought a kid and sacrificed it and offered loaves. He bade him put the meat and the loaves on the rock; when they had been put there he touched them with his rod, and fire, kindled from it, burned them up, and the angel went up to heaven through the flame, as Manoah and his wife watched.

After this the child was born to them and named Samson; he grew up, and the Spirit of the Lord went with him. Seeing a girl of the foreigners at Timnah, a city of the Philistines, he fell in love with her. His parents objected to the marriage with a foreign woman, but he prevailed in marrying her. As he went often to the girl's parents, he met a lion, and having nothing with which to defend himself he crushed the beast with his hands, turned off the road and threw it into the brushwood. Some days later, going again to his betrothed, he found a swarm of bees gathered in the lion's mouth. He took a honeycomb from it and ate, and brought some to his father and mother.

Going to the foreigners, he set a riddle for the thirty young men who feasted with him at the wedding; and he agreed that if they found the answer within seven days he would give each of them a linen sheet and a robe, but if they could not solve the riddle he demanded a robe and a linen cloth from each of them. When the young men had agreed, he put the question: "Out of the eater came food, and out of the strong, sweetness." Unable to understand the riddle, they begged the girl to learn it from her husband and tell them. When the girl begged Samson to tell her the meaning of the riddle, he held out at first, but when she wept he told her everything, and she told the young men. On the seventh day they said to Samson: "What is sweeter than honey, or what stronger than a lion?" Samson was angry, gave the young men what he had promised and renounced the marriage; and her father gave the woman to another.

Provoked by this, Samson caught three hundred foxes, tied lighted torches to their tails and let them loose on the fields of the Philistines when summer was beginning, and so burned up their crops and their vines. In return for this the Philistines burned his former wife and her kin. Samson killed many of the foreigners and sat down on a rock. They marched against the Israelites, saying: "If you wish to be blameless, give Samson into our hands." Three thousand armed men went to the rock. Samson came down and gave himself up willingly to his countrymen, and they handed him over bound to the Philistines. He burst his bonds, seized the jawbone of an ass lying somewhere there, killed a thousand of the foreigners with it and routed the rest. From then on the place was called Jawbone. Then he was so thirsty that he was fainting from thirst, and he wept, praying to God. God sent up a spring of water from the crack which Samson had made when he threw the jawbone on the ground.

He went to Gaza. The people of Gaza lay in wait for him, and so that he might not get out unnoticed, they shut the gates of the city. But he rose at midnight, put the gates on his shoulders with their posts and jambs, and carried them up to the top of the mountain and set them down there. He fell in love with a Philistine courtesan called Delilah and lived with her; and the chiefs of the foreigners promised the woman much if she learned from Samson the cause of his strength and told them. She tried the man in various ways, seeking to know where his great strength came from, and he made up different reasons at different times and told the woman. The woman, testing his words, found them deceits. When she pressed him and begged more warmly, he revealed the secret to her, saying that he grew his hair at the command of God, who had ordered him not to cut it, and that his strength lay in it. The woman cut his hair while he slept and handed him over, weak, to the enemy. They put out the man's eyes, and he who had been feared became their plaything.

As time went on his hair grew again and his strength came back. When the Philistines were holding a drinking party, and the rulers and great men were feasting together in a house held up by pillars, Samson too was brought to be mocked, and a boy led him by the hand. A crowd of men and women, about three thousand, gathered by the house to see Samson abused. Mocked and slapped, he said to the boy leading him: "Bring me to the pillars, so that I may lean on them, for I need rest." Grasping the two pillars, he brought the house down on himself, and he and all in the house perished. Such was his end, after he had judged the people of the Hebrews for twenty years.

## 1.25

Judges no longer led Israel, but the high priest Eli was over them. In his time there was a famine, and a man of Bethlehem called Elimelech moved to the land of Moab because of the famine, with his wife and two sons. He took Moabite wives for his sons. But within ten years Elimelech and his sons died, and there were left widows Naomi, who had been Elimelech's wife, and both his sons' wives, Orpah and Ruth. Naomi returned to her country. Of her daughters-in-law Orpah, at her mother-in-law's urging, stayed among the Moabites, but Ruth went away with Naomi. When they reached Bethlehem, Boaz, who was a kinsman of Elimelech, gave them hospitality. When the townspeople addressed the woman by her name, she said: "Do not call me Naomi, but Mara" (Naomi means good fortune, and Mara pain and bitterness), "for I went away full and have come back empty."

At harvest time Ruth, with her mother-in-law's consent, went out to glean in Boaz's field. He saw the girl, and learning who she was, allowed her to reap what she could and keep it, and ordered his steward to give her a meal. She also received parched grain from him and kept it for her mother-in-law, and went off late bringing the barley she had gathered too, and told her what Boaz had done for her. And again she went out with Boaz's maidservants. Naomi planned to have Ruth lie with him, and sent her daughter-in-law, telling her to lie down and sleep at the feet of the man as he slept on the threshing floor. She did so; and Boaz, waking about midnight, asked who she was. Learning it, he kept quiet then, but at dawn he woke Ruth and told her to go to her mother-in-law. "I myself," he said, "will ask the man who is a nearer kinsman to you than I; if he wishes to marry you, you shall go to the man, but if he lawfully declines, you shall live with me." So the woman went away and told Naomi everything. Boaz called Ruth and the kinsman before the council of elders and said to him: "Are you holding the inheritance of Elimelech, our kinsman, and of his sons?" When he agreed that he held it, according to the laws, by right of kinship, Boaz said: "Then we must not remember the laws by halves, but act according to them in everything. You must therefore also marry the wife of Mahlon, Elimelech's son, so as to raise up seed for our kinsman." He declined, giving up both the inheritance and the woman to Boaz. So, according to the law, before the council of elders Ruth loosed that man's sandal and spat in his face. And so Boaz married her, and she bore him Obed, which in Greek means "serving". From Obed came Jesse, and from him David, who reigned over the Israelites and left the rule to his sons, and it lasted for twenty-one generations.

While Eli the high priest, as has been told, was leading the people, his two sons Hophni and Phinehas were pests, breaking the law in everything and paying no heed to their father's warnings. God, angered by their lawlessness, revealed to Eli through Samuel, who was still a boy, what he and his sons and his house would suffer. Samuel was the son of Elkanah, a Levite, who had two wives, whose names were Hannah and Peninnah. The latter had children by Elkanah, but Hannah had no offspring. When the husband came to Shiloh with both wives and sacrificed, since it was the time for feasting and Peninnah's children were rejoicing with her, Hannah, because she was alone, wept for herself; and running to the tabernacle of God, she prayed the Lord to grant her offspring, vowing to dedicate her first-born to God. As she lingered in her prayers, Eli, sitting before the tabernacle, thought she was drunk and was sending her away. She said: "I have drunk neither wine nor strong drink, lord, but I am wasted with grief for my childlessness." And the priest said to her: "Go, and may God grant you your request." Returning home with her husband, she conceived and bore Samuel; one might say "asked of God". When the boy had grown, she brought him to Eli, as she had vowed, to be brought up for God, growing his hair, living in the temple and using water for drink.

When he was twelve years old he prophesied. Once, as he slept, God called him by name. He went to the high priest, thinking that it was he who was calling him; and this happened three times. Eli, understanding that the call was from God, said to Samuel: "If you are called again, say, 'Here I am, Lord.'" When the boy did so, the one calling told him that a heavy calamity would come on the Israelites, that Eli's sons would die together, and that the priesthood would pass to the house of Eleazar. Samuel did not wish to reveal the words to the priest, but, compelled by oaths, he reported everything as he had heard it.

## 1.26

After this the Philistines marched out against the Israelites and had the better in the battle. The people asked for the ark to be brought to help them. When the ark had been sent there with the sons of Eli, battle was joined. The Hebrews were defeated; about thirty thousand fell, and the sons of the high priest, and the ark was taken by the enemy. Eli the high priest was sitting on a high seat; when he learned of the battle, the slaughter of his sons and the capture of the ark, he was overcome with grief, fell from his seat and died, ninety years old, of which he had led Israel for forty.

The foreigners took the ark to the city of Ashdod and dedicated it to Dagon, for so they called their god. When they went into the house of Dagon at dawn, they found the image lying on the ground before the ark. They raised it up and set it on its former base. When they found this happening often, and every time they set the idol upright they found it lying in the posture of one bowing down before the ark, they were amazed and at a loss. Then a plague fell on the city of Ashdod and the country: a disease of the seat among men, which Josephus calls dysentery, from which many died, and mice coming up from the ground ravaged the whole country. At last the people of Ashdod understood that the ark was the cause of their sufferings, and sent it to Ascalon; and they too suffered the same. In this way the ark went round five cities of the Philistines and brought affliction on them all. So the rulers of the five afflicted cities, Gath, Ekron, Ascalon, Gaza and Ashdod, came together, and some advised one thing about the ark and some another. In the end they all decided to make five golden seats and as many golden mice and put them on the ark; to make a new cart and yoke to it cows that had just calved, keeping back their calves; to put the ark on the cart, take the cows to a crossroads and leave them there, so that they might be free to go whichever way they set out. If they went straight to the Israelites, they would think that the evils were brought on them by the ark and would not hinder its journey to them; but if they went off anywhere else, they would turn back the cows, the cart and what was on it.

Having carried out what they had decided, they let the cows go, following at a distance themselves to see what would come of it. The cows went straight towards the Hebrews and did not stop until they came to a great plain at Beth-shemesh. When the men of that village saw the ark, they rushed to it, lifted it down from the cart, sacrificed the cows, and offered them as a whole burnt offering on the wood of which the cart had been made. Seeing this, the Philistines went back. God, angered, put to death those who touched the ark, not being priests. The rest made known about the ark to the commonwealth of the Hebrews, and they took it, carried it to Kiriath-jearim and placed it in the house of Abinadab, who was of the tribe of Levi. It stayed there twenty years, and among the foreigners four months.

Samuel the prophet, who had by now won a great name and come to great renown, gathered the people, spoke to them about freedom and persuaded them to claim it. Learning this, the Philistines came on the Hebrews unexpectedly and so terrified them that they ran to Samuel, saying that their safety was despaired of unless he asked help from God for them. He urged them to take heart, assured them that God would come to their aid, sacrificed a lamb and prayed to God. Before the sacred flame had consumed the sacrifice, the enemy attacked the Hebrews, thinking they would overcome them more easily as they were unarmed. But the earth was shaken by God with an earthquake, thunder broke out and lightning flashed, and the enemy fell into fear and turned to flight. Samuel and the multitude pursued them and destroyed many; and after this blow they no longer marched against the Israelites, and Samuel recovered for the Hebrews the land the Philistines had taken from them. He judged the people and ruled them.

## 1.27

When he had grown old, he divided the leadership of the people and the judging between his sons. The name of the elder was Joel, of the younger Abijah. They did not walk in their father's ways, but turned aside, betrayed justice for gifts and gave judgements for bribes. Because of this the people gathered before Samuel and said: "Since you are not able to lead us because of your age, make us a king, who will lead the nation and take the field against our enemies." Samuel was grieved at this, but God said to him: "It is not you whom the people have despised, but me. Listen to them, then, and whomever I show you, anoint him king." Samuel gathered the Hebrews and agreed to appoint a king for them, but foretold that they would repent and would ask God for release from their kings. They pressed on, seeking a king, and begged him not to trouble about what would come. The prophet said: "Go away now; when I learn whom God gives you as king, I will send for you."

A man of Benjamin, named Kish, lost his asses, and sent his son, who was tall and handsome, called Saul, with one of his slaves to look for the asses. Having gone round searching without finding them, he came to Samuel at Armathaim to ask about the asses. God revealed to Samuel that this was the one He wished to reign over the people of the Hebrews. So when Samuel was asked about the asses he said they were safe, entertained Saul and sent him on his way at dawn; going with him outside the town and taking the young man aside alone, he poured oil on his head. He kissed him and said: "The Lord has anointed you ruler over His people Israel; and let this be a sign of the anointing: by the tomb of Rachel two men will meet you, bringing you the good news that the asses have been found." He told him other signs too that he spoke the truth, and that he would prophesy at Gibeah, with the prophets there, and he told him to come to Gilgal when he sent for him.

Then he gathered the people and ordered the tribes to cast lots, so that it might be shown from which the future king would come. The lot fell on the tribe of Benjamin, then on the clan of Matri. When the clan was drawn man by man, the lot fell on Saul. When he could not be found on being sought, Samuel was told by God where he was hidden, and he was brought and set in the midst. He stood head and shoulders above all, surpassing them in the stature of his body. The prophet said to the multitude: "This is the one the Lord has anointed king for you; there is none like him among you all." The people acclaimed him and said: "Long live the king." When Saul had become king in this way, many paid heed to him as king and honoured him as was fitting, but many despised and mocked him.

A month later Nahash, the king of the Ammonites, marched against Jabesh-gilead, saying that he would spare the men on no other terms than that they let him put out each one's right eye. They asked a truce of seven days, and, getting it, sent messengers to Gibeah. When the people there heard the Ammonite's words they lamented; but Saul was furiously angry, and taking two oxen he cut them in pieces and sent them to every border of Israel, threatening to treat so those who would not march out with him against the Ammonites. When the people came running together, he counted them and found seven hundred thousand. The Spirit of the Lord leapt upon Saul, and he prophesied, saying that tomorrow the men of Jabesh would be saved. He rose and marched, he and the people, through the whole night, and about the morning watch he fell on the Ammonites, having divided his army in three. Encircling them, he killed many, and the king Nahash himself, and put the survivors to flight; he invaded their country, took a great deal of booty and returned in splendour. Now he was no longer held in low esteem by any, but was famous for his courage and honoured even by those who had mocked him before. Samuel gathered the people again and proclaimed Saul king a second time, anointing him with oil once more.

So the constitution of the Hebrews changed from aristocracy into kingship. For under Moses and Joshua they had been governed by an aristocracy; then for eighteen years they lived without rulers; and after that they were ruled by those called judges, entrusting the best man with judging and managing all affairs.

## 1.28

When Samuel put the people on oath to say whether he had done them any wrong, they all cried out that he had led the nation justly and well. He answered them: "Know that you have sinned greatly in asking a king for yourselves, and by this you have provoked God. This shall be a sign to you: in the height of summer there will be a storm." According to the prophet's word there were thunders and lightnings, and hail came down, so that fear fell on the people and they begged Samuel to make God gracious to them in their sin.

When the Philistines were subduing the Hebrews, taking away their arms and forbidding them to use iron, Saul went down to Gilgal and roused the people to rise for their freedom against the Philistines. But they were wary because of the number of the enemy. The king called Samuel to him. He promised to come after six days and to sacrifice on the seventh, so that they might then engage the foreigners. But Saul, seeing the people slipping away and deserting him, sacrificed. Hearing that Samuel was already coming, he went out to meet him. Samuel said: "Why have you done this, breaking my command? Know, then, that your kingdom will not stand, but the Lord will seek a man after His own heart." And Samuel went away. Saul and his son Jonathan came to Gibeah with six hundred soldiers.

The foreigners, divided in three, were plundering the country of the Israelites. Saul and Jonathan and Ahijah the high priest watched from a height what was being done and were grieved, not being able to resist for want of men. Jonathan, without saying anything to his father, came down from the hill with only his armour-bearer and said: "Let us attack the camp of the foreigners; and if they say to us, 'Go away,' we will not go up to them, but if they say, 'Come up to us,' we must take the word as a signal of victory." And they set off for the enemy's camp. The foreigners said to them: "Come up to us, and we will tell you something." This gave Jonathan courage; and going away from there and creeping up from another side with the man who followed him, he fell on the enemy as they slept. They killed about twenty and threw the rest into panic; and in their confusion and flight they destroyed one another. Saul, seeing the enemy's camp in confusion, and noticing that his son was away with his armour-bearer, also attacked the enemy in their confusion; and those who had hidden earlier out of fear took heart and joined the king. He pursued the enemy and killed them.

Then he laid a curse on his own men, if anyone should stop killing the enemy and eat before night stopped them. In the pursuit they came to a wood where there was a swarm of bees, and Jonathan took a honeycomb and ate, for he had not heard his father's curse. When Saul wished to attack the enemy's camp, he asked God through the high priest whether He would give victory. When God gave no sign, he sought the cause, and said: "I swear by God Himself that I will kill the one who has sinned, even if it is Jonathan my son." When they cast lots, the lot fell on Jonathan. Questioned about his sin, he said he knew of nothing against himself except that yesterday, not knowing of the curse, "I tasted a honeycomb during the pursuit". Hearing this, his father swore to kill him. But the people swore in turn that they would not allow the author of the victory to die, and snatching him away they appeased God with prayers. Saul, having destroyed about sixty thousand of the Philistines, returned. He subdued the Ammonites and Moabites, the Philistines, Idumaeans and Amalekites and the king of Zobah, and rescued Israel from the hand of the foreigners.

## 1.29

Saul had three sons, Jonathan, Ishvi and Malchishua, and two daughters, Merab and Michal; as commander-in-chief he had Abner, the son of Ner, his kinsman.

Samuel told Saul that God bade him strike Amalek, and when he had prevailed to spare no one, neither women nor infants nor beasts of burden nor cattle, but to blot out its name from the earth. Saul engaged Amalek, won by force, and killed them all; he also captured the king of the country, named Agag, and did not kill him, admiring him for his beauty and his size; and he kept the choicest of the herds and flocks and the rest for himself, forgetting the divine command. Samuel, warned by God, who said that He repented of having made Saul king, went to meet the king. Saul said he gave thanks to God for granting him the mastery over the enemy, and that he had done all that had been commanded. The prophet said: "I hear the noise of beasts of burden and other cattle. Where do these come from?" Saul said that the people had kept them to sacrifice to God, and that all the Amalekites had been wiped out except their king. To this Samuel said: "Since you have broken what God commanded you, know that you will be deprived of the kingdom." Saul admitted that he had sinned, and begged the prophet to offer a sacrifice of atonement. Samuel turned away, but Saul caught hold of his cloak and tore it, trying to hold him back by force. The prophet said: "So your kingdom shall be torn, and given to a just and good man." But when the king still entreated him, he gave way, and asked for Agag to be brought; and when he had been killed at his command, he went straight back to Armathaim.

God commanded him to go to Jesse and anoint with oil as king one of his sons, whichever He showed him. He went to Bethlehem, sacrificed, and called Jesse and his sons. Seeing the first-born, tall and handsome, he thought God was pleased with him. But God said: "Do not look at his appearance, nor at the size of his body; for man will look at the face, but God at the heart." When the other sons of Jesse had passed by too, God was pleased with none of them. Samuel said: "The Lord has not chosen among these; if there is still one left, let him come forward." Jesse said: "A little one is still left, tending the sheep." So the prophet ordered him to be called. When David came at the call, a ruddy boy with beautiful eyes and handsome in other ways, God showed Samuel that this was the one to be anointed. He took the horn of oil and anointed him in the midst of his brothers, and went away to Armathaim. The divine Spirit leapt upon David and departed from Saul, and an evil spirit came on Saul, choking him and afflicting the man. So it was decided that someone who knew how to play on the lyre should attend the king, so that when the demon fell on him and troubled him, he might play the lyre and calm the disturbance. So they sent for David as such a one; and the young man was greatly loved by the king and became his armour-bearer. When the evil spirit came on the king, David sang to him, and it went away, and Saul came to himself again.

The foreigners marched against the Israelites, and those of Israel took their stand against them. Between the two armies stood one of the foreigners named Goliath, a mighty man who towered above all: he was six cubits and a span in height, his breastplate weighed five thousand shekels, his spear was like a weaver's beam, and the head of his spear was of six hundred shekels. Standing in the middle, he shouted to the sons of Israel: "Let one of you come down and face me. If he strikes me down, we will serve you; but if I throw him down, you shall be our slaves." He did this for forty days. Saul had sent David back to Jesse, and his father sent him to the camp to bring provisions to his brothers who were serving; for the three eldest were following Saul. When David came to his brothers, Goliath the foreigner again, as was his custom, challenged them to single combat and taunted the Hebrews that no one dared to fight him. Hearing these words, David was indignant and said to those present: "I will fight him." This was reported to the king, and when David stood before him he said: "You will not be able to go into battle against this uncircumcised man, for you are still a boy." David said that he trusted in God, who had given him strength against the bear and the lion when they attacked the flock and snatched sheep: he said he had chased them, rescued what they had snatched, struck the beasts and killed them. For this reason, he said, he trusted that He would let him strike down the foreigner too and take away the reproach from the sons of Israel.

When he had said this, the king allowed him to go, and put his own armour on him. It was a burden to him, and he took it off; and taking his staff, putting five stones in his bag and carrying his sling in his hand, he set out against the foreigner. Goliath laughed at him and asked whether he was going against a dog with a staff and stones. David answered that he thought him worse than a dog. Goliath, angered, boasted that he would give his flesh to the birds and the beasts, and rushed at him. David, quickly putting a stone in the sling, struck the enemy on the forehead. The blow was mighty, and the foreigner fell on his face. David ran, drew the man's sword, cut off Goliath's head and stripped him of his arms. This struck fear into the foreigners, and they turned to flight. Saul led his own army against them as they fled; a great multitude of the Philistines was killed, more were wounded, and their camp was plundered. David brought the head of Goliath to Saul and dedicated the sword to God.

## 1.30

As the army returned, the women came to meet them, and dancing they sang that Saul had slain his thousands, and the virgins answered that David had destroyed his tens of thousands. This stirred Saul to envy, and he said: "What more does he lack but the kingdom?" And he looked askance at David. When the evil spirit fell on Saul and David was singing to him as usual, Saul hurled his spear at him; and he did this twice, and David dodged it, for the Lord was with him. The king, afraid of him, made him a commander of a thousand, so that he might be in danger as he went against the enemy. But with God as his ally he prospered, and the people loved him, and the king's daughter Michal loved David. Her love was reported to the king, and he used the love as a means for a plot. He said he would give him his daughter if he brought him a hundred foreskins, thinking that he would fall fighting the foreigners. Learning this, David went with the men under him, struck down some of the foreigners and brought Saul a hundred foreskins. Josephus says that David brought not a hundred foreskins but six hundred heads of foreigners. And Saul gave him his daughter Michal in marriage.

Seeing that David had God as his ally and was loved by the multitude, he feared him still more, plotted against him and tried to kill him. He ordered his son Jonathan and the most trusted of his servants to kill him. Jonathan, who loved David, told him of the order, advised him to be on his guard, and promised to talk to his father about him and lay the whole matter before him. He did talk to him and brought him round to mildness, for Saul changed his mind and swore that he would do David no harm. Jonathan told this to David and brought him in to the king, and he attended him as before.

When the Philistines stirred again and attacked the Hebrews, David was sent by Saul to face them, and returned victorious. With the man's successes Saul's envy of him grew too. When the evil spirit came on him again and choked him, David was there, striking the lyre and singing to him. Saul, holding his spear, hurled it at David; but he saw it in time, dodged and withdrew. Saul sent guards by night to David's house, to kill him at daybreak. Michal his wife learned of this and let her husband down through a window, and he escaped. In the morning, when Saul sent for David, Michal made up the bed as if someone were lying in it, and put under the coverings the liver of a newly killed goat, so that the covering, stirring with its movement, might give the impression that someone was lying in the bed; and she brought in those who had been sent and said her husband was ill. They reported this to Saul, and he ordered him brought on his bed. Those who had been sent went off, lifted the covering of the bed, saw through the trick and reported it to the king.

Having escaped death in this way, David came to Samuel at Armathaim and stayed with him at Naioth in Ramah. Saul sent men there too to bring David back. They went off, and finding an assembly of prophets, they too prophesied. The king, learning this, sent others; and when they fared the same he sent more; and when the third party too had been possessed by the Spirit, he set out himself in anger, and as he drew near he too began to prophesy. Then those present there uttered the saying that is still repeated: "Is Saul too among the prophets?" Coming to where Samuel was, he was as if out of his mind, stripped off his clothing and lay naked a whole day and night.

## 1.31

David escaped from there and told Jonathan of his father's attempt on him, but Jonathan did not believe it. David asked him to test his father's mind and let him know it while he waited outside the city. So Jonathan spoke to his father about David, and learning that his father's purpose thirsted for the man's death, he reported everything to him secretly, in person, and urged him to save himself. He made David swear to remember him, and, if he should die, to show some kindness to his children. With these words Jonathan returned, and David, fleeing, came to Nob, to Ahimelech the high priest. He asked how he came alone. David said that the king had given him a secret charge, "and I have come alone so that no one may know of it; he ordered me to meet my young men here." With these words he asked for loaves. The high priest said he had no others but the holy ones, and "if you have kept yourselves from women, take these and eat." He also asked to be given a sword. The high priest answered that he had no other weapon but the sword of Goliath, which David himself had dedicated to God.

Taking these, David went off to Gath, to Achish, the king of the foreigners. Recognized for who he was, and afraid, he feigned madness and went about and behaved as one out of his mind. Having escaped from the foreigners in this way, he came to the tribe of Judah and hid in the cave of Adullam. There his brothers and kinsmen came to him; then others too flocked there, so that they were about four hundred in all. From there he went to the Moabites and asked the king of Moab to let his parents live with him until his affairs came to some end. He agreed and received Jesse and his household. David went into the land of Judah, at the bidding of the prophet Gad.

Saul, learning that a crowd had gathered round David, called his friends together and reproached them for siding with him. A slave of Saul, a Syrian by race, named Doeg, who had happened to be present standing by the high priest when he gave David the loaves and the sword of Goliath, told the king that he had seen David come to the high priest, consult God through him, and receive provisions and the sword. The king sent for the high priest and accused him of conspiring with David. He ordered the armed men standing by him to kill the high priest and all his family; and when they shrank from laying hands on those consecrated to God, he ordered Doeg to do the killing. He killed them all, three hundred and five of them, and struck their city, killing all of every age, sparing neither women nor infants nor any other age. One son of the high priest alone escaped death, called Abiathar, who fled to David and told him what had happened.

When the foreigners invaded Keilah and plundered the country, David, at God's bidding, attacked them with his four hundred, struck them down and brought back all the booty. Saul sent a multitude of soldiers to kill him there. He went away from Keilah into the desert, and Saul did not stop seeking to put him to death. Jonathan came to David, encouraged him and urged him to keep good hope. When David fled to the rock in the desert of Maon and Saul came on with a great multitude, David was in danger of being caught, had not a messenger come to Saul saying that the foreigners had attacked the country; for he left David and set out against the enemy.

Then it was reported to him that David was camping in the desert of En-gedi, and he set out with three thousand men. On the way he was troubled by his bowels. There was a cave there, in the inner part of which David and those with him were hiding; Saul went into it to relieve himself. David was urged by those with him to kill Saul. He would not be persuaded, but secretly cut off the skirt of his cloak and took it. When the king had gone out, David came out and called after Saul. The king looked back at the voice. David bowed down and said: "Do not believe the slanders. See, the Lord delivered you into my hands; for when I cut off the skirt of your cloak in the cave, it would have been easier for me to kill you." And at the same time he showed him the piece, and cried out: "May the Lord judge between me and you." Saul, pierced to the heart, raised his voice and said to David, weeping: "You are righteous; for I have been the cause of evils to you, but you have repaid me with good. So I am persuaded that God is keeping you to be king of Israel. Give me pledges, then, that you will not destroy my house, but preserve my line." David swore to Saul, and they parted.

## 1.32

At that time Samuel the prophet died too, a righteous and good man, who ruled Israel after Eli for twelve years alone and for eighteen years after Saul became king. David went off to the desert of Maon. He sent men to Nabal of Carmel, a rich man who was shearing his flocks, to greet him and ask him to give to those with him and to himself whatever he chose, since they had kept his flocks and shepherds unharmed. Nabal, who was harsh and reckless, answered David's men churlishly and shamelessly, saying: "Who is David? And who is the son of Jesse?" and calling him a runaway. David was furious at this, swore to destroy all that Nabal had, and set out against him with four hundred armed men. Nabal's wife, who was sensible and beautiful, learned how David had greeted her husband, how he had kept their property unharmed, and what insults Nabal had answered David; without saying anything to her own husband she loaded the asses with many gifts of every kind and went to David. Meeting him, she bowed down and begged him not to bear a grudge against her husband, for his mind was like his name (for Nabal means folly), but to let his anger go and accept what was brought. David praised the woman, pardoned them and went back. When the woman returned to Nabal, she told him of David's approach; and he, seized with fear and grief, lived no more than ten days and his life broke off. Learning this, David sent to Abigail to woo her for himself. She said she was not worthy to live with such a man, but went all the same; and David took her as his wife.

Saul was still plotting against him, and with three thousand picked men he camped by the hill of Hachilah, where David was staying with the six hundred men he had with him. At night David himself and Abishai went to Saul's camp and entered where Saul was sleeping. When Abishai set out to kill him, David did not let him; but taking the king's spear and his jar of water, they went out without anyone noticing, though Abner, the commander of his force, and many others lay round the king. Going up to the top of the mountain, David shouted to Abner: "Why do you not guard the king? See, some men went in to kill him, and you did not know. Look for the spear and the jar of water, and you will know what danger has passed you by." Saul recognized David's voice, and how he had spared him again when he could have killed him; he said that he had sinned and acted foolishly, and begged him to take heart and return to his home. David said: "May the Lord reward each according to his righteousness and his faithfulness."

David went off with the six hundred with him to Achish, the king of Gath, and stayed there four months. When the Philistines wished to march against Israel, Achish said to David: "Will you go out to the war with me, you and those with you?" And he readily promised to fight on his side. When the foreigners had already advanced into the country of the Israelites, Saul was alarmed and asked God whether he should fight. When He did not answer, he sought a woman who had a familiar spirit and went to her in disguise, so that he might not be recognized, and asked for the soul of Samuel to be brought up for him. When it seemed to have been brought up by certain spells, Saul said: "I am in great distress, for the foreigners have gathered against me and the Lord has left me." The apparition of Samuel answered: "The Lord has done to you what He foretold through me; He is taking the kingdom from you and giving it to David, and He is subjecting the people to the enemy; and you and your sons will fall tomorrow." Saul was greatly grieved at hearing this and went away.

The foreigners camped, and David and the six hundred with him at the rear. But it did not please the satraps that David should march with them, and they said: "He may do us some harm by going over to his own countrymen in the battle," and they asked that he be sent back. Achish, yielding to the satraps, told David to go away. He went off with those with him and found Ziklag, which Achish had given him to live in, burned, and both his wives and all who were there taken captive by the Amalekites. David and those with him lamented over this, and he asked God through the high priest Abiathar whether he should pursue them. God allowed it, and he pursued with four hundred, leaving two hundred to guard the baggage. Finding them scattered, eating and drinking, he fell on them suddenly and killed them all except four hundred who fled on swift camels, and recovered all the booty. When they returned, the four hundred who had been with David did not want the two hundred to share with them, but thought they should be content with having their wives saved. David judged this opinion unjust and declared that the spoil must be shared equally among all. This became a law from then on, ordering those who guard the baggage to share the spoils equally with those who fight.

When battle broke out between the Israelites and the Philistines, very many of the Hebrews were killed, and the three sons of Saul fell, and Saul himself was wounded below the ribs. Knowing that he had been struck mortally and would not live, he told his armour-bearer to run him through, so that he might not be taken alive by the enemy. When the man would not do as he was told, he fell on his own sword and died. His armour-bearer, seeing the king fallen, killed himself too. The next day the foreigners, stripping the fallen Israelites, came on the bodies of Saul and his sons on the mountains of Gilboa; they cut off their heads and sent them to their own country, making a show of them everywhere and spreading the good news, dedicated their armour to their own gods, and hung the bodies on the wall of Bethshan, which was afterwards called Scythopolis. When the inhabitants of the city of Jabesh in Gilead learned what had befallen Saul, the strongest of them went out, and pressing on through the whole night they took down the bodies of Saul and Jonathan, brought them to Jabesh, burned them, gathered the bones and buried them. Saul reigned eighteen years while Samuel was still alive, and twenty-two after his death.

## 2.1

David had just returned from the slaughter of the foreigners when a man came to him reporting the defeat of the Israelites and the death of Saul and Jonathan. Questioned whether his report was true, he said that Saul had been mortally wounded, and, being unable through weakness to kill himself, had begged him, so that he might not be taken alive by the enemy, to drive home the wound as he fell on his own sword; and that he had done as he was ordered and finished the king off. As proof of his words he held out the royal crown from Saul's head and the gold from his arms, and gave them to David. David and those with him lamented Saul and Jonathan, and he punished the man who had killed Saul, because he had laid his hand on the Lord's anointed. After this, at God's command, he went up to Hebron, a city of Judah, with both his wives, Ahinoam and Abigail, and the men with him with all their households; and there he was chosen king by the tribe of Judah. David blessed the men of Jabesh for having buried the bodies of Saul and Jonathan.

Abner, Saul's commander-in-chief, took the surviving son of his lord, Ishbosheth, and proclaimed him king over all Israel except Judah. Ishbosheth was forty years old when he became king, and he held the kingdom for two years. Abner was angry with the tribe of Judah for having made David king, and set out against it. Joab, David's commander-in-chief, met him with a force. First twelve from each army fought in single combat; when all of them had fallen, both armies attacked each other, and Abner's men were defeated. Abishai and Asahel were Joab's brothers. Asahel, proud of his swiftness of foot, pursued Abner from behind. Abner urged him to turn back, and when he could not persuade him, he struck him behind with a mortal blow and killed him. The dead man's brothers, angry at their brother's death, chased Abner. He stood on the top of one of the hills and called out to Joab: "Do not stir up men of the same nation to battle. Asahel too did wrong by not heeding my advice to turn back, and so he was wounded and died." Joab, taking these words as an appeal, ordered the trumpet to sound the recall and stopped the pursuit. From that time civil war began among the Hebrews and lasted very long, David's side growing stronger and Saul's growing weak.

Six sons were born to David by different wives while he was at Hebron, of whom the first-born was Amnon. When Abner lay with a concubine of Saul, Ishbosheth was angry with him because of his father's concubine. This was the reason for Abner's going over to David. David sent to Ishbosheth asking for his own wife Michal. He took her away from the husband to whom Saul had given her after David's flight, and sent her to David. Abner spoke with the elders of the Israelite multitude and the captains, and persuaded them to change over to David, all of them being eager for it. Having done this he came to David to receive sworn pledges. Welcomed gladly, he went off to gather the multitude, so that David might be proclaimed by all. Joab came to Hebron, learned what had happened about Abner, and, afraid that Abner would rise above him, said much against the man to David; failing to persuade him, he secretly sent someone to call Abner back as if from David, and Abner turned back. Joab met him, and leading him away from his men as if to tell him something secret, killed him, striking him with a sword under the flank. When David learned this he was deeply grieved, cursed the man who had killed him, lamented the dead man and buried him with honour.

A little later Ishbosheth was the victim of a plot and was murdered as he slept. His killers, who were two, took his head and brought it to David. He not only did not think the men had done him a favour, but ordered them to be put to death, exacting justice for the murder. Then all Israel went to David and anointed him king of all the tribes. David was thirty years old when he became king over Judah; he ruled at Hebron over the tribe of Judah alone for seven years, and over all Israel for thirty-three.

## 2.2

He left Hebron, he and those with him, and came to Jerusalem. At that time the Jebusites lived in the city and that land, which is why the city was called Jebus; they were akin by race to the Canaanites. In mockery they set the blind, the lame and the otherwise maimed on the battlements, and said that they would not let David enter the city. Angered by this, David took the city by siege, drove the Jebusites out, rebuilt it and named it after himself the City of David; in Abraham's time it was called Solyma. He lived in it, having built a palace. More children were born to him by several wives and concubines.

The foreigners marched against David, and he, with God's consent, went out against them with his army and won. They gathered again in much greater numbers and came on. When David asked God again, God ordered him to camp with the army in the groves of the Weeping, and not to attack the enemy until the groves began to shake. Doing this, he routed the enemy, and pursuing them as far as their borders he killed them.

After this David called the priests and Levites and all the people together to Kiriath-jearim. The priests put the ark of the Lord on a new cart to bring it down to Jerusalem, and the people and the king sang as they went before it. When the ark was in danger of being overturned, Uzzah the son of Abinadab stretched out his hand, held it and steadied it so that it should not fall. He died at once, God being angry because he had touched the ark, since he was not a priest; and from this that place was called the Breach. David was afraid to bring the ark into the city, and placed it in the house of Obed-edom, a Levite; it stayed there three months, and the house of that man enjoyed many blessings. Hearing this, David brought the ark from there to his own city, with seven choirs going before it; and he himself struck the lyre and danced in play. His wife Michal, seeing him dancing and leaping, reproached him for behaving unseemly; but he said that he was not ashamed, since these things were not unseemly when done for God. They brought in the ark and placed it in the tabernacle which David had pitched for it.

Then he wished to build a house for the ark. God commanded the prophet Nathan to tell David: "You shall not build me a house, but your son, who will succeed to your kingdom when you die." When the prophet told this to David, he turned to thanksgiving. After this he struck the foreigners and routed them and their allies, made Syria tributary and set a garrison in Damascus. On his way back he struck Idumaea, and all the Idumaeans became David's slaves.

Remembering his agreement with Jonathan the son of Saul, David inquired whether anyone of the family of Saul was left; and learning that a son of Jonathan survived, lame in his feet, called Mephibosheth, he sent for him, gave the man all his grandfather's property, ordered him to share his table always, and assigned him his father's servants as well; one of them, called Ziba, he entrusted with managing what had been given to his master and bringing him its revenue. Mephibosheth lived in Jerusalem and ate at the king's table.

## 2.3

About that time the king of the Ammonites, who was a friend of David, died, leaving a son, Hanun, who succeeded to his father's rule. David sent men to Hanun to console him in his grief for his father and to tell him that David would keep the friendship with him too. But Hanun, supposing that those sent were spies, shaved off half their beards, cut off half their cloaks and sent the men away. When David saw them in this state, he gave the flower of the army to Joab his commander-in-chief and sent him against the Ammonites. Joab engaged them and defeated them and their Syrian allies, and they turned to flight. They took on allies again and came to battle. David engaged them with his whole army and killed many, and Shobach, the commander of the enemy's force, was mortally wounded in the battle and died of the wound. The allies of the Ammonites, who were Syrians from Mesopotamia, surrendered to David. Again he sent Joab against the Ammonites; he shut them up in the chief city of their country and besieged it.

David, staying in Jerusalem, saw from the palace a woman bathing in her house, called Bathsheba, and fell in love with her. He sent for the woman and lay with her. She conceived and told the king. He called her husband, named Uriah, from the siege, asked him about the army, gave him food from his own table and bade him go to his house. But he did not go, saying it was not right for his general and his fellow soldiers to suffer hardship in the camp while he lay with his wife. When the king again urged him to go home, and plied him with toasts until he was almost drunk, he slept in front of the doors of the palace. So the king sent Uriah back to the camp, handing him a letter to Joab which ordered him to station Uriah where the enemy were hardest to fight, and for those with him to turn back and leave him there alone, so that he might be killed in this way.
