It was done as the king had ordered, and Uriah died. His wife mourned for her husband; and when she had finished mourning David took her as his wife, and she bore him a son.

God was angry and sent Nathan to David. He came to the king and said: "There were two men; one had flocks and herds, and the other owned one ewe lamb. When a guest came to the rich man, he did not sacrifice from his own, but took the poor man's one lamb, slaughtered it and feasted his friend." David sentenced the man who had done this to repay the lamb fourfold, and to die. Nathan said: "You have pronounced sentence against yourself, O king; for you are the one who has done this lawless deed." And he listed for him the adultery and the murder, and God's anger because of them, and foretold what he would suffer, and that the son born to him from the adultery would die. David cried out with deep feeling, "I have sinned against the Lord," and Nathan answered that the sin had been passed over by God.

Afterwards the child born of Bathsheba fell ill. David was deeply grieved; for seven days he would not eat, put on black, lay on sackcloth and prayed to God for the child's life. On the seventh day the child died; and learning this he rose, bathed, changed his clothes, gave thanks to God and ordered a table to be prepared for him. When the king's servants wondered at what had happened, he said: "While the child was still alive, I humbled myself and prayed, hoping that God would be moved"; but now that he was dead, grief and prayer for him were in vain. Bathsheba bore David another son, and he called him Solomon.

Joab, besieging the Ammonites, had the capture of the city very close, and told the king. He went there, took the city and gave it to the army to plunder; he took the crown of its king and wore it himself, which weighed a talent of gold and had a precious stone in it; and he destroyed the population of the city. He did the same to the other cities of the Ammonites, and returned to Jerusalem.

## 2.4

David had a daughter named Tamar, a full sister of Absalom. Amnon, David's first-born son, loved her, and his love was so great that the lover fell ill. When his father came to visit him, he asked that Tamar come to wait on him, and David consented. So Tamar came and prepared cakes for him. He sent everyone out and asked that his sister alone wait on him. When the girl was left alone, he forced her, and having violated her he at once felt a hatred opposite to his former love, told her to leave immediately and ordered his servant to throw her out. She, deeply grieved at the violence and the outrage, tore her tunic, sprinkled dust on her head and went away lamenting. Her brother Absalom advised her to keep quiet, but he nursed his anger against Amnon because of his sister. The king, learning what had happened, was distressed, but did not wish to grieve Amnon, since he was his first-born.

When two years had passed, Absalom was about to shear his own flocks, and he invited his brothers to a drinking party. He ordered his servants, as the drinking went on, to strike Amnon and kill him; and Absalom's servants did so. The other sons of the king, seeing what had happened, fled. When a rumour went ahead that Absalom had killed all the king's sons, David tore his clothes and fell on the ground lamenting. Meanwhile the king's sons arrived and, mourning, reported the murder of Amnon. Absalom fled to his grandfather on his mother's side, the king of Geshur, and stayed there for three years.

When anger had eased with time, Joab contrived to reconcile the king with Absalom. He prepared a woman to come to the king as if in mourning and lament that she had two sons, and one had killed the other, and her kinsmen were seeking to kill the survivor for his brother's murder; and to beg the king to grant her the boy's safety. She came to the king and said this with deep feeling, and the king promised to grant her the boy's life. The woman thanked him: "But so that I may have a pledge of what you have promised," she said, "first be reconciled yourself with your own son, and do not add a second, willing death to the death of the son who died against your will." The king understood that the woman's words were a pretext and had been devised by Joab, and allowed him to bring Absalom back. Joab went and brought him to Jerusalem; but he was not allowed to come into his father's sight.

After this the king was reconciled with Absalom, called him, pardoned him when he asked forgiveness, and promised him an amnesty for his offence. Absalom acquired horses and chariots and many followers, went every day to the palace, and by speaking to the people to win their favour made many well disposed to him. When four years had gone by since his return, he made the pretext of going to Hebron to sacrifice, as he had vowed to do while in exile; and his father let him go. Once there, he sent to many, and very many flocked to him, among others Ahithophel, his father's counsellor. And he was proclaimed king by all.

Learning this, David left Jerusalem with those about him; and the six hundred men who had been with him in Saul's time set out with him again. It was reported to him too that Ahithophel had gone over to Absalom, which made his fear greater and his grief more. He called on God and entrusted the judgement of everything to Him. Hushai, a companion of David, deserted to Absalom in pretence, as David wished. As David went away, Ziba, the servant of Mephibosheth, came to him leading a pair of asses carrying provisions, and accused his master of staying in Jerusalem and expecting that the kingdom would come round to him, as the people would choose him. David was indignant at this, and gave Ziba everything he had given Mephibosheth before. Then on the road Shimei, a man of Saul's family, came at him, threw stones at the king, cursed him and called him a man of blood and a lawbreaker. When those with David set out to kill Shimei, the king did not allow it, saying: "The Lord has told him to do this; leave him alone." Coming to the Jordan, he let those with him, who were worn out, refresh themselves there.

## 2.5

When Absalom came to Jerusalem, he said to Hushai when he came to him: "Why have you, my father's companion, left him and come to me?" He said: "I will be with the one chosen by God, and follow the one preferred by all the people." Absalom asked Ahithophel what he ought to do. He advised him to lie with his father's concubines, "so that all may know that your enmity towards your father admits no truce." Absalom did as this advice said. When Ahithophel asked for ten thousand men, so that he might pursue King David at once while he and those with him were exhausted with toil, strike him down and bring the others back, Absalom asked Hushai about the advice. He declared it was not good, saying: "You know the courage and experience of your father and those with him. I think you should gather an army from the whole country and so set out yourself against your father and command in the war; and you will surely win, clashing with few when you have many." Absalom and those with him said: "The counsel of Hushai is better than the counsel of Ahithophel." Hushai told the priests Zadok and Abiathar to make all this known to David. They sent men to David to tell him what had happened. Absalom was told about the men and pursued them. Learning this, they took refuge in someone's house, went down into a well and hid there unseen; and coming out of it they reported to David what they had been told. He crossed the Jordan by night with those with him. Ahithophel, outdone in reputation and knowing that evil would befall Absalom, went off to his house and hanged himself.

David counted those with him and found four thousand. He divided the army into three parts and set a general over each; he himself was not allowed by his friends to march out with them, for they said that if they were routed with him there they would lose all hope, but while he was safe some hope was still left them. So he sent his people out to fight, and begged them to spare Absalom if they won. Battle was joined, and David's men prevailed and pursued Absalom as he fled. He was riding a mule, and as he went into a wood his hair caught in a great oak (for he grew his hair very long), and he was left hanging from the oak while the mule went on. Someone, seeing Absalom hanging like this, reported it to Joab. Joab shot him with three darts through the heart and killed him, and sent off a man to report the victory to the king. When the victory was announced to him, he asked whether his son was alive; and hearing that he was dead, he lamented. Joab came to the king and by what he said persuaded him to stop lamenting; and when he had stopped and appeared before the soldiers, he praised them. All submitted to him as before.

He went off to Jerusalem; and as he went Shimei met him, asking pardon for the wrong he had done. David swore to forgive him his offence too, and all who had wronged him. Mephibosheth also met him. The king asked why he had not shared his flight. He said that Ziba was most to blame for this, for not preparing what he needed for the journey, while he himself, being lame in his feet, could not follow; and that Ziba had also slandered him maliciously and lied about him. At this David pardoned him, and ordered half his property to be restored to him.

## 2.6

When the king reached Gilgal, all the people of Israel gathered there, complaining that the tribe of Judah had come to him secretly. Those of Judah said they ought not to be blamed for this, being of the king's own kin. This answer seemed harsh to the people. A Benjamite, seditious and wicked, called Sheba, cried out: "We have no portion in David, nor inheritance in the son of Jesse"; and blowing a horn he made all fall away from David, only the tribe of Judah staying with him. The king appointed Amasa general and sent him out to gather an army from the tribe of Judah and come back within three days. When Amasa delayed, he ordered Joab to take the force that was there and set out against Sheba, "so that," he said, "the enemy may not be better prepared through our delay." Joab at once set out to war. As he went with a great force, Amasa met him; and Joab, as Amasa came up to him, let the sword he wore fall out of its scabbard, picked it up from the ground, and coming close to Amasa to embrace him, thrust the sword into his belly and killed him. Taking his men too, he pursued Sheba, who took refuge in a strong city. Joab besieged the city, and a sensible woman cried out to him from the wall: "Why do you wish to destroy a mother city of Israel?" He answered: "Because of Sheba." She went to the citizens and persuaded them to cut off Sheba's head and throw it out. When this had been done, Joab raised the siege and went off to Jerusalem, and was appointed again commander-in-chief of the whole force.

After this there was a famine, and for three years it pressed on the people. When the king inquired about it, God revealed that the famine came because of the Gibeonites, and that they must have vengeance for those of them whom Saul had killed, not keeping the oaths made to them by Joshua and the council of elders. So the king called the Gibeonites and asked what they wished done for them in atonement; they asked for seven men of the house of Saul to be given to them. The king gave them the seven men, as they asked, sparing Mephibosheth; and they took the men and killed them. And God brought down rain on the earth.

When there was war after this with the foreigners, David pursued the enemy with his own army. He became worn out and slackened from exhaustion; and one of the enemy, seeing him so, rushed to strike him down, but Abishai, the brother of Joab, shielded the king and killed the foreigner. Because of this David's men swore that they would never again allow him to go out to war. When the foreigners took the field once more, the king sent an army against them and caused many to be killed and the survivors to flee. After a short interval the foreigners came again against the country of the Israelites. There was among them a man of giant size, with six fingers on each hand and likewise on his feet; Jonathan, David's nephew, grappled with him, threw him down, stripped him of his armour and turned the scale towards victory for his countrymen. The foreigners did not again attempt to fight the Hebrews.

## 2.7

Having peace from then on, David composed songs to God, and making instruments he taught the Levites to sing the hymns with them. The instruments were these: the lyre had ten strings and was struck with a plectrum; the harp, with twelve notes, was plucked with the fingers; and there were cymbals of bronze, large and broad. About David there was also a company of brave men; the most distinguished of them were thirty-seven, who often won the prize of valour. Once, when the foreigners were camped at Bethlehem, the king longed to drink water from the well at Bethlehem. Three of the brave men about him ran off, went through the middle of the enemy's camp and drew water from that well; and passing back through the enemy, who were struck by their daring and kept still, they brought the water to the king. But he said: "The Lord be merciful to me, I will not drink this, brought at the cost of these men's blood and the danger of their lives." So he did not drink it, but poured it out as an offering to God.

Wishing to number the whole people, he ordered Joab to carry out the count. When Joab did not approve the plan and said the deed was not good, the king would not listen. Joab went and numbered the people, and the number of Israel was eight hundred thousand men of strength who drew the sword, and five hundred thousand fighting men of the tribe of Judah. Then David repented and confessed that he had sinned. The prophet Gad, at God's command, went to David and said: "Choose from these three whichever you will: do you choose that for seven years there be famine in your country, or that for three months you be pursued by your enemies, or that death fall on your people for three days?" He said: "I am hemmed in on every side, but it is better for me to fall into the hands of God." Death came on the people from morning until the hour of the midday meal, and seventy thousand of all the people died. David besought God. The angel destroying the people stretched out his hand over Jerusalem. Then the king said: "I have sinned, Lord, I the shepherd; but the flock, the people, have not sinned. I deserve punishment, and not they." God, moved by the king's contrition, stopped the destruction, and through the prophet Gad ordered him to set up an altar on the threshing floor of Araunah the Jebusite and sacrifice there. David did so, buying the threshing floor from Araunah. As Josephus wrote, it was to that place that Abraham is said to have brought Isaac to offer him as a burnt offering. David wished to build a temple to God there too, but was prevented by God.

When David had grown very old, he could not keep warm because of his age. So they chose a young girl, named Abishag, and the girl lay with the king and warmed him. And he did not know her; for because of his age he could not have intercourse. Adonijah, the fourth of the king's sons, very handsome, seeing his father grown old, laid claim to the kingdom, with the help of Joab the commander-in-chief and Abiathar the high priest. Once he prepared a lavish sacrifice and invited his brothers, the leading men of the tribe of Judah, Abiathar and Joab; but he did not invite Solomon, the prophet Nathan, or Benaiah, the captain of the bodyguard. So Nathan said to Bathsheba: "Have you heard that Adonijah has become king?" She went in to the king and said: "You swore, my lord the king, that Solomon your son would reign after you; and see, Adonijah has become king, and you do not know." While Bathsheba was still speaking, the prophet Nathan too came in and asked the king whether Adonijah had become king with his consent. He swore by God: "Today Solomon shall be king." Calling Zadok the high priest and Benaiah, he ordered them, taking the prophet and the armed men of the royal court, to set Solomon on the king's mule, take him outside the city to the spring called Gihon, anoint him with the holy oil, proclaim him king with the sounding of horns, and escort him through the middle of the city. They did at once as they were ordered, and the proclamation of Solomon was noised everywhere. When it became known to Adonijah and those feasting with him, the rest went off to their homes, but Adonijah, the more afraid, fled to the altar and asked Solomon for pledges of his safety. Solomon pardoned him his offence for the time; "but if he is found doing anything evil again," he said, "he shall be put to death."

David, seeing death drawing near, called Solomon and charged him to keep God's commandments and live by His laws, "so that our seed," he said, "as the Lord promised me, may be on my throne for ever." He added that he should not leave Joab unpunished, who had killed Abner and Amasa, nor Shimei, who had cursed him in his flight. He gave him other charges too, and bade him build a temple to the Lord, gave him the plan for the building of the temple, and roused him, the rulers and the tribe of Levi to the work with words, saying that he was leaving much for the building of the temple: ten thousand talents of gold, a hundred thousand talents of silver, emeralds and other costly stones, bronze and iron beyond number and other material in abundance; and that he was now adding to what had already been gathered three thousand talents of pure gold for the inner sanctuary, and ten thousand staters, and ten thousand talents of silver. And whoever had a precious stone brought it and handed it over to the treasuries. A little later David died, having lived seventy years and reigned seven years at Hebron and thirty-three at Jerusalem, a man who had achieved every virtue fitting for a king. His son Solomon buried him royally in Jerusalem and buried great wealth with him.

## 2.8

Solomon was very young when he received the kingdom, in the twelfth year of his age. But Adonijah went to Bathsheba, Solomon's mother, and said: "You know that the kingdom belonged to me both by seniority and by the people's choice; but it has passed to Solomon my brother, and I must accept what has happened as having happened by God's will. I make one request: that the girl who lay with my father, Abishag, be given me in marriage, since my father did not know her because of his age, but she is still a virgin." Bathsheba promised to put his words to her son and to bring about the marriage for him with all zeal. She went to her son and begged him to give Abishag to his brother Adonijah. He, angered at the request, said he wondered that she did not ask him to give up the kingdom to Adonijah as the elder. At once he ordered Benaiah, the captain of the bodyguard, to kill Adonijah. He ordered Abiathar to go to his native place and live on his estates. "For," he said, "all the toils you shared with my father save you from death." So, as God had foretold to Eli, the honour of the priesthood was taken from the house of Ithamar and passed to Zadok, who was of the line of Phinehas. Joab the general, learning this and terrified, fled to the altar, and when called said he would never come out, but would die there and nowhere else. This was reported to the king and persuaded him to send men to kill Joab there. From then on Benaiah was appointed general of the whole force, and Zadok was granted the high priesthood. Shimei the king confined to Jerusalem, so that he might not leave it; if he did, he fixed death as his penalty. Three years later, when some slaves of his ran away, he went out in search of them; and learning this, Solomon ordered the man to be put to death.

Solomon married a daughter of Pharaoh, and raised the walls of Jerusalem higher and made them stronger. God spoke to him in his sleep and told him to ask what he wished; and he asked to be given understanding and wisdom. God, pleased with his request, promised to give him wisdom and a great mind such as no one before him or after him had, and to add what he had not asked, wealth, victory and glory, if he kept His commandments and ordinances.

After this two women came to him. One said: "O king, we lived together and no one with us. We both gave birth to a boy at the same time. A little later this woman lay on her son, and when she woke she found him dead. While I slept she put the dead child in my arms, took the living one from me and claimed it as her own. When I discovered the deed, I asked for my child, but she will not give him to me." The other insisted that the living child was hers and the dead one her opponent's, and swore she had not gone so far in wickedness. Those standing by were at a loss how the truth might be found, but the king ordered the living child to be cut in two with a sword and half given to each of the women. The true mother of the baby took this hard, begged him to withdraw the sentence and entreated that the child be given alive to her opponent; but the one who falsely called herself the boy's mother praised the judgement and urged on the dividing. And the king ordered the child to be given alive to the woman who begged that he not be divided, saying: "This is his mother, who was grieved beyond bearing when the child was about to be slaughtered." All the people took this as a sure proof of the king's wisdom.

He surpassed all who had gone before in wisdom and understanding. He composed three thousand parables and five thousand songs, and wrote about everything from the cedar to the hyssop; he studied and discoursed on the nature of animals, those that walk, fly and swim, and their properties; and against demons he discovered spells for the good of men and left methods of exorcism by which demons were driven out. Josephus says that this cure was still effective in his own day, and tells a story of this kind to confirm it. He says that Eleazar, one of his countrymen, had a ring with, under its seal, a root of the kind Solomon had shown; he would hold it to the nostrils of the possessed man, and with the smell draw the demon out through the sufferer's nostrils; then, invoking Solomon, he would say spells from those Solomon had composed and adjure the demon never to come back into the man. He says Eleazar did this in the presence of Vespasian and his sons; and to give proof of his power over the demons he would set down a cup full of water and command the demon, as it came out of the sufferer, to overturn it; and the cup would be overturned with no one seeing who overturned it.

## 2.9

He began building the temple in the fourth year of his reign, four hundred and forty years after the people's departure from Egypt. About the measurements of its height and length and about its construction, the Third Book of Kingdoms and Josephus, writing about the temple in the eighth book of the Antiquities, do not agree, but differ in most things. Whoever wishes to examine the differences closely, and about the two cherubim which he made of gold and set in the inner sanctuary, the ark, the bronze sea and the basins, how large the bronze altar was, what the golden table was like, how many vessels of silver and gold Solomon dedicated to the temple, and the rest, will find the Third Book of Kingdoms and Josephus in the Antiquities enough to set out the exact details of each.

The king finished the temple and everything about it in seven years, and called the people together to Jerusalem. The priests lifted the ark, the tabernacle which Moses had pitched and the vessels used in the sacrifices and carried them to the temple, with the king and all the multitude going before, and the Levites pouring libations and burning incense. The ark was placed in the inner sanctuary between the two cherubim, with the two stone tablets inside it. When the priests came out of the inner sanctuary, the house was filled with the glory of the Lord and a cloud was spread over the whole temple, from which they imagined that God had come to the temple and taken up His dwelling in it. The king, glad at this, gave thanks to God, blessed the people and prayed that the Divine would hear all who prayed in it. Then he brought sacrifices to the altar. And God, showing that He had received them graciously, made fire flash through the air and come down on the altar and consume the whole sacrifice. Seeing this, the people blessed God and the king gave thanks; and after feasting lavishly they broke up the gathering. A dream that came to the king showed that God had heard his prayer, and that the temple would be preserved and he himself raised to the height of good fortune, and that his descendants would rule the country, if he and they and the people did not transgress the divine commands; but if they did, He threatened to cut off his race root and branch, to subject the people to slavery and countless afflictions, to give the temple over to burning and plunder, and the city to destruction and pillage.

So Solomon completed the temple. After this he built himself a palace, costly and splendid, strengthened the walls of Jerusalem further, built other cities as well, and brought under him the Canaanites on Mount Lebanon, who had not been subdued before, and made them tributary. Hiram, the king of the Tyrians, sent him puzzles and riddling sayings, asking him to explain them; and he solved them all and showed their meaning. Josephus records that ancient writers mention this, Dius and besides him Menander.

When the wisdom and understanding of Solomon were noised everywhere, a queen of Egypt and Ethiopia, who loved and sought wisdom, came to Jerusalem. The king received her with honour and easily solved the puzzles she set, so that the queen was astonished and said she saw more than she had heard. She also admired the palace, the lavishness of the dinners, the whole royal service, the sacrifices in the temple and the good order of those who sacrificed. She presented the king with gold, precious stones, spices beyond telling and roots of balsam, so that from her the balsam came to grow in Palestine. Solomon repaid the queen with many good things, and she went back to her own country.

## 2.10

But the king, who had become the most glorious of all before him and excelled in wisdom and wealth, did not abide by the divine ordinances. He fell sick with incontinence in love and went mad for women; he was not content with women of his own race, but married very many foreigners too, and to please them, for love's sake, he worshipped their gods as well. For he married seven hundred daughters of famous rulers, and had three hundred concubines, and the daughter of the king of the Egyptians. So the Lord said to Solomon: "Because you have not kept what I commanded you, I will tear your kingdom apart and give it to your servant. Yet I will not take the rule from you while you live, for your father's sake; but when you have died I will do this to your son. Nor will I take the whole kingdom from him; leaving him two tribes and Jerusalem, for the sake of his grandfather David, I will give the ten to his servant."

Not much time passed before Hadad became an enemy to Israel. Hadad was an Idumaean of royal descent, who as a boy had fled to Egypt when Joab subdued Idumaea in David's time; he was kindly received by Pharaoh, grew dear to him and married the sister of Pharaoh's wife. When he had grown to manhood and learned that David and Joab were dead, he asked to go back to his ancestral realm, but Pharaoh would not allow it. When Solomon's affairs were already going badly, Hadad was allowed to go and returned to Idumaea. Since it was securely guarded, he went to Syria, and gathering a band of robbers about him he took possession of it and plundered the land of the Hebrews.

Jeroboam, the son of Nebat, served the king as a youth. Solomon, seeing that he was noble in spirit, put him in charge of the building when he set a wall round Jerusalem. Once, as Jeroboam was going somewhere, the prophet Ahijah the Shilonite met him, and taking him aside from the road tore his own cloak into twelve pieces and gave him ten, saying: "So will the Lord tear the kingdom of Solomon; to his son He will leave two tribes for his grandfather's sake, but to you He will give the ten and make you king over them. But you, keep to the laws of the Lord and be just." Elated by this, Jeroboam attempted a revolt. The king learned of the matter and sought to kill him. He fled to Egypt and stayed there until Solomon died.

King Solomon died, as the book of Kingdoms records, having lived fifty-two years (for he is recorded to have come to the throne at twelve) and reigned forty years; but as Josephus wrote, he lived ninety-four years and reigned eighty. He lived happily and gloriously, but broke the law in his old age, persuaded by unholy loves for foreign women and following their worship.

## 2.11

He was succeeded in the kingdom by his son Rehoboam. All the people gathered before him and begged him to lighten for them the yoke of slavery which Solomon had laid on them, and to show himself kinder than his father. He said he would consider it and answer within three days. So he called the older of his father's attendants and asked what he should answer the people; and they advised him to speak to them gently, not haughtily or pompously. After the elders he shared the question with the young men who had been brought up with him, telling them the advice of the older men too. They advised him to address the people more harshly and make his words fit the grandeur of his rule and the royal dignity. He sided with them, and when the multitude had gathered again he said: "If my father made your yoke hard, I will put a heavier one on you; and if he used whips against you, I will punish you with scorpions; and you will find my little finger thicker than my father's loins." When the multitude heard this they cried out, "What portion have we in David?" and went away in anger. When Rehoboam sent one of his household to talk to them and calm them, they stoned the man to death in their rage. Rehoboam, afraid, fled to Jerusalem; the tribe of Judah stayed with him, and with it the tribe of Benjamin. The rest, since Jeroboam had already returned from Egypt after Solomon's death, called him and chose him king. Rehoboam prepared to fight the Israelites and Jeroboam for the kingdom, but was prevented by God through the prophet Shemaiah.

Jeroboam, afraid that the multitude, going to Jerusalem and mixing with the people there, might turn back to their former king and abandon him, made two heifers of gold, and set one at Bethel and the other at Dan. Calling the multitude to an assembly he said: "Men, God is everywhere, and not only in Jerusalem. See, I have made two heifers in the name of God, and you no longer need to go to Jerusalem. Go to them, bow down and sacrifice; and I will appoint priests from among yourselves." Deceived by this, the people transgressed their ancestral customs and provoked God, so that Israel was given over into captivity to the foreigners.

Jeroboam made priests and an altar and held a festival, and went up to the altar himself with the priests about him. As he was about to sacrifice, a prophet sent by God stood before him and said: "Thus says the Lord: Altar, there shall be one of David's line called Josiah, who will sacrifice your priests on you and burn their bones. And so that it may be clear that my words are true, see, the altar will be split, and the fat on it poured out on the ground." At once the altar split and the fat of the victims was poured out. Jeroboam stretched out his hand, ordering the prophet to be seized; but it withered and stayed motionless. When Jeroboam begged the prophet, his hand moved again and worked. He asked him to dine with him. The prophet would not consent, saying that God had forbidden him to eat bread or drink water in that city, or to return by the road by which he had come into the city; and he went off by another way.

There was in that city a false prophet who deceived Jeroboam and spoke to please him. Learning what the prophet of God had said and done and that he was leaving, he pursued him, and overtaking him asked him to turn back and be his guest. When he refused, since God had forbidden it, that wicked man said: "But I too am a prophet, and I have come at God's command to bring you back to share my table." The prophet believed the false prophet's words and returned. While he was a guest there, the word of the Lord came to the prophet that, because he had not kept what he had been commanded, he would be killed on the road by a lion. As he went back a lion killed him, and sat by and guarded the body and the beast of burden. The false prophet, learning what had happened, brought in the dead man's body and buried it, and ordered his own sons to bury his own body with the prophet when he died. What was his scheme in this? That when, according to the prophet's prophecy, the altar was razed and the bones of the priests and false prophets given to the fire, he himself might escape the outrage by being laid with the man of God.

## 2.12

So these things happened. Jeroboam kept to the same impiety, and even went further in breaking the law every day, and offered the false priesthood of the high places for sale for money to any who wished. This deed was reckoned to him as a sin, and brought about the disappearance and ruin of his house. If selling that unholy priesthood for money was reckoned a sin, what could one say of those who sell and buy the truly divine priesthood, that which performs the awesome and bloodless sacrifice?

Abijah, the son of Jeroboam, fell ill, and the king sent his wife to the prophet Ahijah to ask whether he would live. She put on the dress of a common woman and went. The prophet, seeing through the play, said: "Do not hide yourself, wife of Jeroboam. Go and tell your husband: Since you have abandoned God and made yourself molten gods, expect that the kingdom will be taken from you and you yourself destroyed with all your family; and because the multitude followed you in your impiety, that too will not stay unpunished. But go, woman, and you will find your child dead, and he will be buried with lamentation; for he alone of Jeroboam's line was good." The woman went away, found the child dead, and told her husband what the prophet had said. But he was not made any better by it at all, being without understanding.

While Rehoboam, the son of Solomon, was reigning in Jerusalem, the priests and Levites among the Israelites, and those of the multitude who understood what was good, left their own cities and came to Jerusalem; and from this Rehoboam's kingdom grew. But he too was impious towards God, and the whole house of Judah neglected the worship of God. So Shishak, the king of Egypt, came up against Jerusalem with a heavy force in the fifth year of Rehoboam's reign. Rehoboam, shutting himself and the people up in the city, prayed to God to grant them safety, and they confessed their sins against God. God, entreated, said He would not destroy them, but would make them subject to the Egyptians. So when Rehoboam received the Egyptian into the city on terms, he broke the treaty, plundered the treasures of God, emptied the royal ones, and took away all the golden shields which Solomon had made and the golden spears which David had dedicated. Rehoboam died having lived fifty-seven years, of which he reigned seventeen, leaving his son Abijah as successor.

Against him came Jeroboam, who had always been at war with Rehoboam too. Abijah did not cower at the attack, but set his own men against him and won a famous victory, so that Jeroboam no longer dared to face him. Abijah died having reigned three years in Jerusalem, having done evil in the sight of the Lord; and his son Asa became king, who kept the divine ordinances like David his forefather.

Jeroboam also died, having held the rule for twenty-two years. He was succeeded by his son Nadab, who was as impious as his father. While besieging Gibbethon, a city of the foreigners, he was the victim of a plot by Baasha, the son of Ahijah, and died after ruling two years. Baasha, having gained the kingdom of the ten tribes, destroyed the whole race of Jeroboam. He too broke the law like Jeroboam and made the people sin likewise. God threatened him through the prophet Jehu to destroy his house like the house of Jeroboam. He too died, having reigned over Israel for twenty-four years. Elah his son succeeded to his father's kingdom. Zimri, the commander of his cavalry, killed him while he was drunk, after he had reigned two years, took the kingdom from him and destroyed the whole race of Baasha. The army of the Israelites, which was besieging Gibbethon, learning that Zimri had killed Elah and become king, proclaimed their own general, Omri, in the camp, and, seizing the city of Tirzah with him first, held it. Zimri, the first, went into the innermost part of the palace and set it on fire, destroying himself with it, after holding the rule seven days. Then the people were divided: one part chose Omri, the other Tibni. But Omri's men prevailed, killed Tibni and secured the rule for Omri without dispute. He reigned first at Tirzah, then on the mountain of Shemer, on which he built the city that is called Samaria, for he called the mountain Samareon after Shemer, from whom he had bought it. He was worse than those before him. He ended his life in Samaria, having reigned twelve years; and the kingdom passed to his son Ahab.

So the kings of the Israelites were destroyed one after another within a short time because of their lawlessness. But Asa, the king of Jerusalem, was dear to God. In the tenth year of his reign the king of the Ethiopians marched against him with a heavy force. He called on God as his ally, and engaging the Ethiopians he killed many, pursued the rest as they fled and plundered their camp, and he and the people carried off much spoil. As the king and the army were returning, the prophet Azariah met him and said that God had given the victory to him and to the people because they cared for justice; and he promised them happiness in the future too if they pursued virtue, but much sorrow if they neglected God's commandments. Asa, having spent his life in piety and reached a great age, died after reigning forty-one years, leaving the rule to his son Jehoshaphat.

## 2.13

Ahab held the palace in Samaria, outrunning those before him in impiety, and drawn further into wickedness by his wife Jezebel, who was the daughter of the king of Tyre and Sidon, a reckless and bold woman. She built a temple to her own god Bel, planted a grove and appointed priests and false prophets for him. Elijah the prophet, from the city of Tishbe, zealous at the impieties of the king and the people, said to Ahab: "As the Lord lives, there shall be no rain except by my word." With these words he went away to the brook Cherith, at God's command; there he was fed by ravens, which brought him bread in the morning and meat in the evening, and the brook gave him drink. When it dried up, obeying God, he went to Zarephath, a city between Sidon and Tyre. Meeting a woman before the city, he begged her to bring him water. As the woman went off, he asked her to bring him bread as well. She swore she had only one handful of flour and very little oil, and that she was gathering sticks to go and make a little food for herself and her children; when it was gone, they too would perish of hunger. The prophet told her to take heart; for neither would flour fail from the jar that held it nor oil from the flask until there was rain. She went, and fed the prophet, who lodged with her, and herself and her children from that very little flour until the famine had passed.

When this woman's son fell ill and died, she railed against the prophet. He asked to be given the dead child. Taking him, he carried him up to the upper room where he lived and laid him on his bed; and praying to God he breathed three times on the dead boy, and he came back to life. He gave the boy alive to his mother, and she gave thanks.

After this God commanded Elijah to go and tell Ahab beforehand that there would be rain. The famine had grown severe in Samaria and the whole country. Ahab took his steward Obadiah, a good man, and went out to see if he could find fodder for the horses. He went one way and Obadiah another, searching where they might find grass by springs or brooks. Elijah met Obadiah and asked him to tell the king about him. Obadiah said that Ahab was searching hard for him, to kill him if he found him, and advised him to hide; he said that he himself was hiding a hundred prophets, feeding them and so saving them, since Jezebel had killed all the others. Elijah swore that he would be seen by Ahab that very day. So Obadiah went and told the king about Elijah. When he saw the prophet, he asked whether he was the man who was perverting Israel. Elijah said: "Rather you and your father's house are perverting the people, abandoning the Lord and honouring false gods. But gather the people at Carmel, and your prophets and your wife's."

When all had gathered, Elijah said: "I alone am left as a prophet of God, but the false prophets are very many. Give me alone one ox, and them all another; let us sacrifice the oxen and put the victims on wood, but not set fire to it; and let each call on his own god; and the one by whom fire blazes out of itself and burns up the wood and the victims, let him be reckoned the true God." The multitude approved his words, and the priests of shame did so: they sacrificed the calf and put it on the wood without fire, and called on Baal from morning until noon. The prophet, mocking them, advised them to shout loudly, in case their god was asleep or had some business. When nothing came of their many prayers, the prophet took twelve stones, one for each tribe, built an altar, dug a trench, stacked the split wood on the altar, put the victims on it, and ordered water to be poured over the altar until the trench was filled. Then he called on God, and fire came down from heaven on the altar as all watched, and consumed the sacrifice, the wood and the stones, the water and the dust. The people, amazed, fell on the ground, confessing one God, true and great. At the prophet's command they seized the false prophets and killed them. He told the king that God would send rain soon, and as he had foretold there was a violent downpour.

When Jezebel threatened to kill Elijah, he was afraid and fled, and in the desert, in despair, he prayed God that he might die. He fell asleep and was woken by someone telling him to get up and eat. He saw there a loaf of spelt and water. He ate and slept again, and again the angel told him to eat, since a long journey lay before him. He ate again, and strengthened by that food he journeyed forty days. Coming to Mount Horeb he went into a cave, and heard a voice: "Why have you come here?" He said: "Because in my zeal I killed Jezebel's prophets, and she is seeking to kill me." The voice answered him again: "Go out tomorrow and stand before the Lord." He did so, and felt a wind and an earthquake, and saw a fire burning. Then, when a gentle breeze came, he heard a voice from it bidding him go back and anoint Hazael king of Syria, Jehu king over Israel, and Elisha prophet in his own place, who would destroy the impious. Elijah went back as he was told and found Elisha ploughing, and threw his mantle on him. He left everything and followed him, having been judged worthy to prophesy.

## 2.14

Naboth, who owned a vineyard near the fields of King Ahab, was asked to sell it to the king or exchange it, but he would not agree; and the king was grieved at this. Jezebel, learning the cause of his grief, wrote a letter as from Ahab to the leading men of the district where Naboth lived, ordering them to accuse the man of having blasphemed against God and the king, to prepare some men to bear witness against him, and to stone him. She sealed this letter with the king's seal and sent it to the men. They did as they were ordered: Naboth was stoned and died, and Jezebel told Ahab to inherit Naboth's vineyard now that he was dead. He was grieved at the murder, but took the vineyard for himself. Elijah, at God's command, went to Ahab and said: "Because you have murdered Naboth to get his vineyard, therefore thus says the Lord: Where the dogs licked his blood, there they will lick your blood and your wife's, and the harlots will bathe in your blood, and your whole race will be destroyed." At this Ahab was pierced to the heart; he wept, put on sackcloth and fasted, repenting of what had been done. God, because of his repentance, revealed to the prophet that He would not bring on him what He had threatened, but would fulfil it in the days of his son.

The son of Hadad, king of Syria, marched against Samaria with a heavy force and many allies. Besieging it, he sent to Ahab, saying: "Your wealth, your wives and your children are mine; for I will take them in war. But if you let me take whatever I wish, I will raise the siege and go away." Ahab said: "I and all mine are yours." He gathered the elders of the land and told them what the enemy had sent to him. The multitude advised him not to give in to him. He told the enemy's envoys that he could not do what was demanded. This, reported to the king of Syria, roused him to anger, and he ordered a palisade to be thrown round the city and mounds raised. A prophet told Ahab that God was giving the enemy into his hands if he attacked them with the young men of the princes; he counted the young men and found two hundred and thirty-two. While the enemy were feasting and drunk at midday, Ahab went out taking the young men. The king of Syria, seeing them, ordered some of his men to bring those who were coming to him bound. The young men engaged their attackers and killed many. The whole force of Israel, following behind the young men, suddenly charged the Syrians and put them to flight, so that their king barely escaped and was saved. Returning from the pursuit, Ahab plundered the enemy's camp and went back to Samaria.

The prophet foretold to him another attack by the Syrian. When spring had come, the Syrian marched again against Israel and camped in a plain; for those about him told him that the God of the Hebrews had power on mountains, but not in valleys. The prophet promised Ahab victory, "so that," he said, "God may show His strength in valleys too." For some days the armies kept quiet, but on the seventh battle was joined and the Syrians fled; and Ahab pursued and killed them. Many who escaped to the city of Aphek perished there when the wall fell on them. The son of Hadad fled with a few and hid. Those about him put on sackcloth, tied ropes round their heads, came to Ahab and begged him to spare their lord; and he consented. They brought their leader to Ahab as he rode in his chariot. He took him up into the chariot, embraced him and told him to take heart. Then he let him go on terms, after giving him many gifts.

The prophet Micaiah asked one of his countrymen to strike him on the head. When he would not, he foretold that he would be struck down by a lion; and the prediction came true. Then he made the same request of another; when he had struck him, he bound up his head, came to Ahab and said that he had received a prisoner from his captain to guard, and when he escaped the captain was going to kill him. When Ahab said this was just, he unbound his head and was recognized as the prophet Micaiah. He said to Ahab: "Since you have let the king of Syria, a deadly man, go unpunished, you yourself will die in his place, and your people in place of his people." Ahab was grieved at hearing this.

After three years of peace, wishing to take from the Syrians the city of Ramoth, as belonging to him, he asked Jehoshaphat, the king of Jerusalem, to march out with him. Jehoshaphat consented, and advised him to ask God through a prophet whether He approved. Ahab called his own prophets together and asked whether he should go to war. When they promised victory, King Jehoshaphat asked for a prophet of the Lord. Ahab said there was one, but that he loathed him because he foretold evil. Jehoshaphat asked for him to be called. When Micaiah was called, Ahab asked him whether he should fight or not. He said: "I have seen Israel scattered on the mountains like a flock without a shepherd. The Lord told me that they would be saved and return, but that you alone would fall at Ramoth." When Micaiah had said this, Ahab said to Jehoshaphat: "Did I not tell you that he always prophesies evil for me?" Zedekiah, one of the false prophets, struck Micaiah on the cheek. He said to him: "Soon you will go from one inner room to another, hiding." King Ahab ordered him to be shut up until he himself returned. Micaiah said: "If you return in peace, the Lord has not spoken through me."

When the kings had set out, the king of the Syrians drew up against them, ordering his soldiers to fight no one else but Ahab alone. Ahab changed his royal robe and put on that of a common man. The commanders of the Syrians, seeing Jehoshaphat in royal dress, thought he was Ahab and surrounded him; but when they saw he was not the king of Israel, they withdrew. But one man drew his bow and shot Ahab. Knowing he was mortally wounded, he ordered his charioteer to drive the chariot out of the battle, and about sunset he died. The Syrians, learning that Ahab was dead, went home, and the king's body was brought to Samaria and buried. The chariot, stained with the king's blood, they washed in the spring of Samaria, and the dogs licked the blood and the harlots bathed at the spring, as Elijah had foretold. Ahab died where Micaiah had foretold, not escaping destruction though it had been foretold to him, after reigning twenty-two years. His son Ahaziah received the kingdom after him.

## 2.15

When Jehoshaphat, the king of Jerusalem, returned from his alliance with Ahab, the prophet Jehu reproached him for having fought on the side of an impious man; but he said that he had been rescued by God, though God was displeased at his deed. The king gave thanks. Then, going round the country under him, he commanded them to keep the laws, and appointing judges everywhere he ordered them to judge justly. When the Moabites and Ammonites, taking the Arabs with them, attacked him, he called on God for help. A prophet said that God would fight for him, and bade him lead out the army, but not engage the enemy, and stand and watch the help from God. At dawn the king went out with the army and ordered it to stand there, and the priests to stand before the army with the trumpets, and the Levites and singers to sing a song of thanksgiving to God. The enemy turned against one another and killed one another, so that none was saved. Jehoshaphat gave the enemy's camp to the army to plunder. From then on the foreigners, fearing him as one who had God for his ally, kept quiet.

Ahaziah, the king of the Israelites, was like his parents in wickedness. About the second year of his reign the Moabites held back the tribute they had paid to his father. Falling ill, he sent to the god of Ekron, called the Fly, to ask whether he would recover. Elijah met those who had been sent and told them to turn back and say to the man who had sent to the god of the foreigners, as if Israel had no God, that he would not rise from his bed. The men went back and said what they had heard. Ahaziah asked who it was who had said this. When they said they did not know, he demanded that they describe the man's appearance. When they said he was hairy and wore a leather belt, he understood that it was Elijah. He sent a captain of fifty with his men and ordered the prophet to be brought. The man who was sent found him on the top of the mountain and told him to come to the king. Elijah prayed, and fire flowing from heaven destroyed the captain of fifty and those with him. The king sent another captain of fifty with the fifty; and fire from heaven consumed them likewise. Again a third captain was sent with his men. He came to the prophet, fell down and bowed before him, and begged to be spared his punishment, saying he had come unwillingly, obeying the king's command, and asked him to come to the king. Elijah, since an angel told him to go and not be afraid, came down from the mountain, went to the king and said: "Since you sent to a foreign god to ask whether you would live, know that you will not rise from your sickness, but will die." So Ahaziah died according to the prophet's prediction, leaving the kingdom to his brother Jehoram. He broke the pillars of Baal which Ahab his father had set up, but in other things he was impious and wicked and like his parents.

When Elijah was about to be taken up, he went with Elisha. Coming to the Jordan, he struck the water with his mantle, and the water was divided, and they both crossed on dry ground. Then he said to Elisha: "Ask what I shall do for you." He asked that a double portion of the grace of the Spirit in him might come on him. Elijah said the request was a heavy one, "but if you see me taken up, it shall be yours." While they were still talking, a chariot of fire with horses of fire appeared and snatched Elijah away. As he was carried up, he threw his mantle down to Elisha. Elisha took it, and seeing Elijah no more, went back. Coming to the Jordan, he made trial whether he had received the grace he had asked for, and struck the water with the mantle; but it was not divided. He said, "Where is the God of Elijah?" and struck it again, and the water parted, and he crossed. Fifty of the sons of the prophets, seeing him, said, "The spirit of Elijah rests on Elisha," and followed him. They asked to go and search, in case the Spirit of the Lord had taken Elijah up and thrown him down on one of the mountains or hills. Elisha would not allow it, but when they pressed him he consented. Fifty men went and searched for three days and did not find him.

Elisha was at Jericho. The men of the city came to him begging him to change the water of that place, which was bad and made women barren. He asked for salt to be brought to him in a new vessel, and when it was brought he threw the salt into the spring of the water, calling on God, and the water changed from bitterness to sweetness, and nothing harmful remained in it for them. Once, as the prophet Elisha was going somewhere, little boys mocked him, shouting, "Go up, baldhead." Pained by this, he cursed them. Bears came out of the mountain and killed forty-two of the children.

Jehoram the son of Ahab, about to march against the Moabites, asked Jehoshaphat, the king of Judaea, to fight on his side. He promised, and to take as an ally too the king of the Idumaeans, who was subject to him. The three kings joined and marched through the desert for seven days, and the water gave out for the armies. So the three kings came to Elisha and begged him to save them and the army. He said to Jehoram: "Go to the prophets of your father and your mother," and swore that he would not have answered him except for Jehoshaphat. Then he ordered a harpist to be called. As he played, the Spirit of the Lord inspired the prophet, and he ordered many pits to be dug by the brook: "For you will see," he said, "the brook full, though no wind has blown and no rain has fallen, and you will drink your fill and master your enemies." So he foretold; and at dawn the next day the brook was filled with water, and the flood covered the whole face of that land. When the sun rose fiery and struck the waters, they looked red in the sun's light. So the Moabites, who were camped in the mountains and watching from above, took the water for blood, and thinking that the kings had fought one another and their soldiers had killed one another, rushed in disorder to plunder the spoil. The enemy surrounded them; some were killed and others fled. The Israelites, pursuing, invaded the land of Moab, pulled down the cities and laid the country waste. The king of Moab, going back into the city, led his first-born son up onto the wall and in despair sacrificed him as a burnt offering to God. The kings, seeing this and pitying him in his despair, raised the siege.

Jehoshaphat returned to Jerusalem and died, having lived sixty years and reigned twenty-five of them, a man who was god-fearing and just. He left many sons, and made the eldest successor to the kingdom, called Jehoram, the same name as the king of the Israelites, who was his mother's brother.

## 2.16

While Elisha was staying in Samaria, a widow came to him lamenting that she owed a debt and, having no means to pay it, her sons were being taken by the lender to be his slaves. The prophet asked her what she had in the house. She said she had nothing but a very little oil. He told her to borrow many vessels and pour from the oil into them all. The woman did as she was told, and all were filled with oil; and she told the prophet what had happened. He advised her to sell the oil, pay the debt and use the rest for her living.

He was a guest of the Shunammite woman and rested in her house; she had no son, and he foretold to her that she would conceive and bear a son. According to the prophet's prediction she bore one. When the child had grown, he died; and the woman went to Elisha at Carmel, fell at his feet and lamented her misfortune. He told his disciple Gehazi to go, giving him his own staff to lay on the dead child. The woman swore that she would not go back unless he himself came with her. So the prophet agreed, went and found the boy laid on his bed; and after praying he went up on the bed, lay down with the boy and breathed into him. When the prophet had done so seven times, the dead boy came back to life, and he gave him alive to his mother.

From there he went to Gilgal. There was a famine, and the sons of the prophets came to him. He ordered his servant to prepare a stew for them. The man gathered herbs from the field, with which wild gourd was mixed (the plant is poisonous), stewed them and set them before the men. As they ate they recognized the gourd and cried out to the prophet that they would die. He ordered meal to be thrown into the pot and let them eat, and no one came to any harm from the food. When someone brought him twenty loaves and cakes of figs, he told his servant to set them before the people. When he said, "What would these be among a hundred men?", Elisha said: "Give them, for they will be satisfied and leave some over." And the word came true.

Naaman, a great man with the king of Syria, was a leper. A young Israelite girl had been taken captive, and Naaman's wife had her as a slave. She said to her mistress: "If my master went to the prophet Elisha, he would be rid of his disease." She told her husband the girl's words, and Naaman reported what had been said to the king of Syria. The king wrote a letter to the king of Israel about the healing of Naaman. Naaman, taking ten talents of silver, six thousand gold pieces and ten changes of clothing, went to Samaria and handed the letter to the king. He read it, tore his clothes and said: "The king of Syria is seeking a pretext against us." Elisha, learning this, sent word to the king of Israel: "Let Naaman come to me." When he came, Elisha sent to tell him to go to the Jordan and bathe seven times, and so he would be rid of the leprosy. Naaman was angry that the prophet did not come out to him, pray and lay his hand on him, and in indignation he went off, saying: "Are there not rivers in our country better than the Jordan? I will go and bathe in them and be cleansed." As he went off, his servants said to him: "Do what the prophet says, since it is easy." Naaman went down and dipped himself seven times in the Jordan and was cleansed; and returning he thanked the prophet, confessed that there was no other god but the one in Israel, and asked him to take something of what he had brought; but he refused. And Naaman went back. Gehazi pursued him and said: "The prophet has sent me to take from you a talent of silver and two robes, to give to some needy men who have just come to him." Naaman gave them to him, and Gehazi returned to Elisha. But the prophet knew what Gehazi had done, and said to him: "Since you took the silver and the clothes from Naaman, his leprosy shall cling to you and your seed." And he went out a leper.

The sons of the prophets who were with Elisha went to the Jordan to cut wood to make themselves huts, and Elisha went with them. As they cut the wood, the iron head of one man's axe fell into the river. The owner of the axe called on the prophet and showed him the place where the iron had sunk. He took a piece of wood and threw it in there. The iron came up, and when it floated the man who had lost it took it.

The king of Syria set an ambush to catch King Jehoram. The prophet sent to Jehoram telling him to be on his guard and not go out hunting. He did not go out, and Hadad, having failed in his plot, suspected that his own men were betraying his plans. When they said that it was not they, but the prophet Elisha who revealed his secrets to Jehoram, he sent an army to Dothan to seize him. The prophet's servant, seeing the troops round the city, came to him in terror reporting it. He said, "Do not be afraid," and prayed God to show his servant how he was guarded by Him. And God gave the prophet's servant the grace to see him surrounded by chariots and horses of fire. The prophet, praying, also darkened the eyes of the enemy, and going out to them said: "Follow me, and I will give you Elisha." They followed, harmed both in sight and in mind. He led them to Samaria and told the king to surround them with his force. And he asked God to take the mist from their eyes. When it was taken away, the Syrians saw themselves caught in the midst of their enemies. Elisha allowed no one to attack them, but advised that they rather be entertained and released unharmed. So Jehoram, according to the prophet's word, feasted them generously and let them go. They went off and told their own king what had happened, and he was astonished.

## 2.17

After this the king of Syria came against Samaria with his whole army and besieged it. There was a very severe famine in it, so that an ass's head was sold for fifty pieces of silver, and a sextarius of pigeons' dung for five pieces of the same. As King Jehoram was going round the wall, a woman cried out: "Save me, O king." Thinking the woman was asking for food, he cursed her and said: "How shall I save you, when I have neither threshing floor nor winepress?" When she said she was asking for judgement, he allowed her to speak. The woman said: "I made an agreement with another woman, my neighbour, to sacrifice our children and feed each other a day at a time. I sacrificed mine, but she is breaking what was agreed." Hearing this, the king was overcome with grief and tore his clothes, and he was enraged against the prophet for not praying God to end the affliction, and sent a man to kill him. Elisha, talking with his disciples, told them beforehand: "The son of a murderer has sent to kill me; but shut the door and keep the slayer from entering, for the king will follow, having repented." They did as the prophet said, and Jehoram, changing his mind, came in haste to the prophet and blamed him for not praying God to end their sufferings. He said: "Tomorrow two measures of barley will be sold for a shekel, and a measure of fine flour for a shekel." One of those attending the king said: "You say incredible things, prophet." He said: "You will see what has been promised, but you will not enjoy it, because of your unbelief."

That night God put into the ears of the Syrians the clatter of chariots, horses and arms, and the war-cry of a multitude. Thinking that Jehoram had taken on a great allied force and that it was coming against them, they left their camp and ran away in terror. There were four lepers outside the city, since the law ordered lepers to be put out of the cities. These lepers, now perishing of hunger, decided to go to the camp of the Syrians, saying: "Either they will spare us and we shall eat and live, or we shall be killed and be rid of our miseries." So they came at dawn to the Syrian camp, and no one was to be seen; they went into one tent and found it empty of men; from it they went into another and found it deserted; and when they went into more and met no one, they first ate and drank, then took gold, silver and clothing from there and hid them, and so came into the city and proclaimed what had happened. The king, hearing it, said it was an ambush of the enemy and ordered that no one go out, but sent men to scout. They went off and met no one along the road, but found it full of clothing and equipment. Learning this, the king allowed the multitude to plunder the camp. From this there came such abundance of necessities that two measures of barley were sold for a shekel, and a measure of fine flour for a shekel (the measure is an Italian half-medimnus). Only the king's officer did not enjoy that abundance, as the prophet had said, because he had disbelieved his prediction; for when the multitude flocked to the gate of the city he was trampled and died.

After this the king of Damascus fell ill. Elisha went to Damascus then; and the sick man heard about the prophet and sent Hazael, the most honoured of his attendants, to him to ask whether he would live. He also sent him many gifts. When the prophet was asked, he foretold that the sick king would die, but told Hazael not to say anything to him; and he himself wept. Asked by Hazael the cause of his weeping, he said: "I lament, foreseeing what terrible things will befall the Israelites from you; for you will be king of Syria." Hazael returned to his master and reported that the prophet said he would live. The next day he threw a soaked net over him and smothered him, and seized the kingdom himself.

## 2.18

Jehoram, the king of Judah (for both kings had the same name), was rolled down into impiety by his wife Athaliah, who was the daughter of Ahab. He killed his brothers and his father's attendants, and forced the people to share his impiety. While he was living so, the prophet wrote to him, revealing his coming ruin and the destruction of his wives and children because of his impiety and the affliction of the people. These things came to pass when the Arabs invaded Judaea, and one of his sons, Ahaziah, survived. Afterwards Jehoram fell ill, as the prophet had foretold, and died a violent death, having lived forty years and reigned eight. The rule passed to his son Ahaziah.

The other Jehoram, the king of the Israelites, marching against the Syrians, happened to be shot by an arrow in the battle. He withdrew to have his wound treated, leaving Jehu the son of Nimshi as commander of the army. Elisha sent one of his disciples to anoint Jehu king, since God had chosen him. The disciple came, took Jehu aside from the others as if to tell him something secret, went into the inner room and poured the oil on his head, saying that God chose him as king so that he might wipe out the race of Ahab. As soon as he had said it, he ran quickly out of the inner room. When those with Jehu asked what the man who had called him wanted, likening him to a madman, Jehu said: "You judged rightly, for he told me words full of madness; he said that God appoints me king." Hearing this, the people sounded the trumpet and proclaimed Jehu king.

He at once took the picked men of the cavalry and set out, so as to fall suddenly on Jehoram and his men. When the watchman told Jehoram that horsemen were coming, he sent someone to ask what they wanted. Jehu gave no answer and bade him follow. Then he sent a second; when he too did not come back, Jehoram himself mounted his chariot, with Ahaziah, his sister's son, who had come to visit him, and met Jehu in the field of Naboth, and asked how things stood with the army; but Jehu poured abuse on him. Jehoram turned and fled in fear. Jehu drew his bow and shot him. He fell from the chariot and died, and was thrown into the field of Naboth, according to the prophecy Elijah had made to Ahab. Jehu shot Ahaziah too as he fled, and he died of the wound a little later, having reigned one year.

Jezebel, leaning out from the tower, adorned to make herself more alluring, said: "A good servant, who killed his master!" Jehu ordered the eunuchs to throw her down from above. As she fell, the wall was spattered with her blood, and she died. When Jehu ordered her to be buried because of her royal birth, those who were ordered found nothing of her body but the bones alone; for she had been torn to pieces by dogs, as Elijah had prophesied.

Ahab had seventy sons in Samaria. Jehu wrote to the rulers of Samaria and the guardians of Ahab's sons, bidding them make the best of them king. He wrote this to test the men. They wrote back that they had him as their master. Jehu wrote again that if they acknowledged themselves his slaves, they should bring him the heads of all Ahab's sons. They did what they were ordered. He ordered the heads to be set in two heaps before the gate of the city. When this had been done he came out and said: "I killed the king; but these have perished according to the prophet's prediction." And searching out all Ahab's kinsmen too, Jehu killed them. As he went to Samaria, Jehonadab met him, greeted him and blessed him for having destroyed the race of Ahab.

Then, so that none of the false prophets and priests of Baal might escape, he proclaimed that he was going to offer a lavish sacrifice, and ordered all the priests and prophets of Baal to be present; if anyone was not present, he said, the penalty would be death. When they had gathered, he gave them robes, went with them into the house of Baal and ordered that no one else be mixed in with them. When they said that no one else was present, he set soldiers round outside and ordered them to kill those within. They killed them all and burned the house of Baal and its pillar; but he did not remove the golden heifers. God promised him, for having wiped out the house of Ahab, that the kingdom would last to the fourth generation of his children.

## 2.19

So the race of Ahab was destroyed. But his daughter Athaliah, who had been the wife of Ahaziah, the king of Jerusalem, took control of the kingdom of Judah when he died, and she too set herself the task of destroying the race of David. She killed them all except one. For Ahaziah's sister, called Jehosheba, when her brother's sons were being killed, was able to hide one who was an infant, named Joash, and she brought him up for six years in secret. In the seventh year Jehoiada the high priest, whose wife Jehosheba was, called some of the captains of hundreds, showed them Joash, and conferring with the men persuaded them to rise against Athaliah and recover the boy's ancestral kingdom for him. They confirmed their agreement with oaths, went away and gathered the priests, Levites and heads of tribes before Jehoiada. He showed them Joash, saying: "This is your king, of the seed of David." He surrounded the temple with the people, and setting Joash in the middle he put the diadem on him, poured the oil of anointing over him and declared him king; and the people acclaimed him.

Athaliah, hearing the shout, rushed out of the palace in alarm, with the soldiers following her. When she came to the temple the priests let her in, but the multitude kept those with her from entering. Seeing the boy crowned, she threatened to kill those who had made an attempt on her rule. Jehoiada told the captains of hundreds to drag Athaliah outside and kill her. They did so, and Jehoiada put the king and the people on oath to honour God and keep the laws. They went at once to the temple of Baal, razed it, broke the images of Baal and killed his priest. They installed the king in the palace, being then seven years old. The high priest Jehoiada took great care that faith in God was kept and the laws observed.

When the king came of age he married two wives. Since the temple had been damaged in some parts by Athaliah, Joash was eager to restore it. He called Jehoiada the priest and ordered him to send men to ask from the whole country, for the repair of the temple, half a shekel of silver for each head. Jehoiada, seeing that the people would think this burdensome, went about collecting the money another way. He made a chest closed on every side, cut a hole in the top of it, put it by the temple and asked people to put in through the hole as much as each wished for the restoration of the temple. Since what was given was left to each one's choice, they gave without resentment and vied with one another in paying in money, so that much gold and a very great deal of silver was gathered; from it the temple was repaired and many vessels were made for the sacrifices, which were offered generously to God as long as Jehoiada lived. When he had lived justly and according to the law for a hundred and thirty years and so ended his life, King Joash grew careless about honouring God, and the heads of the tribes and the other leaders of the multitude were corrupted with him; and though prophets warned them about their impiety towards God, they did not stop. When Zechariah, the son of the high priest Jehoiada, prophesied and urged the king and the people to live by the laws, Joash was angry and ordered him to be stoned. As he was being killed, the prophet prayed that God would judge, since he was being robbed of his life by the man who had been so well served by his father. And God judged quickly: Hazael, the king of the Syrians, came and besieged Jerusalem. Joash took all the treasures, both those of God and the royal ones, and all that was in offerings, and gave them to Hazael; and he, pleased with the quantity of money, gave up the siege.

This Syrian, attacking the Israelites, did them great harm. Jehu, the king of the Israelites, who had broken the divine commandments,

## 2.20

died after reigning twenty-seven years, and his son Jehoahaz took the kingdom. In his reign the leader of the Syrians afflicted the country, took many of his cities and put his army to the sword. Meanwhile Jehoahaz prayed to God and found deliverance and a respite from his troubles. He died after reigning seventeen years, having as successor to the kingdom his son Joash, who had the same name as the king of the tribe of Judah. This Joash was not like his father, but good. When Elisha fell ill and was dying, he lamented, calling him father and weapon, since through him he prevailed over his enemies. The prophet comforted him and told him to take a bow and shoot arrows. When the king had shot three times and stopped, Elisha said: "If you had shot more arrows, you would have cut out the rule of the Syrians by the root; but now you will win as many victories over them as the arrows, and recover for your rule the land they took away." Then the prophet died and was given a magnificent burial. Some robbers, having killed a man, threw him into the prophet's tomb; and the man who was thrown in came back to life and stood up.

Hazael, the king of Syria, also died, leaving his son Hadad as successor. Joash, the king of the Israelites, engaged him, defeated him three times and recovered for Israel the country and cities which Hazael, Hadad's father, had taken.

Joash, the king of Judah, who was seven years old when he was proclaimed, died after reigning forty years, killed by his attendants. His son Amaziah took on the kingdom of Jerusalem; he was pious, twenty-five years old when he came to the throne, and he held the kingdom for twenty-nine years. He punished those who had killed his father, but let their children go unharmed, according to the law that says: "Fathers shall not die for sons, nor sons because of fathers." Marching against the Idumaeans and Amalekites, he hired allies from the Israelites. A prophet advised him to leave his allies behind and fight the war with his own men alone. He obeyed and dismissed the Israelites, letting them keep their pay. Engaging the foreigners, he won. But the dismissed allies, in anger, overran the country of the Jews and killed many.

Amaziah, puffed up by his success and impious towards God, sent to Joash, the king of Israel, demanding that he come to him, since he ought to be subject to him as the descendant of David and Solomon. Joash sent back word to him: "The thorn asked the cedar for a marriage alliance for its children. Meanwhile the beasts of the field, passing by, trampled the thorn, and it perished. Let this be example enough for you; and do not be proud because you have had the good fortune to strike down the foreigners, nor drive your people and yourself into danger." Angered at this, Amaziah marched against Joash. Fear fell on his army, and it turned to flight; Amaziah, left alone, was captured by the enemy and brought to Joash. He brought him bound to Jerusalem, pulled down part of the wall, drove through the breach into the city in a chariot, and took the sacred treasures and all that was stored in the palace. Then he released Amaziah and returned to Samaria. He died after reigning sixteen years in all, and his son Jeroboam became king. Amaziah afterwards fell victim to a plot and was killed, having lived fifty-four years and reigned twenty-nine. His son Uzziah was anointed king at the age of sixteen.

## 2.21

Jeroboam the son of Joash was impious and lawless. Yet, such as he was, since God took pity on the Israelite people in their humiliation, he made war on the Syrians and won, the prophet Jonah having foretold the victory to him.

God sent this prophet to the city of Nineveh to proclaim its overthrow and the destruction of the Ninevites. But he did not go, and ran away by boarding a ship. A storm arose and the ship was in danger. When those on board tried by lot to learn who was to blame, the lot fell on Jonah. Questioned who he was, he told his story, and urged them to throw him into the sea; "for in no other way," he said, "will you escape the danger." So he was thrown in, and the storm abated. A sea monster swallowed the prophet and after three days and as many nights spat him out alive and well. He asked God's pardon for his disobedience, and was sent again to proclaim what he had been ordered. He went and proclaimed that Nineveh would be overthrown after three days. The king of the city and all the people believed the prophet, turned to repentance and besought God to have mercy on them. And He, bent by the repentance of the multitude, turned the destruction away from them.

Jeroboam died after reigning forty-one years, and after him his son Zechariah held the rule.

Uzziah, the king of Jerusalem, was pious towards God and just towards his subjects; so he defeated many of the foreigners and made them tributary. But having prospered and grown conceited, he fell away from what was fitting. Robed as a priest, he set about burning incense to God on the golden altar; and when Azariah the high priest and the priests tried to stop him and urged him not to break the law, he threatened them with death unless they kept quiet. Then a kind of light shone out in the temple and struck the king's face, and made leprosy break out on it. The priests told the king of his misfortune and advised him to leave the city.
