He lived so for some time, and died of despondency, having reigned fifty-two years and lived sixty-eight in all, handing the rule to his son Jotham.

Zechariah the son of Jeroboam died after six months as king of the Israelites, having lived impiously, murdered by Shallum. Shallum, after reigning thirty days, was killed by Menahem, who reigned over Israel in his place. When Pul, the king of the Assyrians, made war on him, he gave him a thousand talents of silver, which he collected from the people, and ended the war. Menahem, after reigning ten years, impious and harsh, died, passing the kingdom to his son Pekahiah. He too was impious and cruel, and was murdered by Pekah the son of Remaliah, who reigned in place of the murdered man, and he too held the rule impiously and lawlessly. At that time Tiglath-pileser, the king of the Assyrians, invaded the country of the Israelites, overthrew many cities and carried off to his own country a host of captives not easy to count.

Jotham the son of Uzziah, king of Jerusalem, was pious and just; he cared for the city and the temple, and made the Ammonites tributary after defeating them in battle. Nahum, who was prophesying then, foretold the overthrow of Nineveh; and his prediction came true when a hundred and fifteen years had passed. Jotham's life failed him when he had lived forty-one years and reigned sixteen.

## 2.22

The rule of Jerusalem passed to his son, who was impious towards God and broke His laws; for he bowed down to idols, set up an altar to them in Jerusalem and offered his own son to them as a burnt offering. Against him Rezin, the ruler of the Syrians, and Pekah, the king of the Israelites, made common cause and besieged him for a long time in Jerusalem. Rezin, having taken other cities, killed their inhabitants, settled Syrians in them and returned to Damascus. Ahaz, when Pekah alone was left, engaged him and was defeated, losing a hundred and twenty thousand of his people and many men of note, and his son. Pekah went back to Samaria, he and his army, carrying a great multitude of captives away to their own country. But a prophet met them before the walls of Samaria, rebuked them for taking their kinsmen captive and advised them to set them free; and they were persuaded and released the captives.

Ahaz asked the king of the Assyrians to help him against the Syrians and the Israelites. He came, ravaged the country of the Syrians, took Damascus, killed Rezin, overran the country of the Israelites and carried off very many captives. In return for this Ahaz gave Tiglath-pileser the royal treasures and all that was stored in the temple. He went so far in impiety that he not only worshipped the gods of the foreigners, but also shut up the temple and plundered its offerings. Having lived so, he died at thirty-six, after reigning sixteen years, leaving the kingdom to his son Hezekiah. It happened that the king of the Israelites also died then, through the plot of Hoshea. He too was wicked; so when Shalmaneser, the king of the Assyrians, marched against him, he was made his slave and ordered to pay tribute every year.

Hezekiah, being pious and just, called an assembly of the priests and Levites, ordered them to open the temple and sacrifice as was their law, and bade the people put nothing before the honour of God. He also sent to the whole country, calling everyone to keep the feast of unleavened bread, which had not been celebrated for a long time because of the lawlessness of those in power. He urged the Israelites too to worship the God of their fathers according to the ancient custom. But they would not be persuaded, and even murdered the prophets who urged the same; though some, persuaded, turned to piety and flocked to Jerusalem to worship God. King Hezekiah went with the leaders of the people to the temple, offered lavish sacrifices through the priests, gave the people many animals for feasting, and ordered the priests to go out into the country and purify it. Then he made war on the foreigners and took cities from them.

Hoshea, the king of the Israelites, called on So, the king of Egypt, to ally with him against the Assyrians. Learning this, Shalmaneser was angry; he besieged Samaria for three years, took the city and wiped out the kingdom, and carried the people captive into his own country, with Hoshea, the king of the Israelites, himself. Moving other nations out of his own country, from a place called Cuthah, he settled them in Samaria and its territory. So the ten tribes of the Israelites were carried away nine hundred and forty-seven years after they left Egypt, and two hundred and forty years and seven months after they abandoned Rehoboam and chose Jeroboam as their king.

Those who were moved into Samaria, called Cuthaeans from the place in Persia where they had lived before, being five nations, again worshipped their own gods. As the Fourth Book of Kingdoms says, God roused lions and destroyed many of them; as Josephus records, a plague fell on them. Knowing that the evils came on them through God's anger, they begged their king to send them priests from among the Israelites, to initiate them in the worship of the Hebrews; and when this was done the destruction stopped. They are called Cuthaeans by the Hebrews, and Samaritans by the Greeks.

## 2.23

In the fourteenth year of the reign of Hezekiah, king of Jerusalem, Sennacherib, the reigning king of the Assyrians, came against Judaea and took all the cities of the two tribes. Hezekiah, afraid for Jerusalem, sent to him promising to pay whatever tribute he imposed. Sennacherib asked for three hundred talents of silver and thirty of gold, and gave sworn pledges that he would withdraw if he received them. Having received them, he set off himself against the Egyptians and Ethiopians, and left his commander-in-chief Rabshakeh with two other generals and a heavy force to sack Jerusalem. They threw up a palisade before the walls and asked to speak with Hezekiah. He was afraid to go out, but sent three men of rank under him. Rabshakeh said to them: "If Hezekiah trusts in the Egyptians, he is a fool, like a man leaning on a staff of reed, and a cracked one, which gave no help to the man holding it, but he fell and the reed pierced his hand. Such is Pharaoh the Egyptian." Rabshakeh said this in Hebrew to those who had been sent. They asked him to speak to them in the Syrian language, so that the multitude might not understand what was said. But he raised his voice the more and said again in Hebrew, in the hearing of all: "Do not let Hezekiah deceive you, saying, 'The Lord will rescue us.' Did He rescue Samaria?"

They reported this to Hezekiah. Hearing it, he tore his clothes and put on sackcloth, and falling down he prayed to God, asking for help in a hopeless plight; and he sent to Isaiah the prophet and begged him to pray to God to be merciful to the people. Isaiah foretold that the enemy would be defeated without a battle, and that Sennacherib would come back from Egypt having achieved nothing and be killed. Sennacherib also wrote to Hezekiah: "Do not let your God, in whom you trust, encourage you that Jerusalem will not be delivered into my hands. Consider where are the kings of the nations whom my fathers destroyed." Hezekiah read this letter, laid it in the temple and besought God, saying: "See, Lord, and hear the words of Sennacherib, which he sent to taunt you, the living God, and save us from his hand." Isaiah encouraged the king, saying that God had heard him. That night an angel killed a hundred and eighty-five thousand in the camp of the Assyrians, and at daybreak their bodies were found dead. What was left of the army departed from Jerusalem.

Sennacherib, besieging Pelusium, raised the siege when in a single night mice ate the bows of the Assyrians and the rest of their arms. This is not recorded in the book of Kingdoms, but Josephus tells it, having found it in Herodotus. He says that Berossus, who wrote the history of Chaldaea, states that when Sennacherib returned from the war with the Egyptians he found the army with Rabshakeh at Jerusalem; and when God struck the Assyrians with a pestilence, in one night a hundred and eighty-five thousand perished, with their commanders and captains. Afraid for the rest of the army, he left there with the force that remained and went off to Nineveh. Shortly after, he fell victim to a plot by his elder sons and was killed; his murderers fled, and his son Esarhaddon succeeded to the rule.

Hezekiah, freed from fear, gave thanks to God. Shortly after, when he fell ill, Isaiah came to him at God's command and said: "Set your house in order, for you will not live." The expectation of death, and that, having no children, he would leave the kingdom without a lawful heir, made the misfortune greater for him; and despondent at this he wept, asking God to grant him more life. Isaiah came again and brought him the good news of health after the third day and an addition of fifteen years of life. Considering how extraordinary the wonder was, he asked the prophet to show him a sign as pledge of what had been promised. When the prophet prayed God that a sign be given, the sun, which had already declined ten steps and cast a shadow, turned back ten steps. From then on Hezekiah was rid of the disease.

Baladan, the king of the Babylonians, sent an embassy to Hezekiah with gifts, asking to have him as ally and friend. He showed the envoys his treasures, his arms and all the royal splendour, sent gifts back to the Babylonian and dismissed them. Isaiah said to Hezekiah: "Days will come, and the wealth you have put on show will be carried off to Babylon, and your sons will be eunuchs to the king of Babylon." Hezekiah, since the word of God could not fail, said: "Let there be peace in my days." He lived out the time added to him peacefully and died, having reached his fifty-fourth year and reigned twenty-nine.

His son Manasseh, succeeding to the rule, at first showed every kind of wickedness and impiety, defiling the temple and not even sparing the prophets. God, angered at this, moved the army of the Babylonians against Judaea, which plundered the country and captured Manasseh. He, understanding that he himself was the cause of his misfortune, turned to repentance and begged God to release him from his troubles. And God heard him, freed him from captivity and restored him to the kingdom. When he returned he pursued piety himself and persuaded the people to pursue it. Having lived so, he became admired for his piety, and died after reigning fifty-five years and living sixty-seven in all.

## 2.24

The kingdom passed to his son Amon, who imitated his father's youth. He died the victim of a plot, having lived twenty-four years and reigned two. The multitude punished his murderers and declared his son Josiah king, then in his eighth year; he was pious and persuaded the people to be pious. Wishing to repair the temple, he urged his subjects to give for the repair as much as each wished. With the money collected in this way the temple was restored, and he used what was left over for mixing-bowls, libation cups and bowls, under the supervision of the high priest Hilkiah. While bringing the silver out of the temple treasury, Hilkiah came upon the sacred books of Moses, which were stored there, and sent them to the king by the scribe. When they were read to him, he tore his clothes and sent Hilkiah the high priest and others to the prophetess Huldah, the wife of Shallum, asking her to appease God. She said that God had already passed sentence against the country and the people for their impiety, but because the king was just He would not bring the evils now; when he had passed away, what had been decreed would happen. Hearing this, he gathered the people at Jerusalem and had the sacred books read in the hearing of all. Then he required oaths from all to keep the laws and worship God. And the people, sacrificing, appeased the Lord.

He also burned the offerings to the idols, put their priests to death, and burned the bones of the false prophets on the altar which Jeroboam had set up, according to the prediction of the prophet who came to Jeroboam as he sacrificed and foretold that a descendant of David, Josiah, would do these things three hundred and sixty-one years later. Josiah also went to the Israelites who had escaped the captivity and urged them to worship the God of their fathers and live by the laws. Having wiped out the idols from the whole country, he gathered the multitude at Jerusalem and kept the feast of unleavened bread, which they call Pascha. He led a peaceful life, but at the end he was shot by an arrow and died. For Necho, the king of the Egyptians, was going off to make war on the Medes, and Josiah tried to stop him passing through his country. Necho told him that he was not marching against him; but Josiah would not give way. So they were drawn up to fight, and the Egyptian shot Josiah as he was marshalling his own people, and so ended the war. The king went back to Jerusalem and died, having reigned thirty-one years and lived eight before his reign.

In his time Jeremiah was the prophet, who foretold the terrible things that would befall Jerusalem and its capture by the Romans. And not he alone wrote about these things beforehand: Ezekiel too was the first to leave two books about them. Both were of the priestly tribe.

When Josiah died, his son Jehoahaz ruled, at twenty-three years old, an impious man. The king of the Egyptians, returning from the war, put him in chains and gave the kingdom to his elder brother Eliakim, renaming him Jehoiakim, and imposed on the country a tribute of a hundred talents of silver and one of gold. Jehoahaz he took away to Egypt, where he died. The length of his reign was three months and ten days.

Jehoiakim, who was impious and wicked, was already in the fourth year of his reign when Nebuchadnezzar took on the rule of the Babylonians. He defeated Necho the Egyptian and took away a great part of his country. In the eighth year of Jehoiakim's reign he invaded Judaea with a heavy force. Jehoiakim, afraid, made his country tributary to the Babylonian. For three years he brought the tribute; then, hearing that the Egyptians were marching against the Babylonian, he held back the payment of tribute, although the prophet Jeremiah foretold the capture of the city by the Babylonian and the death of King Jehoiakim, and not only said it but also wrote it and read the writing in the hearing of the multitude. The leaders took it and brought it to the king; and he, enraged at what was written, burned the writing in the fire, and sought to punish the prophet and his scribe Baruch. They fled and avoided his anger. Not long after, the Babylonian marched against him, and Jehoiakim received him into the city without resistance. But he killed the better citizens and the king himself, carried off three thousand of the finest as captives, among whom was the prophet Ezekiel, then a boy, and declared the son of the murdered king, also named Jehoiakim, king of Judaea. So Jehoiakim met his end, having lived thirty-six years and reigned eleven.

The Babylonian, suspecting that his son would bear a grudge for the murder of his father and revolt, sent and besieged him. He, not wishing the city to be endangered because of him, gave himself and his kin up to the enemy on terms that they would do no harm to the city or to them. The Babylonian broke these terms, ordering all the youth in the city and the craftsmen to be brought to him, who numbered ten thousand eight hundred and thirty-two. Jehoiakim, his mother and his friends he shut up and kept under guard, after he had reigned three months

## 2.25

and ten days. He handed over the kingdom of Jerusalem to Zedekiah, Jehoiakim's uncle, who swore that he would not favour the Egyptians, but would keep the country for him. Zedekiah was young, for he was twenty-one when he received the rule, and he despised what was just and fitting; he did not heed Jeremiah when he urged him to give up his impieties and lawlessness, nor believe him when he told of the calamities that would come on him and on the people, which Ezekiel too foretold in Babylon and wrote and sent to Jerusalem. Zedekiah disbelieved both prophets because they seemed to disagree. On other matters they agreed, but about Zedekiah himself they seemed to contradict each other: Ezekiel prophesied that he would not see Babylon, while Jeremiah foretold that he would be led in chains to Babylon. But what was foretold about him agreed with both prophets; for he was blinded in Judaea and led away in chains, and did not see Babylon.

This Zedekiah kept faith with the Babylonian for eight years, and then turned to the Egyptians. So Nebuchadnezzar marched out and besieged Jerusalem. When the Egyptians came to help the Jews, the Babylonian went out to meet them, routed them and pursued them. The false prophets said that the enemy would no longer besiege them; but Jeremiah foretold that they would come back, take the city, raze it, burn the temple, and carry off as captives of war the people who were left by then, to serve for seventy years until the Persians and Medes put an end to the rule of the Babylonians; and then they would be freed from captivity and rebuild the temple and the city. Jeremiah said this; but the king and those about him mocked his words. As the prophet was going to his native place, Anathoth, one of the officials met him, seized him as a deserter and brought him to the rulers; and they mistreated him and kept him for punishment.

In the ninth year of Zedekiah's reign the Babylonian came against Jerusalem the second time, and for a year and a half besieged the city hard. Famine came with the siege, and after it pestilence. Jeremiah, though imprisoned, urged the multitude to receive the Babylonian, for so they would be saved; if not, they would stay and perish by famine or the sword; "but whoever goes over to the enemy," he said, "will escape death." Yet no one thought the prophet trustworthy, and the leaders even stirred up the king against him. He, courting them because of the crisis, let them deal with the prophet as they wished. And they threw him into a muddy pit. Zedekiah, learning this, ordered him to be pulled out of there, called him secretly and asked what he said about the present dangers. He advised him to hand over the city to the Babylonian. Zedekiah said he was afraid he would be killed. The prophet insisted that if he did so he would meet with nothing hard. So the king let him go; but the Babylonian pressed the siege of the city with violent assaults and took it in the eleventh year of Zedekiah's rule.

Zedekiah fled from the city with his wives, children, kin and friends. The enemy pursued him and caught him alone, for his leaders and friends had scattered. They took him alive with his children and wives and brought them to the Babylonian. He reproached him with ingratitude and faithlessness, ordered his sons and his friends to be killed before his eyes, then blinded him and took him in chains to Babylon. Then he understood that both prophets had foretold the truth.

This was the end of those of the seed of David who had reigned. They were twenty-one in all, and five hundred and fourteen years, six months and sixteen days had passed since Saul came to the throne. Nebuzaradan, the chief cook of the Babylonian, was sent and burned the temple and the palace, razed the city and plundered everything in the temple. The temple was burned four hundred and seventy years, six months and ten days from the time it was built; one thousand and sixty-two years, six months and ten days after the departure of the Hebrews from Egypt; one thousand nine hundred and fifty-seven years, six months and ten days after the flood; and from the bringing forth of the first man until then three thousand five hundred and thirteen years, six months and ten days had passed.

## 3.1

Nebuzaradan, having razed Jerusalem, took the people, the high priest Seraiah and the sacred vessels to Babylon. Nebuchadnezzar dedicated the vessels of God to his own gods and settled the captives in his own country. Nebuzaradan left in Judaea those who had deserted to the Babylonians during the siege, and the poor, and set over them as governor Gedaliah, a man of good family, fair and just; he told them to work the land and ordered them to pay tribute to Nebuchadnezzar. He urged the prophet Jeremiah to go to Babylon; but he wished to live among the ruins of his country. So he left him there, ordering Gedaliah to take every care of him. He also released to him his disciple Baruch, who was exceptionally learned in his native tongue. All who had escaped from Jerusalem because of the siege came to Gedaliah. He advised them to farm the land, to live in whatever city each wished, and to rebuild the ruins. Knowing Gedaliah to be a good man, they loved him dearly.

Among the others there returned a man called Ishmael, of the royal family, who had fled to the Ammonites at the time of the siege. When he came to Gedaliah, some told him that Ishmael was plotting against him, and that if he would let them kill the man he would escape the plot. But he did not believe it, and said he thought it better to die himself than to kill a man who had trusted himself to him. Some time later this Ishmael came to Gedaliah with others, and when he had been entertained he killed Gedaliah and his fellow guests. Then, falling by night on the Babylonians left in the city, he killed them and all the Jews there. The next day, when eighty men came from the country to Gedaliah with gifts, Ishmael killed them too, except a few, who promised to show him furniture, clothing and grain hidden in the fields. Taking many captives as well, he went off to the Ammonites. Some leaders of the Jews, learning this and rousing those with them, pursued him; they recovered the captives, but Ishmael escaped with eight men.

The survivors, afraid of the Babylonians, planned to go to Egypt. Jeremiah advised them to stay, saying that if they stayed God would watch over them so that they suffered no harm, but if they left He would not be with them; but they did not believe him, and disobeying they departed for Egypt, taking Jeremiah and Baruch with them. When the prophet had gone there, God revealed the future to him. He foretold to his countrymen that the Babylonian would march against the Egyptians and master Egypt, and would kill some of them and carry off others as captives. For this he was stoned to death by his fellow tribesmen. But what he foretold happened: in the fifth year after the sack of Jerusalem Nebuchadnezzar set out against Coele Syria, and having taken it he attacked the Ammonites and Moabites, and from there marched on Egypt; he subdued it, killed its king and set up another, and carried the Jews there off to Babylon. Jeremiah was buried in Egypt, and it is said that King Alexander, when he became master of Egypt among other lands, and the Egyptians were singing the prophet's praises, moved his bones to the city he had founded, Alexandria.

So the prophet foretold, as I have said, what would happen; and the race of the Hebrews met this end, carried beyond the Euphrates. For the ten tribes were taken captive by the Assyrians from Samaria in the reign of Hoshea, and the two tribes were taken captive afterwards by Nebuchadnezzar, with all who were left after the capture of Jerusalem. Shalmaneser moved the nation of the Cuthaeans from Persia to Samaria, but Nebuchadnezzar left Judaea empty, and it remained so for seventy years. The time between the captivity of the ten tribes from Samaria and that of the two from Jerusalem was a hundred and thirty years, six months and ten days.

## 3.2

The Babylonian made eunuchs of the noblest boys among the captives, those related to King Zedekiah and those who surpassed the rest in vigour and beauty, handed them to tutors, and had them taught the letters of the Chaldaeans and the learning of the country, assigning them their food from his own table. Four of the boys were of the royal family, handsome in looks and good in nature. The Babylonian renamed the one called Daniel Belteshazzar, the one named Hananiah Shadrach, the other, called Mishael, Meshach, and the last, Azariah, Abednego. He held them in more honour than the rest, since through their gifts they were advancing in wisdom. These young men begged the eunuch in charge of them not to feed them on what was supplied from the royal table, but on pulses, dates and other such things. He said he was afraid that if they were fed on such things their bodies would grow thin, and that he himself would be punished by the king for it. They begged him to feed them so for ten days; if the condition of their bodies did not change, he should feed them so from then on, but if he saw their bodies growing thin, he should go back to the former diet. The man in charge of the boys agreed, and when he did so and saw that they suffered nothing from such food, but rather became more flourishing than the others, he gave them food without meat. With their minds made keener, they mastered the whole of Chaldaean and Assyrian learning. Daniel also applied himself to the interpretation of dreams.

Then Nebuchadnezzar saw an extraordinary dream, and learning its interpretation in his sleep, he forgot both the dream and its interpretation. So at dawn he sent for the Chaldaeans, the seers and the magi, and demanded that they tell him the dream and its meaning. They wanted him first to tell the dream, and then ask them what it signified. The king was angry at this, and condemned them all to death when they said they could not find the dream. As wise men, Daniel and the three boys were summoned and condemned with them. Daniel went to the commander of the royal bodyguard and asked that the execution of the condemned be put off for one night; "for perhaps," he said, "during it we shall pray to God and come to know the dream." The king granted the request. Daniel, taking with him the three boys, his kinsmen, besought God through the night. Both the dream and what it signified were revealed to him by God. At daybreak he went to the king and told him that the dream and its meaning had been made known to him by God, who had pitied those in danger of dying unjustly for it, "and no less you, who wished to kill so many without cause because you demanded impossible things, which are known to the divine nature alone. What you saw is this. You seemed to see a great statue standing, whose head was of gold, its shoulders and arms of silver, its belly and thighs of bronze, and its legs and feet partly of iron and partly of clay. Then a stone broken off from a mountain seemed to fall on the statue and shatter it, and to grind the gold, the silver, the bronze, the iron and the clay so fine that they were winnowed and scattered by the wind. The stone seemed to grow and reach such a size that the whole earth was filled by it. That is your dream. And what is revealed to you by it, as you ponder about the kingdom and who will rule after you, is this. The golden head is you and the other kings of the Babylonians, and the gold shows the great wealth of your rule. The two hands and the shoulders image two kingdoms by which your rule will be brought to an end. The bronze belly and thighs show a king clothed in bronze, who will overthrow those who end your rule. And his kingdom in turn another, more powerful, will bring to an end; for iron is more powerful than gold, silver and bronze."

For gold, especially the purest, is softer than silver; silver is denser than gold, but looser than bronze; and iron again is harder than bronze. The kingdom of the Assyrians, then, is likened to the head, as earlier than the others and first in time. As second he named that of the Medes and Persians, who destroyed the rule of the Assyrians. For Cyrus, as will be told, was a Persian on his father's side, but related to the Medes through his mother; he took both nations with him, made war on the Assyrians and ended their kingdom. The pair of these two nations is imaged by the pair of hands. The third kingdom he calls that of the Macedonians. He likened it to bronze as being more powerful, since it ended the rule of the Persians; and by the belly the sole rule of Alexander is signified, and by the thighs the division of the kingdom after his death and its partition. The fourth kingdom he called that of the Romans, to which he assigned iron because of its hardness. He imaged it by the legs because it came later in time: for the kingdom of the Romans is later than the other great kingdoms, and the legs and feet are the last parts of the body. That clay is mixed with the iron hints that the kingdom is strong, yet has in itself something brittle and weak; for such is the nature of clay, being made of mud.

## 3.3

If one wished to refer the vision to the earlier condition of the Roman government, when the senate, the dictators, the consuls, the tribunes and the people shared in managing public affairs, he will find the people often at odds with the senate and seditions arising, the senate ordering some things and the multitude bringing in others against them, so that from this they fought one another, and their affairs, through the discord, at times tipped towards the worse and weaker. One might image the senate by iron, for the firmness of its judgement; and the multitude, mixed with those in office and with the council, might be likened to clay, both for its rabble-like and lowly nature (for such is the mud from which clay is made) and for its changeability and weakness of mind. For the multitude is apt to be unstable and easily led and to change its mind; and anyone who reads the ancient writings about the Roman state will find this happening to it very often.

If, then, one refers the difference of the materials and their mixing to the earlier condition of the Roman state, he might perhaps reckon so. But if to the later one, when the affairs of the Romans passed from aristocracy to monarchy, and to the condition of the empire that followed, he would find then too that the discord of the Romans with one another did the state very great harm. For Caesar, reaching for sole rule, brought about civil wars against Pompey the Great and the others who laid claim to the old liberty; Caesar Augustus and Antony in turn fought similar battles against those who killed him; and then these two, quarrelling with each other over sole power, stirred up heavy wars, men of the same race perishing on both sides in all the battles, so that the Roman power was diminished. Nor would one see the seditions abate in the times that followed. And if one compares the nature of iron and clay with the later reigns, he will find the rule of the Romans strong in one way and weak in another, having lost the greater part of its ancient dominion, and now confined to a small fraction of that ancient empire.

So much for the four kingdoms. The remaining one, which cannot be destroyed, imaged by the stone which was cut without hands, ground fine the materials of which the image was made and filled the whole earth, which the prophet said would not be destroyed for ever, would be the future appearing of our Lord Jesus Christ. It will appear when the iron kingdom has become weaker through the admixture of clay; then all things will be subjected to Him, and He will grant an endless and incorruptible kingdom to those who have made themselves worthy of it. For Scripture is accustomed to call Christ a stone. David said: "The stone which the builders rejected, this has become the head of the corner"; and Isaiah says in the person of God: "See, I lay in Zion a cornerstone." The great Paul too calls Christ the cornerstone, on which he wrote that the believers are built up; and again he says: "No one can lay another foundation than the one that is laid, which is Jesus Christ"; and again elsewhere: "They drank from the spiritual rock that followed them, and the rock was Christ." He is also called a rock of offence and a stone of stumbling, and in many places the Lord may be found called a stone and a rock. That it was cut without hands foretold His birth beyond nature, without seed and without marital union. For the mountain may be taken as the tribe of Judah, from which David came; the stone is Christ, cut from it in His human nature; and without hands, because He came forth not by the laws of nature but from a holy womb that knew no man. He seemed small at first because of the nature of His humanity, but as He went on He filled the whole inhabited world with His glory, and will fill it yet more at His future appearing.

So the prophet told Nebuchadnezzar the dream and revealed its interpretation. Amazed, he fell on his face, bowed down before Daniel and ordered sacrifice to be made to him, that arrogant and haughty man not giving all the honour to him, but to the God whom he worshipped. For he said: "In truth this God of yours is God of gods and Lord of kings, and a revealer of mysteries." He gave him many gifts, made him ruler of all Babylon, and named him Belteshazzar, giving him the name of his own god.

## 3.4

Then he set up a golden statue, sixty cubits high and six broad, and called together the leading men from all his realm, ordering that when they heard the trumpet they should fall down and worship the statue, and that those who disobeyed should be thrown into the furnace of fire, which he lit there, great and fierce. While all were worshipping, the three young men whom the history has recorded as Daniel's kinsmen, unwilling to break their ancestral laws, did not worship. They were thrown at once into the furnace; but the fire did not touch them, and standing in the midst of that irresistible flame they sent up a hymn to God. With those three stood a fourth: it was an angel who had come down with them and was turning that fiery air into a dewy breeze. This astonished the beastlike and stubborn soul of that king; putting aside his conceit, he came forward, called the young men by name and blessed their God, held those men worthy of honour and made them leaders of all the Jews in Babylon.

A little later that Assyrian saw another dream. He saw a tree, great and reaching to the ends of the earth, beautiful with leaves and laden with fruit. Under it the wild beasts made their dwelling, and the birds lived in its branches. Then he saw a Watcher come from heaven and call out with strength: "Cut down the tree, strip off its branches, shake off its leaves and scatter its fruit; but leave the stock of its roots. With a band of iron, in the grass of the earth and in the dew of heaven he shall lodge, and his portion shall be with the beasts; his heart shall be changed from that of men, the heart of a beast shall be given him, and seven times shall pass over him." Seeing this vision, he again called together the magi and the wise men of Babylon and sought to learn what was signified by it. They were at a loss and could not tell the meaning of the dream. Then again he had recourse to Daniel, told him the dream and asked him to give its interpretation; "for you can," he said, "since the holy Spirit of God is in you." He said: "My lord the king, may this dream be for those who hate you." Then he explained its meaning to him, saying: "The great tree which you saw is you, O king, for you have become great and strong, and your greatness has reached to heaven and your lordship to the ends of the earth." By greatness he meant the exaltation of his mind and the arrogance of his spirit. "And since you saw a holy Watcher coming down from heaven," that is, a divine angel (for *ir*, put into Greek, means "the one who is awake", by which the bodiless is hinted at; for one who wears a body is enslaved to sleep, but one not enslaved to it has a bodiless nature), "since, then," he said, "you saw that Watcher call out that the tree be cut down but the stock of its roots be left, and the rest, expect to fall from the kingdom and be driven out of the company of men, to live with the beasts and eat grass; yet not to be wholly deprived of the kingdom, but to have it restored to you again; for this is what the leaving of the stock of the tree's roots signifies."

Such was the interpretation of the vision that Daniel gave Nebuchadnezzar. And everything happened according to the divine oracle: the king fell sick with madness because of his boastfulness and arrogance and spent seven years in the desert; and since no one laid hands on his kingdom, he recovered it after appeasing God. He died after reigning forty-three years. Josephus says that many of the ancient historians mention him, Berossus, Megasthenes, Diocles and Philostratus.

When he died, his elder son Evil-merodach succeeded to the rule. He at once released from his chains King Jehoiakim, also called Jeconiah, whom Nebuchadnezzar had kept in prison, held him worthy of honour, gave him gifts, and had him at his table always.

## 3.5

When he too had passed away, the kingdom went to his brother, called Belshazzar, against whom Cyrus marched with the Persians and Medes. This man gave a drinking party for his nobles, and as he lay at table with his concubines and was getting drunk, he ordered the sacred vessels to be brought which Nebuchadnezzar had carried off from Jerusalem and dedicated to his own gods. When they were brought, he used them, though his father had not, and as he drank he blasphemed against God. Then he saw the knuckles of a human hand coming out of the wall and writing on the plaster of the wall; he was beside himself and his looks changed. He called the Chaldaeans and the magi and demanded the meaning of the letters, and proclaimed that he would give great rewards to whoever made their sense clear; but what was written was unknown to all. When the king was despondent and troubled, his grandmother said: "If Belteshazzar the Jew is sent for, who explained many things to Nebuchadnezzar that no one else could tell, he will reveal to you the secret of these syllables; for the holy Spirit of God is in him." So Belshazzar the ruler at once called Daniel and asked him to explain the letters to him, and not to hold back even if what they signified was gloomy; and he promised to give him as a reward to wear purple and a gold collar, and the third part of the rule.

Daniel declined the gifts, but said the writing foretold to him the end of his life, because he had not been taught restraint even by what his father had suffered for impiety, but had used the vessels of God for service with his concubines. He read the letters thus: "Mene, tekel, peres"; and interpreting their meaning he said: "God has numbered the time of your life and your rule, and it has come to be very short." For the word, put into Greek, would mean number. So he interpreted *mene*; and *tekel*, he said, meant weight: "God, having weighed the limit of your kingdom, shows that it is sinking and broken"; and *tekel* means a fragment in the Greek language. *Peres* the prophet interpreted as division, for that is what the word means when put into Greek speech, and he said that his kingdom had been divided by God between the Persians and Medes. So Daniel read what was written on the wall and gave such an interpretation of those syllables, that is, an explanation; and grief seized the king. Soon after, the prophet's prediction came true, when Cyrus, king of the Persians, marched against him; Babylon was taken by him and Belshazzar was killed in the capture. Some say that on that night on which he saw the knuckles writing, the city was taken and he was killed.

After the capture of Babylon the prophet Daniel was taken to Media by Darius the Mede (who was also named Cyaxares and was Cyrus's uncle on his mother's side, being the son of Astyages, who had reigned over the Medes) and was held worthy of every honour. In this position he was envied by the leading men among the Medes, and they contrived a means by which they hoped to destroy him. Seeing him pray to God three times a day, they came to Cyaxares, also called Darius, and asked him to make a law that for thirty days no one should pray to the gods or make requests of the king himself, and that whoever did not keep the law should be thrown into the pit of lions. He, not knowing what they were devising against Daniel, put out an edict ordering what has been said. The others kept quiet according to the decree, but Daniel prayed to God as was his custom. The satraps denounced him to the king and demanded that by the law he be thrown into the pit of lions. When the prophet had been thrown in there, the king sealed the stone over the mouth and withdrew. In anguish and pain he passed the night without sleep; for he hoped that Daniel would not be harmed by the beasts, knowing that the holy Spirit of God was in him, yet he was afraid and anxious that some harm might befall him, being a barbarian and not fully knowing the power of God. As soon as day dawned he stood at the pit and called Daniel, and he answered. The king at once ordered the prophet to be pulled up, and was astonished to see him untouched and safe. When those who had denounced him said that the lions were full of food and so had not touched Daniel, the king, hating them for their wickedness and understanding their scheme and trick against the prophet, ordered abundant food to be set before the beasts, and when they were full ordered the prophet's accusers to be thrown into the beasts' pit, saying: "So that we may know whether the lions will not touch them because they are full." When those wicked men were torn to pieces at once, it became clear to all that a divine power had preserved Daniel by muzzling the beasts. So the king magnified the prophet's God, and held him in still greater honour.

## 3.6

These things happened later; but in the first year of the reign of Belshazzar the prophet saw a dream. The vision was this. Four beasts seemed to come up out of the sea. The first had the form of a lioness and wings like an eagle's; it was lifted up from the earth and made to stand on the feet of a man, and the heart of a man was given to it. The sea signified human life, with its many waves, undergoing very many and continual changes and reversals; the beast, the lioness, imaged the kingdom of the Assyrians; and its being lifted from the earth showed the ending of that rule and the taking away of its power. Its standing on the feet of a man hinted that it would become equal to its subjects; and the heart of a man being given to it, that it would be taught by experience to think human thoughts and not imagine anything beyond human nature out of arrogance; for the wings which grew on the beast against nature showed the pride of those kings and the way their minds soared above the clouds in haughtiness.

The second beast was like a bear, and it stood on one side, and it had three ribs and wings in its mouth; and it was told: "Arise, eat much flesh." The bear was a figure of the kingdom of the Persians, because of the cruelty and harshness of the nation; for of all the barbarians the Persians are the harshest in punishing, inflicting the most bitter and longest punishments by the boats and by flaying.

Some probably do not know the punishment of the boats; so it is well to make it clear to those who do not know it. They fit two equal boats together, cutting them so that the head of the man being punished is left outside the boats, and his hands, and his feet too; they lay the victim on his back inside, and so nail the boats to each other. Then they mix honey and milk and pour it into the mouth of that wretched man until he is more than sated; and they pour some of the mixture over his face, feet and hands, and setting him in the open they let him bake in the sun, and do this to him every day. Flies, wasps and bees, gathering because of the sweetness of what has been poured on, settle on his face and the other limbs which, as has been said, are left outside the boats, some tormenting the wretch and some even stinging him. His belly, filled with the honey and milk, lets out liquid excrement, and as it rots maggots and worms swarm up; and he, his flesh rotting in the wet and himself consumed by the worms, so dies more pitifully and slowly. Parysatis, the mother of Cyrus and Artaxerxes, is recorded to have used this punishment against the man who boasted of having killed Cyrus as he fought his brother for the kingdom; he lasted under the punishment of the boats until the fourteenth day. Such, then, the punishment of the boats is recorded to be.

"Arise, eat flesh" is not a permission but a prediction of what would be; for there was a great slaughter of the Assyrians and their allies by the Persians and Medes. One might also say that "eat much flesh" signifies the cruelty of the nation. The three ribs, or wings, which it held in its mouth foreshadowed its rule over three parts of the inhabited world. For Cyrus, who destroyed the kingdom of the Assyrians, subdued the whole east as far as the Hellespont; his son Cambyses brought Egypt and Ethiopia under him, and these lands are in the south; and Darius the son of Hystaspes also subdued the nomad Scythians, who live in the northern region. Xerxes the son of Darius set out against Europe too, trying to master it, but was beaten back, defeated by the Athenians in a sea battle, and went home in disgrace. The beast's standing on one side signifies that it would not lose its rule entirely, but be confined to a small part of it. For that kingdom, which had ruled all Asia before and Ethiopia, Egypt, Palestine, Phoenicia and Libya itself, lost the rest, and Media and Persis were left to it. Or the beast's standing on one side may be taken in another way: that the kingdom of the Persians would be idle for a time and do nothing. For when Alexander had destroyed it and then died, his sole rule was divided into four realms; and when his successors turned against one another and were weakened in their battles with each other, the Persians seized their chance, took heart again and recovered much of their rule.

The third beast was like a leopard, and it had four wings of a bird on its back, and four heads grew on this beast, and he says that power was given to it. This beast imaged the Macedonian kingdom. Alexander was likened to a leopard for his swiftness and nimbleness, which the wings also showed. Their being four hinted at the four parts of the inhabited world, which he went through like a bird and mastered almost entirely. The four heads signified the division of the rule after his death. For his dominion was divided into four kingdoms: Ptolemy the son of Lagus ruled Egypt, and his descendants down to Cleopatra; Seleucus became master of Syria and the lands next to it; Antigonus brought Asia under himself; and Antipater ruled Macedonia, or, as some record, Arrhidaeus, Alexander's half-brother on his father's side. For Plutarch of Chaeronea says that Perdiccas, who had the greatest power after Alexander's death, at once dragged Arrhidaeus about as a kind of bodyguard of the kingship, though he was born of an obscure and common woman and was deficient in mind; not that he was born so, but Olympias had ruined his understanding with drugs.

## 3.7

Of the fourth beast the prophet did not give the form, but said it was fearful and exceedingly terrible, with great teeth of iron, devouring and grinding and trampling the rest, and he said it was very different from the beasts before it. By it the kingdom of the Romans is signified. That is why the prophet gave it neither form nor name. For the rule of the Romans has had many forms: first it was directed by kings, from Romulus to the Tarquins; when their tyranny was ended it was an aristocracy, the senate and the consuls managing affairs, and at times a democracy, when the people often rose against the senate; afterwards it fell again into monarchy, and from this it came back to the name of kingship. He called the beast fearful and terrible because this kingdom became more powerful than the others, and brought under itself all that the Macedonian kingdom had not subdued: the whole of Greece, Carthage with Libya, Sicily, Sardinia and other islands, and various other western nations, which Alexander did not rule. Whoever wishes to know these things, let him read the books of the Roman Dio and the writings of Polybius.

He said the beast was fearful and terrible and had teeth of iron, since the rule of the Romans became more dreadful than all the other kingdoms; for in the image seen by Nebuchadnezzar in the dream he set iron as the figure of the fourth kingdom. So he said that the beast devoured and ground with its iron teeth, and trampled the rest with its feet. Devouring and grinding are taken to mean the levying of taxes, since heavier tributes were laid on the subjects, which feed and fatten the rulers but make those from whom they are exacted thin with poverty. Those who would not endure paying tribute, claiming their freedom, the beast trampled with its feet and destroyed; and by the feet the soldiery is hinted at, since the kingdom rests and stands on it and moves by it from one place to another. He says the beast had ten horns, suggesting that at the last the kingdom will be divided into many realms. In the midst of the ten a little horn came up, and three of the horns were uprooted by it; that horn had eyes and a mouth speaking great things, and made war on the saints. Some have taken this horn to mean the Antichrist. It was called little as being destined to spring from a small tribe of the Hebrews. He will destroy three of the ten kings. By the eyes he hinted at his wickedness and cunning, since through these he will deceive many; by the mouth speaking great things he revealed his boastfulness and the pride of his spirit. Making war with the saints shows that he will display all zeal to make everyone share his own wickedness and blasphemy. And "he will speak words against the Most High" shows his blasphemy against God.

After this the prophet went on: "I watched until thrones were set, and the Ancient of Days sat down; his garment was white as snow, and the hair of his head like pure wool; his throne was a flame of fire, its wheels burning fire; a river of fire flowed out before him; a thousand thousands served him, and ten thousand times ten thousand stood before him. The court sat, and books were opened." What the horn has been said to do was shown to Daniel as not lasting long, until the Eternal One set the court, that is, fixed the time of judgement, and unrolled the record of what each had done. For he called the records books, and the Eternal One the Ancient of Days. The whiteness of the hair and of the garment shows His purity and blamelessness in every way. And to show that what the Antichrist will do will happen by God's permission, and not as if the divine nature were unable to stop it or to refuse to let it happen at all, he brought in the thousand thousands of ministers and the ten thousand times ten thousand standing by, the throne of fire, the wheels like it and the flaming river. And he watched until the beast was killed and its body given to be burned with fire. For because of the madness of the Antichrist the whole kingdom will be taken away. And it is not the beast itself that will be given to the burning, but its body. For since the beast hints at the kingdom, it is likely that in the kingdom there are many who worship God and pursue virtue. It is not these, he says, who are given to the burning, but the workers of wickedness, those always devoted to the desires of the body and who have had no spiritual thought.

After the destruction of the beast the prophet says he saw one like a son of man coming on the clouds and reaching the Ancient of Days; to him, he says, were given rule and honour and the kingdom, and all peoples, tongues and tribes served him; his power would be eternal and would not pass away, and his kingdom would not be destroyed. This clearly foretells the second appearing of our Saviour Jesus Christ. He is called son of man because of our nature, which He took on, and coming on the clouds according to His own promise, when He said: "You will see the Son of Man coming on the clouds of heaven."

## 3.8

These things Daniel was taught in the first year of the reign of Belshazzar; and in the third he saw another vision. "For being in Susa," he says, "I raised my eyes, and see, a ram standing at the Ubal, and it had high horns, and one was higher than the other, and the higher came up last. It was butting towards the sea, the north and the south, and no beasts could stand before it; and it did as it pleased and grew great." Here again the prophet is taught about the kingdoms. The rule of the Persians was likened to a ram because of the wealth that surrounded it; and the ram stood at the Ubal, that is, at the gate of Susa, for this city was the capital of the Persians, the kings had their palace there, and the prophet saw the vision while living in this city. The two horns, which directed this kingdom, figured two families. The lesser horn imaged Cyrus and his son Cambyses, for the rule of the kingdom stopped with him, and the line of Cyrus went no further; the greater horn hinted at the line of Darius, which went on until the last Darius, whom Alexander defeated. For when Cambyses, the son of Cyrus, died, the magi seized the kingdom by trickery; and when they had ruled a short time and it was discovered who they were, the seven houses of the Persians overthrew them. Of these Darius the son of Hystaspes became king, and his line lasted to the end of the kingdom. This is what the prophet shows when he adds that the higher came up last: that one of the horns lasted long, until the end of the Persian kingdom.

The ram butted towards the south, the north and the sea: for it subdued the southern region and the northern, and enslaved most of the islands, which he hinted at by the sea; and the islanders fought on the side of Xerxes when he marched against Greece; that is why the prophet saw the ram butting towards the sea. And none of the beasts could stand before it. He called the lesser kingdoms beasts, as fearsome to their subjects; these would be that of the Syrians, the Cilicians, the Arabs, the Egyptians, the Jews and others. None of them could hold out in the end against the rule of the Persians, but it subdued all it attacked. "And it grew great": either because the rule became great, mastering many nations and lands, or because, swollen with good fortune, it imagined things great and beyond human nature.

While the prophet was at a loss about what he saw, he saw a he-goat coming from the south-west over the whole earth, and it had one conspicuous horn between its eyes. Reaching the ram, it was enraged against it, struck it, broke its horns, threw it to the ground and trampled it, and there was no one to rescue the ram from its hand. The he-goat prefigured the Macedonian kingdom, for it is swifter than the ram and more nimble. It came from the south-west, because Alexander first brought Egypt under him and then set out against Darius the second time, routed him and ended the kingdom of the Persians. For he had engaged him earlier at Issus in Cilicia and defeated him, and captured his wife, his daughters, his chariot and his bow and plundered the Persian camp; Darius fled from there, gathered forces again and fought Alexander when he set out against him from Egypt, and was defeated again at Arbela and perished in flight. He calls the horn conspicuous, meaning notable and admired; by this too he hints at the kingship of Alexander. He says the horn grew between its eyes because of Alexander's quickness of mind, his understanding and the nobility of his spirit. "And the he-goat came," he says, "to the ram that had the horns, and I saw it reach the ram, and it was enraged against it, struck the ram and broke both its horns, and the ram had no strength to stand before it, and it threw it to the ground and trampled it." Both the ram's horns were broken, he says, when the he-goat dashed against it: that is, both the forces in which it trusted, the Persian and the Median. For the kings of the Persians ruled Persians and Medes, since Cyrus, as has been said, was sprung from both and held the kingdom of both under him, and both these races ended the kingdom of the Assyrians. "And the he-goat grew very great." For he not only brought under himself the subjects of the Assyrian rule, but subdued many others too: he attacked India, defeated Porus, won over Taxiles and took other parts of India. Then the prophet, showing that he too, after prospering, would pass away soon, went on: "And when it was strong, the great horn was broken, and four horns grew up beneath it." For when Alexander died soon after that success, his rule was divided into four, as has already been said about the third beast, which seemed to Daniel in his sleep to come out of the sea, having four heads.

"And from one of the horns came out a fearful horn, and it grew exceedingly great towards the south and the east and the south-west and towards the host. And it grew great up to the host of heaven, and some of the host and of the stars fell to the earth, and it trampled them." By this Daniel is taught beforehand about Antiochus Epiphanes, who was the son of Antiochus the Great, but grew stronger than the kings before him, those descended from Seleucus; he mastered Egypt and other lands, marched against the Jews, became master of the city of Jerusalem and indeed of the whole nation, plundered the temple and defiled it by sacrificing swine in it, set up an altar to Zeus and forced the whole nation to adopt Greek ways, forbidding them to circumcise their males. "It grew great up to the host of heaven" hints at his madness against God. And "some of the host and of the stars fell to the earth, and it trampled them" signifies those of the Jewish people who turned impious under the compulsion brought on them by him, and fell from the lawful and divine way of life, yielding to the tyrant and being trampled by him. They were called stars because of the purity and brightness of piety, or because God promised Abraham to multiply his seed like the stars of heaven.

"And it set itself up even against the prince of the host": for he raged even against God Himself, the ruler of the powers above. "And the sacrifice was disturbed by a fall, and it came to pass and prospered, and sin was laid on the sacrifice, and righteousness was thrown to the ground." For by offering forbidden victims he disturbed the sacrifice, that is, confounded and defiled it. He calls the swine falls, and the sin laid on the sacrifice the lawlessness done at it. By "righteousness was thrown to the ground" the setting aside of the laws is shown, and the unjust murders of those who would not accept impiety. Yet even while doing these things and being so impious, he says, Antiochus prospered.

After this Daniel adds: "And one holy one said to Phelmouni, How long will the vision stand, and the sacrifice taken away, and the sin of desolation that has been given? And he said to him, Until evening and morning, two thousand three hundred days, and the sanctuary will be cleansed." Angels are sent to the prophet in his anguish at what he saw, to show him that the troubles will have an end and to teach him the time of their passing. One asked the other; for *phelmouni*, put into Greek, means "a certain one". He asked not as not knowing, but so that Daniel might learn. The one asked answered: "Two thousand days, until evening and morning, and the sanctuary will be cleansed." He called evening the beginning of the troubles, or their whole duration, for sorrows are like night and darkness; and morning the peaceful state after the calamities. This then is what he signifies by saying that from the beginning of the evils to their end so much time will pass. The days, counted as years, make six years and as many months by the Hebrew reckoning; for so long did the calamity brought on the Jews under Antiochus prevail. These things were taught to the prophet by the archangel Gabriel when he sought understanding.

## 3.9

Then, under Darius again, the ruler of the Medes, who was also named Cyaxares and Ahasuerus, the son of Astyages, as the prophet was lamenting the captivity of his countrymen and praying to God, a man whose name was Gabriel touched him. He touched him about the hour of the evening sacrifice, either at the time of the evening service, or for as long a time as the evening service was being performed. And he said to Daniel: "Now I have come out to give you understanding and tell you that you are a man of desires. Consider the word and understand the vision"; that is, pay close attention to what is said, since it is in riddles and needs more effort. Sometimes divine things are revealed through riddles, so that they may not be plain to all and so be easily despised. Daniel was called a man of desires either as one who played the man against fleshly desires and nobly resisted them, or as one who desired to learn what would befall the people and his fellow tribesmen, or because he was desirable and lovable for the virtue he pursued.

Then the angel goes on: "Seventy weeks have been cut off for your people and for your holy city, until the transgression grows old and the sin is completed, and to seal sin and atone for iniquity, and to bring in everlasting righteousness, and to seal vision and prophet, and to anoint the holy of holies." The prophet in his prayer said to God, "your people and your city"; but the angel says to Daniel, "your people and your city", as though the people were not worthy to be called God's. The same is found in the time of Moses, when the Israelites made the calf and offered sacrifice to it; for then too, when the prophet prayed, God spoke in the same way. So the angel says that Jerusalem will be built and your people will return and remain for four hundred and ninety years; for the seventy weeks add up to this number, each day being counted as a year. "Have been cut off" stands for "have been decided and fixed". "Until the transgression grows old and the sin is completed": that is, until it lasts and grows; for this is what the sin growing old and becoming complete means. By this he shows the daring and the frenzy of the Jews against the Lord. For they had sinned much before this and paid the penalty, but were called back again; but when they raged against the Saviour and reached the extreme of wickedness and committed sin in full, they were no longer called back.

"To seal sin and atone for iniquity" signifies the forgiveness of failings given by the Lord to those who believe in Him; for He is the one who takes away the sin of the world; and sealing signifies stopping. "To bring in everlasting righteousness" means that He came into the world, who is righteousness and redemption and sanctification. "To seal vision and prophet" signifies that the prophecies have reached their end, either because what the prophets said about Christ has already come to pass, or because the gift of prophecy has failed among the Jews, or both. For Christ, when He came, sealed the prophecies, that is, fulfilled and confirmed them, and also ended them; for there is no longer among them ruler or prophet or leader. "And to bring in everlasting righteousness", which is our Lord and Saviour Himself. For the apostle says: "He was made for us wisdom from God, righteousness and sanctification and redemption"; and elsewhere, writing about the gospel, he says: "In it righteousness is revealed." And the Lord teaches: "Seek the kingdom of God and His righteousness", which He Himself brought in, making it manifest to us by His teaching. "To anoint the holy of holies" signifies the coming of the Master Christ Himself. For who else could be called the holy of holies except our Lord and Saviour? He, being the fountain of holiness, is anointed with the Holy Spirit in His humanity, as Isaiah proclaimed beforehand, saying: "The Spirit of the Lord is upon me, because He has anointed me"; and as David sang, saying: "God, your God, has anointed you with the oil of gladness above your fellows"; and as Peter, the chief and crown of the apostles, taught, writing of the Saviour that "God anointed Him with the Holy Spirit and with power". He seals and confirms the old Scriptures, doing and suffering everything that had been foretold in them, and atones for iniquity, reconciling sinners to God the Father and making Him gracious to those who have failed.

Then the divine archangel goes on: "And you will know and understand: from the going out of the word to answer and to build Jerusalem until Christ the leader, seven weeks and sixty-two weeks." The temple began to be built under Cyrus; for Cyrus first allowed the nation of the Jews to return; but it was stopped by Cambyses, the son of Cyrus, through the envy of the nations bordering on them, and the raising of it was permitted to the Jews again when Darius the son of Hystaspes came to the throne of the Persians. Counted from neither of these is the time found to fit the archangel's words, but it is exceeded; but if the time is counted from the years of the reign of Artaxerxes, it would be found neither too long nor too short. And it must be counted from then, because it was then that the city was surrounded with a wall, what was lacking was added to the temple and the city was filled with inhabitants, when Nehemiah, Artaxerxes' cupbearer, begged him to let him return to his country, and went and walled the city, settled it and completed the temple, which was unfinished. If, then, one counts back the time from the appearing of the Saviour, through whom alone forgiveness of sins was given, righteousness brought in and the predictions of the prophets fulfilled, he will find it completed back to the twentieth year of the reign of Artaxerxes, when Nehemiah was permitted to go back to Jerusalem; which the history will tell more fully as it proceeds in order. Him whom he called above the holy of holies, he now called Christ the leader, naming Him in His humanity. For as He is called the first-born of the new creation and the first-born from the dead, so He is also called leader. And the divine apostle Peter named the Saviour the author of life when speaking to the Jews, as the divine Luke wrote in the Acts. And as He is called, according to the great Paul, our first-born and the first among us in His humanity, so too He is called leader by the archangel Gabriel.

## 3.10

One might ask why the weeks are counted separately and not together, divided into seven and sixty-two and one more. The archangel did not do this at random, but to teach by it certain new things that would happen at the divisions of time. For if the years are counted from the building of Jerusalem under Nehemiah, as has been said, to the high priest Hyrcanus, in whose time the line of the Hasmonaeans ceased to be priests, the number of sixty-two weeks is completed. From the twentieth year of the reign of Artaxerxes, who granted Nehemiah the return to Jerusalem to wall it, to Darius the son of Arsames, whom Alexander overthrew, there are a hundred and fourteen years; from the overthrow of the kingdom of the Persians, which happened in the sixth year of Alexander's campaign into Asia, to Gaius Julius Caesar, who was the first to attempt monarchy over the Romans, two hundred and eighty-two years passed; and from Caesar's sole rule to the fifteenth year of Tiberius Caesar, when Christ was baptized by John and began His signs, seventy-three years; so that these add up to four hundred and sixty-nine years. These years make four hundred and eighty-three years by the Hebrew reckoning of years. For the Hebrews do not reckon the year as we do, but counting it by the course of the moon they reckon it at three hundred and fifty-four days, so that eleven days are left over from each year; and when these extra days are added up into years, the four hundred and eighty-three Hebrew years result. And the angel, speaking to Daniel, a Hebrew, surely spoke of years familiar to him.

So the sixty-two weeks were measured out up to the death of the high priest Hyrcanus, whom King Herod killed (the story of this will be told more fully). From then on, since the high priesthood was no longer fixed for life on those thought worthy of it, as the law required, but was given unlawfully for a year or limited to a shorter time, and those who wished bought it with money, as Josephus records, the seven weeks passed. Showing this, the divine archangel said: "And after the sixty-two weeks the anointing will be destroyed, and there is no judgement in it"; that is, the anointing of the high priesthood will be ruined. For since those promoted to it were anointed unlawfully, the anointing did not have its proper force, but, being corrupted, lacked the divine grace. That is why he added, "and there is no judgement in it": that is, they will not be appointed by judgement and examination, but without judgement, for favour or for money. While this was happening the seven weeks too slipped away, lasting, that is, until the appearing of the Lord.

The division of the sixty-two weeks and the seven was made for these reasons. Since one more remains of the seventy weeks spoken of at first, the archangel said this about it too: "And one week will strengthen the covenant for many, and in the half of the week sacrifice and libation will be taken away, and on the temple the abomination of desolation, and until the end of the time an end will be put to the desolation." The remaining week, he says, after the sixty-nine, will bring in the new covenant so strong that through it the sacrifices and libations will be taken away, that is, removed, in the half of the week. For after His baptism Christ taught for three years and more and showed signs, and so came to His passion; and when He, the Lamb of God who takes away the sin of the world, was sacrificed for the whole world, the sacrifice and libation according to the law ceased. From then on the Jews sacrificed (as they still do) unlawfully, but their sacrifice and libation were not acceptable to God. "Will strengthen the covenant for many" may also be taken in another way: that that week will make the holy apostles strong for the preaching. For the archangel appears to divide the week into two, the time before the passion and the time after it. For the Lord, having preached for about three and a half years, according to the gospel of the son of thunder, and having established His divine apostles in faith in Him by the truth of His words and the display of wonderful works, so went to His passion, accepted the cross and endured death. Then, for the rest of the week, after the resurrection from the dead, the ascent to the Father's bosom, from which He had never been parted, and the coming of the Comforter and His divine inspiration, His holy disciples in Jerusalem, confirming the gospel and the preaching with wonders and drawing multitudes of men to the knowledge of the true God, scattered among the nations and enlightened them. Foretelling this to the prophet Daniel, the archangel said, "One week will strengthen the covenant for many," that is, will gird many with power, namely the disciples. Having joined the time before the Lord's passion and the time after it, during which the apostles first stayed and preached in Jerusalem and then went round to the ends of the world and taught, he divides this week again, saying: "And in the half of the week sacrifice and libation will be taken away," foretelling by this the ending of the shadow of the law.

Then he goes on: "And on the temple the abomination of desolation." For when these things happen, he says, the temple, formerly holy and revered, will be reckoned an abomination; or after this last week some abomination will be brought into the temple, a sign of the desolation of it and of the city. This happened under Pilate, when he brought in by night standards bearing the emperor's images, which the Jews thought an abomination; for they were forbidden to possess or revere an image of a man or of any animal. And the Lord said: "When you see the abomination of desolation standing in the holy place, know that its desolation has drawn near," that is, Jerusalem's. And elsewhere the Lord said: "See, your house is left to you desolate." Teaching Daniel these things beforehand, the archangel, after foretelling that the worship of the law would be removed, adds: "And on the temple the abomination of desolation." And so that the Jews might not think the desolation temporary, as if the city would be rebuilt for them again and the temple raised, as had happened often before to the temple and the city, he went on: "And until the end of the time an end will be put to the desolation"; that is, the desolation will remain until the age is ended, and it too will end then, when there is no longer time for either the city or the temple to rise.

These things Daniel saw and was taught about his countrymen. But he saw other visions and dreams too, through which the change to come in the affairs of the world was revealed to him beforehand by God. He was taught not only the foreknowledge of the change, but also the times at which each of them would be fulfilled. Whoever wishes will surely learn these by going through the book of the prophet's visions more fully, and will gain from it a firm grasp that nothing is without providence or carried along at random and by chance, but everything is governed and directed by divine providence. So these things stand, and for the pious

## 3.11

are glorified. But we must not pass over the story of the manly-minded woman Judith, who destroyed Holofernes and saved her city and the whole nation. Her story is this. In the twelfth year of his reign the leader of the Assyrians, Nebuchadnezzar, made war on Arphaxad, the king of the Medes, and called the surrounding nations to his alliance. When many others, and the Jews among them, did not accept the summons and refused to fight on his side, Nebuchadnezzar was enraged, and swore that when he had finished the war against the Medes he would come against Cilicia, Damascus, Syria, Judaea and Egypt, ravage and sack them and make them a desert. So he drew up against Arphaxad the Mede and prevailed over him by force, mastered his other cities and Ecbatana itself, where Arphaxad had his palace, and captured and killed him. Then he called Holofernes, the commander-in-chief of his own force, and ordered him to fulfil the oath he had sworn: to march with a heavy force against those who would not fight on his side when he went against the Medes; to preserve those who submitted to him and leave their cities unsacked, but not to spare the disobedient, but to put the men to the sword and give their cities over to plunder and destruction.

Nebuchadnezzar gave these orders to his chief satrap. Holofernes marched out and, attacking the nations against which he had been sent, dealt with the others as he had been ordered; but when he was about to march against the Israelites and take up arms against them (for they did not come to him or submit), he asked who these people were and in what they trusted to resist. Achior, the leader of the sons of Ammon, told him their genealogy, how they had first settled in those cities, and the strength of their God. Then he went on: "Now we must consider: if they are sinning against their God, we will go up and defeat them in war; but if not, let my lord pass them by, lest their God shield them and we be defeated." When Achior said this, Holofernes, seized with anger, ordered those standing by him to bind Achior, lead him away and hand him over to the sons of Israel, so that he might be taken with them and punished. They seized the man and led him off to Bethulia, and the people of the city shot at them with slings and arrows as they came near. They threw Achior down, bound, at the foot of the hill and went back. The men of the city came down, untied Achior, brought him into their city and asked what had happened; and he told them everything. They called on God for help.

The next day Holofernes led his army against Bethulia, a hundred and seventy thousand foot and twelve thousand horse, apart from the baggage train, and camped by it. Then he decided not to fight, but to seize first the waters from which they drew, so that they would perish when they ran out through thirst or surrender their city. He set part of the army round the waters as guards. When the enemy had sat round them for thirty-four days, their water failed them, and the people gathered before the rulers and asked them to surrender the city and themselves to the enemy, so that they might either serve them and live, or be slaughtered and be rid of their sufferings. Ozias, the foremost man of the city, said: "Brothers, let us wait five more days; if help comes to us from God, well; if not, I will do as you advise."

## 3.12

There was in that city a woman named Judith, a widow, chaste, sensible and beautiful to look at. Hearing what the people and the rulers had decided, she sent for Ozias and the other rulers of the city and rebuked them for testing God by saying they would surrender the city to the enemy after five days unless some help came to them from God in that time. They agreed that she spoke well, but pleaded the people's thirst and the pressure from them. She told them to stand at the gate of the city that night; she herself, she said, would go out with her maid, but she would not tell them what she was planning until it had been carried through to action. They said to her: "Go." They went away, and she turned to entreating God. Then she put off the mourning garments of her widowhood, bathed her body in water, anointed it with perfume, changed into a festive robe and put on ornaments. Adorned to make herself more alluring, she gave her maidservant wine and oil in vessels to carry and a bag full of parched grain, fig cakes and loaves, and she and her maid went out of the city towards the enemy's camp.

Meeting the outpost of the Assyrians, she was held and brought to Holofernes. He admired her beauty and said to her: "Do not be afraid; you have come to safety." The woman said to him: "Receive my words, lord, and let the word that Achior spoke be in your heart; for it is true that unless the sons of Israel sin, no sword will prevail against them. But now, since their food and water have failed them, they have resolved to touch even what is forbidden by the law, and they have decided to consume the first-fruits of the grain and the tithes of the wine kept for the priests, which no one else may lawfully even touch. Since those who live in Jerusalem have resolved this too, they have sent to the council of elders there, asking for permission. And if they break the law in this way, there will be no help for them from God, and they will be given over to destruction. Knowing this, I am running away from the city to escape the destruction. Now I will stay with you, master, and go out at night into the ravine and pray to God, and He will tell me when they have sinned, and I will report it to you, and lead you through Judaea to Jerusalem and set your throne in the midst of it." Holofernes was pleased with her words, and ordered her to be brought into the tent where his treasures were and given food. She said: "I will not eat of your food, lest it become a stumbling block to me, but I will feed on what I have brought."
