Holofernes said to her: "And if this runs out, where will such things be supplied to you?" She answered: "As your soul lives, my lord, my food will not run out before God has done by my hand what He has planned." At midnight she asked to go out to the ravine to pray, and Holofernes allowed it; and she did so for three days.

On the fourth day Holofernes prepared a drinking party and said to the eunuch Bagoas, who was in charge of all he had: "Persuade the Hebrew woman to come to us; for it would be shameful for us to let such a woman go without having had her company." Bagoas said to Judith: "Come to my lord, to be honoured in his presence, drink wine with us, and become like one of the women who attend in the house of Nebuchadnezzar." Judith adorned herself and went, and coming in to Holofernes she reclined, and ate and drank what her maid had prepared for her. Holofernes' heart was beside itself and burned with love for the woman. The drinking went on long, and he drank a great deal of wine and was drunk. He lay on his bed heavy-headed from drink, and all went away, and Bagoas closed the tent, leaving Judith alone inside. She had told her maidservant to watch for her coming out, for she would go out to pray. Holofernes lay sunk in drunkenness, sleeping the sleep that is death's brother; and she, calling on God, drew his short sword, cut off his head, went out and gave it to her maidservant, and went off as if to pray according to her custom.

Coming near her city, she called to those at the gates to open to her. Going in, she told the wonders God had done through her, brought out the head of Holofernes, and advised them to hang it from the battlements, and to put on their armour and go out at dawn: "For when the Assyrians see you," she said, "they will run to the tent of their commander-in-chief, and finding him killed they will be beside themselves, fear will fall on them and they will turn to flight; and you will follow them and cut them down, and the plains will be filled with the dead." Having given this advice to her fellow citizens, she said: "Call Achior to me"; and when he came she showed him the head of Holofernes. The man was astonished and said: "Tell me all you have done." She told before them all what God had done through her, and how He had kept her chastity and widowhood undefiled. Achior believed in God and was circumcised. The rulers of the city and its multitude hung the head of Holofernes from the wall and did the rest according to Judith's advice. They cut the Assyrians down like grass, plundered their camp and were filled with much spoil. The tent of Holofernes and everything in it were set apart for Judith, and she went to Jerusalem and dedicated them as an offering to God. Crowned with a shoot of olive, she sang a song to Him, with all Israel; and she returned to her house, grew old in chastity and died at the age of a hundred and five.

## 3.13

So much for the story of Judith. Next, the story of Tobit must be told in brief. Tobit was descended from the tribe of Naphtali, and had been taken captive in the days of Enemessar, king of the Assyrians. He was pious towards God and just towards men; when his fellow tribesmen sacrificed to Baal, he went to Jerusalem and sacrificed to God, ordered his life according to the commands of the law, and when taken captive did not defile himself with gentile food. He became honoured by Enemessar and acquired much property, and shared it with the needy of his own race. Travelling to Media, he deposited ten talents of silver with Gamael, one of his countrymen living there. He married Anna, a woman of his own people, and had a son by her, Tobias.

When Enemessar died, his son Sennacherib succeeded to the rule of the Assyrians; he marched against Judaea, fled shamefully from there and came back home, and in anger at his defeat killed very many of the Israelites; and Tobit buried them by night. He was found out doing this, and when he was sought he fled, and everything in his house was plundered. Before fifty days had passed, Sennacherib was killed by his sons; the parricides fled in fear, and another son of his, Esarhaddon, succeeded to his father's kingdom. He made a kinsman of Tobit, called Achiacharus, the manager of his affairs. This man interceded with the king for Tobit, and he returned to Nineveh.

When Pentecost came, which the Jews kept after seven weeks, a lavish meal was prepared for Tobit. He said to his son Tobias: "Go, child, and bring whatever needy man of our brothers you find who is mindful of the Lord, to share our table." He went and came back saying: "One of our race has been strangled and thrown out in the market-place." At once Tobit went, took up the body and brought it into the house, and when the sun had set he buried him. Returning, he did not go into the house, since by the law he was unclean, having touched the dead, but slept by the wall in the courtyard. Sparrows spending the night on the wall dropped their dung into his eyes, from which white films formed in them, and from these Tobit was blinded. Being in want, he was fed by his wife, who spun for pay. Once, receiving her pay and a kid besides, she came to Tobit. Hearing the kid's bleating, he asked whether it was stolen. She said: "It was given me as a gift besides my pay." He pressed her, saying: "If it is stolen, let it be given back to its owners." The woman, deeply hurt and as if mocking her husband for his strictness about justice, said: "Where are your almsgivings and your righteous deeds?" Tobit, distressed, wept and asked God to be released from life.

On that same day, at Ecbatana in Media, it happened that Sarah, the daughter of Raguel, was reproached by her father's maidservants for strangling her husbands, since she had been married to seven and had belonged to none of them. For a demon called Asmodaeus killed them when they went in to her. Unable to bear the reproach, she decided to hang herself. But so as not to give her father grief and reproach, she did not do it, but stood in prayer and called on God, asking to die and be freed from the reproaches, or to be pitied and so escape the reproach.

So the prayer of both was heard, and the archangel Raphael was sent by God to heal Tobit's blindness, to betroth Sarah to Tobias, and to bind Asmodaeus, the evil demon, so that he might do Tobias no harm if he married Sarah. Tobit called his son Tobias and, after giving him much advice, said to him at last: "I deposited ten talents of silver with Gamael, the son of Gabrias, at Rages in Media; go and get them." And he gave him the bond for them and told him to look for a man who would go with him for pay. Looking for a travelling companion, he met the archangel Raphael, who appeared to him in the form of a man and declared that he knew the road and the house of Gamael and would go with him. Taking him, as a man called Azarias, he set off. In the evening they came to the river Tigris and camped there. Tobias went into the river to wash, and a fish leapt out of it to swallow the youth. The angel told him to catch the fish, and when it was pulled to land he told him to cut it open, and advised Tobias to take its heart, liver and gall and keep them safe.

## 3.14

As they travelled and drew near Ecbatana, Tobias said: "Brother Azarias, what use will the heart, liver and gall of the fish be to us?" He answered: "The heart and liver, burned as incense, drive away demons if they trouble anyone; and the gall, smeared on eyes that have white films, clears them and gives sight." When they were already near Ecbatana, the angel said to Tobias: "Today we shall lodge with Raguel, who is your kinsman, who has a daughter Sarah, sensible and beautiful, and I will speak to have her given to you as wife." He said: "I have heard that the girl has been given to seven husbands and that all died in the bridal chamber, because a demon loves her and destroys those who woo her; and I am afraid that I too will be killed by it." The angel said: "Listen to me, and take no account of the demon. When you go into the bridal chamber, put some of the heart and liver of the fish on coals, and the demon will smell their odour and flee, and will not come back."

They went into Raguel's house. Learning that Tobias was the son of Tobit, he kissed him, and hearing that Tobit was blind, he wept and received them gladly. The angel told Raguel to give Sarah to him in marriage; he consented, called his daughter, took her by the hand, gave her to Tobias, and taking a document wrote a contract. After dinner they brought Tobias to her in the inner room. As he went he took coals, put some of the heart and liver of the fish on them and made smoke, and the smell drove the demon away. At daybreak Raguel sent one of the maidservants to see whether Tobias was alive. When she came back and said he was alive, Raguel blessed God. He said to Tobias: "Let us celebrate the wedding for fourteen days, and you shall not leave here before they have passed. Then take half of what I have and go; the rest you shall take when I and my companion in life are dead." So Tobias said to Raphael: "Brother Azarias, take the bond for me, go to Gamael at Rages and bring the silver; for if we delay, my father will be greatly distressed." The angel went, handed the bond to Gamael, received the silver and came back.

When the days of the wedding had passed, Tobias said to Raguel, "Send me away," and taking his wife and half of Raguel's property he set off. When they were near Nineveh, the angel said to him: "Let us run on ahead and prepare the house. Take the gall of the fish in your hands; your father will open his eyes, and you smear them with the gall; feeling the sting, he will rub them and shed the white films." His mother sat watching the road; and when she saw her son coming, she hurried and said to Tobit, "Your son is coming," and running to him she kissed him and said: "I have seen you, child, and now if I die, I do not care." Tobit came out to the door, stumbling. Tobias took hold of him and smeared the gall on his eyes, and they stung, and he rubbed them, and the white films peeled off them. Seeing his son, he blessed God. When Tobit was told what had happened to Tobias in Media, he went out to meet his daughter-in-law, and seeing her he blessed her. The people of Nineveh, seeing him able to see and hearing of the great things God had done, were amazed.

Tobit said to his son: "Give the man who went with you his pay, child, and indeed we must add something to it." He said: "Father, it is right that he take half of what I have brought." And he said to Raphael: "Brother Azarias, take half of all we have brought, and go in health." Then the angel called the two of them aside and told them all the great things God had done with them, and said that he was Raphael, and had come not of himself but sent by God. He urged them to bless God and give thanks for all He had done with them. "For it is good," he said, "to hide a king's secret, but to proclaim the works of God gloriously." Hearing this, they fell at his feet; and when they rose they saw him no more, and they gave thanks to God for the wonders He had done with them.

Tobit was fifty-eight years old when he lost his sight, and after eight years he was restored to sight; and from then on he feared God the more and did what was good. Having grown very old, he said to his son: "Child, see, I have grown old and am near death; take your sons and go to Media. For I am persuaded that what Jonah foretold about Nineveh, that it would be overthrown, will come to pass, and that Jerusalem will be desolate and the temple burned, and God will again have mercy on the city, and the people will return and build it. But you, keep the law and be merciful and just." Having given his son such charges he died, at the age of a hundred and fifty-eight. Tobias buried him with honour, and his mother, and went off with his wife to Ecbatana, to his father-in-law Raguel; and he buried him too in his old age and inherited from him. Tobias died too, at a hundred and twenty-seven, at Ecbatana in Media, and before he died he heard of the destruction of Nineveh, taken by Nebuchadnezzar and Ahasuerus.

## 3.15

The whole nation of the Hebrews, as has been told, was taken captive by the Assyrians and carried away, and Jerusalem was left desolate; and it was not freed from captivity until the rule of the Assyrians was ended by the Medes and Persians, according to the prophetic prediction, and the seventy years had passed which the prophet Jeremiah had proclaimed beforehand. It would not be out of place to go through the overthrow of the kingdom of the Assyrians in brief too, and to show that the prophetic prediction was true, which foretold that it would be ended by the Medes and Persians. It went as follows.

The Medes and the Persians were kingdoms each by itself. Astyages ruled the one, and Cambyses that of the Persians. Cambyses was married to the daughter of Astyages, called Mandane, who bore Cambyses a son, Cyrus. Brought up under the laws of the Persians, he became most brave and most self-controlled, sensible and most just. The customs of the Persians were these, as Xenophon wrote. They had a square called the Free Square, on which their palace and the other public buildings were built; from it goods for sale, traders, their bustle and their cries were driven away, so as not to mix with the good order of the educated. The Free Square was divided into four quarters: one was set aside for boys, another for youths, another for grown men, and the last assigned to those past the years of military service; and each attended at his own place. It was the custom for the youths to sleep about the public buildings with their light arms, except those who were married; these were not sought unless notice had been given, and were not often allowed to be absent. Each group had twelve officers, for the Persians were divided into twelve tribes.

The boys, going to the schools, learned justice, their officers spending most of the day judging cases for them. For among boys too there were charges against one another, as among men, of theft, violence, robbery, deceit, insult and other things. Those they found guilty of these, they punished; and those they found accusing unjustly, they chastised. They taught the boys self-control, by watching their elders living with self-control; and they taught them restraint in food and drink. When the boys were to eat, they ate with their teacher, bringing bread from home and cress as relish; and for drink they took a cup of river water. Before eating they learned to shoot the bow and throw the javelin. The boys did this until they were sixteen or seventeen; from then they were enrolled among the youths, and for ten years they slept about the public buildings, and during the day they put themselves at the disposal of the officers for whatever they needed them for on public business. When the king went out hunting, he took out half their number. They hunted together, because this exercise seemed to them akin to training for war: it accustomed them to rise early and endure cold and heat, exercised them in marches and running, taught them to shoot and throw at a beast, and prepared them to face one of the fierce beasts. On the hunt they carried a meal larger than the boys', but otherwise similar. And they did not eat it while hunting, unless they had to stay for a beast or wished otherwise to spend time on the hunt. Making their dinner of this meal, they hunted the next day until dinner, and counted these two days as one. They did this so that they might be able to do it if needed in war. For relish they had whatever they caught, and if not, the cress. The tribes that stayed at home spent their time practising, among other things, shooting and throwing the javelin and competing with each other in these. The officers used the youths who stayed for public works needing strength or speed.

When the youths had spent ten years in these things, they passed into the grown men, and from then they lived so for twenty-five years. If they had to go to war, they no longer took bows or javelins, but the arms called close-fighting: a breastplate on the chest, a wicker shield on the left arm, and in the right hand a sword or cleaver. All the officials too were appointed from these. When they had completed the twenty-five years, being over fifty years from birth, they then went out into the elders. The elders no longer served on campaign outside their own country, but stayed at home and judged all cases public and private; they also judged capital cases and chose all the officials. If anyone among the youths or the men broke any of the customs, the elders expelled him; and the one expelled lived in disgrace for the rest of his life. Among the Persians it was thought shameful to spit, to blow one's nose, to be seen full of wind, or to be seen urinating or relieving one's bowels. They could not do these things unless they kept a moderate diet and used up the moisture by exertion. These were the ways and customs of the Persians.

## 3.16

Cyrus, brought up and educated in these, passed beyond the age of youth and was enrolled among the men, and won distinction in everything. When his mother's father Astyages, king of the Medes, had died, his son Cyaxares, who was also named Darius, succeeded to his father's rule, being Cyrus's uncle.

The ruler of the Assyrians, since his kingdom had grown great, ruled the numerous Assyrian race, had brought the Arabs under him, made the Hyrcanians subjects and the Syrians tributary, was besieging the Bactrians, had already subdued the race of the Hebrews and had made very many other nations subject, thought that if he weakened the Medes he would more easily master all those round about. With this in mind he made his subjects ready, and sent to Croesus, the king of the Lydians, to both the Phrygians, to the Paphlagonians and Indians, and to the Carians and Cilicians, asking them to ally with him as he set out against the Medes, since the war was to their advantage too; for he said the nation was powerful, had made marriage ties with the Persians and gained their help besides, and each was strengthened by the other, so that unless someone weakened them first they would attack each of the nations and master them. He sent such words to each of the nations; and some, moved by these words, some won over by money and gifts, and some overcome by fear of the Assyrian, agreed to ally with him.

Cyaxares, learning this, made his own preparations, and sent to the Persians, both to the commonwealth and to their king Cambyses, asking for an alliance and asking that his nephew Cyrus come as commander of the allies; for he had already been counted among the grown men. When the Persians agreed to the alliance, the elders chose Cyrus as commander of the army for the Medes, and gave him thirty thousand targeteers, archers and slingers. Chosen, he took leave of his father and set off for the Medes with the army. When he arrived, since the Assyrian had not yet come, he ordered his men to practise the arts of war.

Meanwhile a messenger came from Cyaxares saying that an embassy of Indians had come, and "you must be present too. I bring you a very fine robe; for he wishes to present you dressed most splendidly, so that you may be seen so by the Indians." When he came to Cyaxares' door with the army, and went in to him dressed in the Persian way, Cyaxares was annoyed at the plainness of his dress. The Indians were called in and said they had been sent by their king to ask the reason for the war between the Medes and the Assyrian, that they would learn the same from him too, and tell both that the king of the Indians, having considered the right, would side with the wronged. To this Cyaxares said: "We have done the Assyrian no wrong; ask him what he says." Cyrus said: "If the Assyrian says he is wronged by us, Indians, we choose your king himself as judge."

When they had heard this they went away. Cyrus, not wishing his army to sit idle, said to Cyaxares: "I remember hearing you say recently that the Armenian neither sends you an army nor pays the tribute, having broken the agreement. If you wish, send me, giving me a moderate number of horsemen, and I think, with the gods' help, he will both send the army and pay the tribute." When Cyaxares let him go, promising to send him the horsemen when he came near the borders of Armenia, Cyrus set off as if going out to hunt; for he was accustomed to hunt on the borders of both, I mean of the Medes and the Armenians. He went off and hunted, and climbed the mountains of Armenia, moving forward quietly. When Cyaxares learned this, he sent him the horsemen. When they came, Cyrus called together the captains and revealed the secret, saying he had come because of the Armenian, who had behaved unfairly towards Cyaxares. He ordered Chrysantas to take half the Persians and get into the mountains of the Armenians, taking care that it should not be known that there was an army there, but sending ahead some men looking like robbers in number and dress, so that if anyone met them, they would think them thieves. Chrysantas went to the mountains with the Persians, and Cyrus sent to the Armenian ordering him to bring the tribute and the army as quickly as possible, and himself advanced in battle order.

When the Armenian heard this he was terrified, and sent to gather his army, and sent his wives, his younger son and his wealth to the mountains; for no wall surrounded their dwelling place. When those sent to the mountains fell in with those lying in wait there, and the Armenian's wives, son and daughters were captured, he was at a loss and gave himself up to Cyrus.

## 3.17

Meanwhile the Armenian's elder son Tigranes, who had been abroad and had once been Cyrus's hunting companion, came back, and learning what had happened went straight to Cyrus as he was; and seeing his father, mother, sisters and his own wife captive, he wept. Cyrus said: "You have come at the right time." After talking at length with the Armenian and convicting him of wrongdoing, he said at last: "Tell me how great a force you have, and how much money." When he said there were eight thousand horsemen and forty thousand foot, and money reckoned in silver more than three thousand talents, Cyrus said: "Send half the army with me; of the money, pay Cyaxares twice the fifty talents of the yearly tribute instead of the fifty; and lend me," he said, "another hundred." With these words he gave the Armenian his wife and children as a gift, and Tigranes his own wife, whom he had just married, and let them all go. The Armenians and their neighbours the Chaldaeans always had quarrels and battles; Cyrus reconciled them with each other, and so went away. The army he had received from the Armenian he sent to Cyaxares; and when he came to the Medes he shared the money among his captains.

Then he invaded the enemy country with Cyaxares, and they camped and gathered much booty from there. When they learned that the Assyrians too were approaching, they advanced in battle order, and they camped a parasang apart. The next day Cyrus led out his army and drew it up, Cyaxares with the Medes on one wing, and opposite them the Assyrians and their allies. Urging one another on, the Persians charged at a run to close quarters, and Cyrus himself. The enemy no longer stood, but fled into their fortification. The Persians, following on as they pushed in, cut down many, and leaping in on those who fell into the ditches killed them. The Median horsemen too charged the enemy horsemen, and they gave way; and there was a pursuit of horses and men and slaughter on both sides. When the women of the Assyrians and their allies saw this, they cried out and ran about in terror, tearing their robes and lacerating their cheeks, begging all to defend them, their children and themselves. Then the kings themselves, with their most trusted men, took their stand at the entrances and fought and urged on the others. Cyrus, afraid that his men, being few, would be overcome by many if they forced their way into the entrenchment, ordered them to draw back step by step out of range; and they quickly did as they were told. Then he led his men back as far as seemed good and camped.

The Assyrians, their ruler having been killed and almost all the best men with him, were despondent. Croesus and all their other allies, seeing this, were distressed. So they left the camp and went away by night. When at daybreak the enemy's entrenchment was seen to be deserted, Cyrus took the Persians across first, and then Cyaxares' men crossed too. Cyrus thought they should pursue the enemy, but Cyaxares preferred the very opposite. Cyrus said: "Then give me some of the Medes to march with me, those who are eager to follow me as volunteers." Cyaxares allowed it, and sent with Cyrus a man to announce this to the Medes.

Meanwhile messengers came from the Hyrcanians. The Hyrcanians are neighbours of the Assyrians, a nation not numerous, and so they were subjects of the Assyrians, who used them without sparing them in labours or dangers. Then too, being about a thousand horsemen, they had been posted to bring up the rear, so that if any danger came from behind it might fall on them before the Assyrians. They, considering how they were treated, sent messengers to Cyrus to say that the Assyrians were justly hated by them, and that if he wished to go against them they would be his allies and guides. He asked whether they would overtake them; and the Hyrcanians said that even if they set out lightly equipped at dawn tomorrow they would overtake them. Cyrus asked for pledges that they spoke the truth; they agreed to give hostages, if he too would give his right hand and pledges by the gods. Cyrus gave them pledges that if they made good what they said, he would treat them as friends and faithful men, and they would have no less from him than the Persians or the Medes.

After this, while it was still light, Cyrus led out the army, Tigranes being with him with the Armenians, and he told the Hyrcanians to wait, so that they might go together. Almost all the Medes went out with Cyrus. He ordered the Hyrcanians to lead; and after covering a long way he came near the army of the Hyrcanians. The messengers told Cyrus this, and he ordered that, if they were friends, they should come to meet him as quickly as possible holding up their right hands. The Hyrcanians, hearing this, came at once, holding up their right hands as had been said. Greeting them, he said: "Tell me, how far is it from here to where the enemy's commanders and their main body are?" They said a little more than a parasang. So he ordered the Hyrcanians to lead. They led, and he went on with the Persians holding the centre, and he posted the horsemen on either side. When it was light and the enemy saw what was happening, no one fought, but some fled and others perished without a battle.

## 3.18

Croesus, the king of the Lydians, had sent the women ahead by night in their covered carriages, so that they might travel more easily in the cool, for it was summer, and he followed himself with the horsemen. They say the ruler of Phrygia by the Hellespont did the same. When the fugitives overtook them and they learned what was happening, they too fled. The king of the Cappadocians, who was still near, the Hyrcanians killed. The others pursued, but Cyrus ordered those with him to ride round the camp, to kill those who came out with arms, and he proclaimed to those who stayed that they should bring out their arms bound together and leave their horses at the tents, and that whoever did not do so would lose his head. Those who had arms brought them out and threw them in one place, and those assigned to it burned them.

Cyrus, considering that they had come with neither food nor drink, proclaimed that all the stewards of the tents should come, and when they came he said: "Come, men, prepare in each tent twice as much food as you used to make each day for your masters and their servants." They did as they were ordered. Some of the Medes caught wagons that had set out ahead, turned them back full of what an army needs and drove them in; others brought covered carriages of the noblest women, and showing Cyrus what they were bringing, drove them off again. Cyrus was stung at this and blamed himself, and calling the captains together he advised that the Persians too should acquire cavalry, so that with it they might pursue horsemen and overtake fugitives; and the others agreed.

When it was already past midday, the Median and Hyrcanian horsemen rode in, bringing captive horses and men. To the captive men he said: "Go away, working your land, living in your houses, living with your wives and enjoying your children, and tell this to the others; for one thing will be new for you, that the same man will not rule you as before, but everything else will be the same for you." They bowed down and went away. Cyrus said: "Now it is time, Medes and Armenians, to dine; dinners have been prepared for you, good friends, as best we could. And you, Hyrcanians," he said, "take them to the tents, the officers to the largest and the others as seems best, and dine yourselves; for your tents are safe and untouched." They did as they were told; and Cyrus sent many of the Persians round to guard the camp, so that if anyone approached from outside he would not go unnoticed, and if anyone inside ran away he would be caught. So the Persians passed the night.

The next day he ordered the spoils to be distributed to the soldiers, and the horses given to the Persians, so that they too might be horsemen. "And for Cyaxares," he said, "choose whatever you know will please him; for the Persians with me whatever is left over will be enough. For we have not been brought up in luxury, so you would perhaps laugh at us if you put anything grand on us; just as I know we shall give you much laughter sitting on the horses, and also, I think, falling on the ground." Calling the captains, he ordered them to take the horses, and to cast lots and each take an equal number.

## 3.19

Meanwhile Gobryas came, an old Assyrian, with a mounted retinue, and asked to see Cyrus. He was brought to him and said: "Master, I am an Assyrian by race; I have a strong fortress and rule much land; I also have about a thousand horse, which I used to provide to the king of the Assyrians, and I was his friend above all. But since he has been killed by you, and his son holds the rule, who is my bitterest enemy because out of envy he killed my only, beloved son, I have come to you and give myself to you as slave and ally, and I ask you to become my avenger." Cyrus answered: "If, Gobryas, you mean what you say, I receive you as a suppliant and promise, with the gods' help, to avenge your son's murder for you." He said: "On these terms, speaking the truth, I give you my right hand and take yours." When this had been done, he bade Gobryas go away keeping his arms. He went off.

The Medes came, having chosen for Cyrus the finest tent and the woman of Susa, who is said to have been the most beautiful woman in Asia, and the two best singing girls, and the second choice for Cyaxares; the extra tents they handed over to Cyrus, to be for the Persians. Cyrus ordered Cyaxares' closest friends to keep what was his, and said: "What is given to me I gladly accept." Calling Araspas, a Mede, who had been his companion from boyhood, he ordered him to guard the woman and the tent for him. She was the wife of Abradatas, the king of Susa, who was not present when the camp was taken, having been sent by the Assyrian to the king of the Bactrians about an alliance, being his guest-friend.

Rising early, they set off for Gobryas, Cyrus on horseback and the Persians, now become horsemen, about two thousand. On the second day, towards evening, they and the allies reached Gobryas' place. Learning there that the Assyrians in Babylon had prepared a force many times greater than before, he said: "Lead us straight on the road to Babylon." When on the fourth day of their march they reached the borders of Gobryas' country and were already in enemy territory, he kept the infantry and some of the horsemen with him, and sent the other horsemen out to overrun the country, ordering them to kill those they met who had arms, and to bring the rest to him, and whatever booty they took. He ordered the Persians to ride out with them too. And many of them came back having tumbled off their horses, and many bringing very much booty. Then he advanced on Babylon drawn up as for battle. When the Assyrians did not come out against him, Cyrus ordered Gobryas to ride up and say that if the king wished to come out and fight for his country, he would fight with him; but if he did not defend his country, he must obey the victors. Gobryas rode up and said this; and the Assyrian sent and said: "Your master, Gobryas, answers: if you wish to fight, come on the thirtieth day; now we have no leisure yet, for we are still preparing."

He reported this to Cyrus; and Cyrus led the army away, and asked Gobryas about Gadatas. Gadatas was the son of a king and a companion of the Assyrian, who seized him when he was drinking with him and gelded him and made him a eunuch; his father having died, he then held the kingdom, a eunuch though he was. Cyrus wished Gobryas to go to him and bring him to his side. He went, and having met him and said what Cyrus had told him, he came back; and Gadatas, pretending to fight on the Assyrian's side, seized by trickery a fort of the Assyrian that was an outpost of the war, handed it over to Cyrus and came to Cyrus himself. Greeted, he heard: "Gadatas, the Assyrian, it seems, has robbed you of begetting children, but has not deprived you of the power of gaining friends." Such were his words.

When the fort had come under Cyrus, the Cadusians, the Sacae and more Hyrcanians also joined his campaign. After this Gadatas came to Cyrus saying that the Assyrian, having learned about the fort, was angry and preparing to invade his country. "If you let me go, I will try to save the walls; for the rest I care less." Cyrus asked: "If you go, when will you be home?" He said: "On the third day I shall dine in my own land." "And I," said Cyrus, "on what day would I reach there with the army?" Gadatas answered: "You could not get there, master, except in six or seven days." Cyrus said: "Then you go as quickly as you can, and I will travel as fast as I can." Gadatas went off, and Cyrus told the commanders of the allies that they must help Gadatas, a benefactor, who was being attacked by the Assyrian. All agreed. He said: "Since you agree, come, let us go, taking the strongest of the horses and men and three days' provisions, so that we may travel light." Making ready in this way, they took the road at midnight, with Gobryas guiding them.

## 3.20

So Cyrus set off. As Gadatas went, one of the powerful men with him plotted against him, and sending to the Assyrian he revealed Gadatas' force and that he was coming, and told him to lay an ambush, so that he might seize Gadatas and those with him. When the Assyrian heard this, he laid an ambush. When Gadatas came near the ambush and seemed already to be easily captured, the Assyrian rose from it. Then the man plotting against Gadatas struck him and wounded him in the shoulder, but not mortally; having done this he went over to the Assyrian at once and joined him in the pursuit. Some of Gadatas' men were captured, and others fled, turning their horses; and then they saw Cyrus coming. When he understood what had happened, he led the army against them. The enemy, seeing him, turned to flight, and Cyrus ordered pursuit. Men and chariots were captured, and many were killed, among them the man who had struck Gadatas; the rest, and the Assyrian himself, fled into one of his great cities. Having done this, Cyrus withdrew into Gadatas' country and went to visit Gadatas. Gadatas met him with his wound bound up and thanked him, saying: "So far as it lay with me I was lost, but through you I have been saved, since you came so eagerly to my aid."

The Cadusians, who had been posted by Cyrus to guard the rear and had not shared in the pursuit, wishing to do something splendid themselves too, overran Babylonia without telling Cyrus anything. The Assyrian, leaving the city where he had taken refuge, came on them; seeing they were alone he attacked them, killed their commander and many others, and after pursuing them some distance turned back. Those of the Cadusians who were saved came into the camp towards evening. Cyrus handed over the wounded to those who would care for them, and comforted the rest with words himself, and urged them to choose a commander from among themselves; and they chose one.

Gadatas came bringing Cyrus gifts of every kind and in great number, and very many horses. Cyrus said he accepted the horses, so that he might complete the Persian cavalry and his horsemen might come to ten thousand; but he told him to take the money away and keep it until he saw that Cyrus too was able to give gifts in return. He urged him to put his city under safe guard and march with him himself. Gadatas was pleased with the advice, made ready and followed Cyrus. When, marching in this way, he reached the borders of the Syrians and Medes, there were three forts of the Syrians there; one Cyrus attacked himself and took by force, and the other two, Cyrus by frightening and Gadatas by persuading, brought the garrisons to surrender.

Then he sent to Cyaxares asking him to come to the camp, so that they might consult about what they needed. The messenger went off; and Cyrus ordered the tent which the Medes had chosen for Cyaxares to be furnished as well as possible with everything, and the woman to be brought into the women's quarters of the tent, and with her the singing girls who had been chosen for Cyaxares. Meanwhile the messenger reported Cyrus's words to Cyaxares, and he set out with the Median horsemen who had stayed with him. When Cyrus learned that he was approaching, he took the Persian horsemen, who were now many, the Medes who were present, the Armenians and the best mounted of the other allies, and went to meet him, showing Cyaxares the force. Cyaxares, seeing many good men following Cyrus, and with himself a small retinue of little worth, thought it a dishonour to himself; grief seized him, and he broke into open tears. Cyrus took him aside, sat by him and talked with him, and asked the cause of his anger and grief. After much was said and heard, he said at last: "By the gods, uncle, stop blaming me for now; and when you have made trial of how we are disposed towards you, then either thank me or blame me." Cyaxares agreed, and Cyrus went up and kissed him. When the Medes, the Persians and the rest saw this, they were overjoyed.

Mounting their horses, Cyrus and Cyaxares went to the camp, and they took Cyaxares to the tent chosen for him. The Medes went to him bringing gifts, each one the finest thing he had taken. When it was time for dinner, Cyaxares was busy with dinner, but Cyrus was taking thought how the allies might stay with him; gathering those of his friends able to help him in what was needed, he asked them to contrive how they might persuade the allies not to leave them, but to go on helping. The next day all gathered before Cyaxares; and before they met him, Cyrus's friends brought to him one after another the various allied nations, who begged him to stay and to let them share the war with him.

## 3.21

Meanwhile Cyaxares came out of the tent and said: "Allies, we must consider whether it seems the time still to campaign, or to disband the army now." Each came forward and spoke, and all gave their opinion that they must go on campaigning. After all of them Cyrus said: "I too am aware, men, that if we disband the army our side would become weaker and the enemy's would grow. But I see opponents coming against us whom we shall not be able to fight: winter is coming, and the lack of provisions. For some have been used up by us, and the rest, because of us, have been carried off by the enemy into strongholds. Who could keep campaigning, fighting hunger and cold? I think we must take the enemy's strongholds from them and make as many as possible for ourselves. For if we have forts, they will make the country foreign to the enemy and be to our advantage." When this and much more of the kind had been said, all said they would eagerly support it, Cyaxares himself among them, and they agreed to make siege engines.

Cyrus, knowing that there would be delay over this, put the army in safety, and led it out to forage, so that they might have plenty of what they needed, be healthier through exertion and be kept mindful of good order. Deserters coming from Babylon said that the Assyrian had gone off to Lydia taking many talents of gold and silver and all kinds of ornaments. The mass of soldiers said that he was already afraid and was putting his money out of the way; but Cyrus said the Assyrian had gone looking for allies, and he prepared himself in turn as though a battle would still be needed. He also decided to send a spy to Lydia to learn what the Assyrian was doing. For this Araspas seemed suitable to him, the man who guarded the beautiful woman. Seized with love for the woman, he had made advances to her; she refused, and was faithful to her husband even though he was away, yet she did not denounce Araspas to Cyrus. But when the lover threatened her that if she would not yield willingly she would do so unwillingly, she was afraid of violence, sent the eunuch to Cyrus and bade him tell everything. Hearing it, he laughed, and sent Artabazus with the eunuch, ordering him to tell Araspas not to use force on such a woman, but if he could persuade her, he said he would not prevent it. When Araspas heard this, he was overwhelmed with grief, partly from shame and partly from fear.

Cyrus, knowing this, called him privately and said: "Stop being afraid and ashamed, Araspas. For I hear that gods are overcome by love, and I know what men, even very sensible ones, have suffered from love. And I am to blame for this, who shut you up with so unconquerable a thing." Araspas answered: "You, Cyrus, are gentle in this too and forgiving of human failings; but other men drown me in grief. My enemies gloat over me, and my friends advise me to get out of the way, lest I suffer something at your hands." Cyrus said: "Know well, then, that with this reputation you will be able to do me a favour and help the allies." "I wish I might be useful to you at the right moment," said Araspas. "If, then," he said, "you were to pretend to flee from me and go to the enemy, you would be trusted by them." "Yes, by Zeus," said Araspas, "and by my friends too." "Then you would come back," he said, "telling us everything about the enemy."

So Araspas went out in this way, took his most trusted attendants and went off. Panthea, learning this, sent to Cyrus and said: "Do not be grieved, Cyrus, that Araspas has gone over to the enemy; for if you let me send to my husband, a friend more faithful than Araspas will come to you." Cyrus told her to send, and she sent. When Abradatas recognized the tokens from his wife, he gladly made his way to Cyrus. When he was at the outposts, he sent to Cyrus saying who he was. Cyrus ordered him to be taken to his wife. When the woman and Abradatas saw each other, they embraced each other after all but losing hope. Then Panthea told of Cyrus's piety and self-control and his compassion for her. After this Abradatas went to Cyrus, took his right hand and said: "In return for the kindness you have done us, Cyrus, I give myself to you as friend, servant and ally." Cyrus said: "And I receive you."

## 3.22

Money came to Cyrus from the Indian, and those who brought it reported to him that the Indian said: "I am glad, Cyrus, that you told me what you needed; I wish to be your guest-friend, and I send you money; and if you need more, send for it. Those from me have been told to do whatever you command." Cyrus said: "Then I command the others to stay where you have camped and guard the money; but three of you go to the enemy as if from the Indian about an alliance, learn what is there, whatever they say and do, and report it as quickly as possible to me and to the Indian." The Indians made ready and set off the next day, and Cyrus prepared for the war on a grand scale. The Persian horsemen were now fully ten thousand, and there were about a hundred scythed chariots; Abradatas built another hundred, and the Median chariots, another hundred, were refitted in the same way from the Trojan and Libyan style of chariot; and on the camels two archers each had been posted.

While Cyrus was arranging matters for the war in this way, the Indians came back from the enemy and said that Croesus had been chosen leader and general, and that many kings and many nations, and Greeks, had made ready to fight on their side. When Cyrus's army heard this, it was anxious. When Cyrus noticed fear running through the army, he spoke to them and changed them to better spirits. Then he said: "I think, men, we should go against them as quickly as possible." And all agreed. Cyaxares stayed behind with a third of the Medes, so that their home country might not be left unguarded, and Cyrus marched as quickly as he could.

When the scouts going ahead seemed to see smoke or dust rising and men gathering fodder and wood, they guessed that the enemy's army was somewhere near, and reported it to Cyrus. He ordered them to stay at their lookouts and report whatever they saw, and sent a squadron of horsemen ahead to capture some men. They rode down into the plain, caught some men and brought them in. The captives said they were from the camp. Cyrus said: "How far is the army from here?" They said two parasangs. He asked further: "Was there any talk of us among them?" "Yes, by Zeus," they said, "and much of it, that you were already near and coming on." "Well then," said Cyrus, "were they glad to hear we were coming?" "No, by Zeus," they said, "they were very much upset." "And who is the one marshalling them?" Cyrus said. They answered: "Croesus himself, and a Greek, and a Mede, who was said to be a deserter from you." To this Cyrus said: "O Zeus, may I take him as I wish." He ordered the captives to be led away.

Not long after, the spy sent long before, Araspas, was reported riding in. When Cyrus heard, he leapt up, went to meet him and greeted him, and the others were astonished at it, until Cyrus said: "Friends, a most excellent man has come to us, who did not go away because he was overcome by something shameful or in fear of me, but sent by me, to learn the enemy's affairs and report them to us clearly. So all must honour him; for he endured disgrace and risked danger for our good." Then they all embraced Araspas. Cyrus said: "Tell us, Araspas, all you have seen." He told of the number of the enemy, the place of their battle line and the enemy's plans. Cyrus ordered each to go away and look over his arms and horses, and at dawn be ready for battle.

So then they went away; and the next day each armed himself, and Abradatas, who had drawn by lot to be stationed opposite the Egyptians. As he was arming, Panthea brought him a golden helmet, armlets and bracelets for his wrists, a purple tunic reaching to the feet and a crest dyed the colour of hyacinth, which she had made for her husband. Seeing them, he said: "Surely, wife, you have not broken up your own ornaments and made me the arms?" "Yes, by Zeus," said Panthea, "for you are my greatest ornament." With these words she put the arms on her husband, and tried to hide that she was weeping, but the tears ran down her cheeks. When Abradatas was ready to mount the chariot, Panthea ordered those present to withdraw and said: "That I value you, Abradatas, more than my own life, I think you know. Yet, feeling so towards you, I swear by my love and yours that I would rather go under the earth with you, having proved a good man, than live in shame with a man disgraced. And I think we owe Cyrus great gratitude, because when I was a captive and chosen for him, he did not use me as a slave nor as a free woman with a dishonourable name, but kept me for you, taking me as though I were his brother's wife. Besides, when Araspas, who guarded me, left him, I promised him that if he let me send to you, you would come and prove far more faithful and better than Araspas." So she spoke; and Abradatas, delighted with her words, prayed: "Grant me, O Zeus, to show myself a husband worthy of Panthea and a friend worthy of Cyrus." With these words he mounted the chariot.

## 3.23

Cyrus called the commanders together and addressed them, encouraging them for the battle, and so set out. When they came near the enemy, so that they could see each other, Cyrus drew up his men as well as possible, and going along the ranks he spurred the men on to battle with words. Croesus set his army facing Cyrus's army and signalled them to advance against the enemy. Three phalanxes came on, one against the front, and of the other two one against the right and one against the left, so that fear came on the whole of Cyrus's army; for it was surrounded by the enemy on every side except the rear. Nevertheless all Cyrus's men, when he gave the order, turned to face the enemy. For a while there was deep silence everywhere; but when it seemed to Cyrus the right moment, he began the paean, and the whole army joined in. Cyrus rose up and engaged the enemy with the horsemen, and the infantry, drawn up with him, followed. Artagerses, the commander of the camel corps, attacked on the left, sending the camels forward, as Cyrus had ordered. The horses would not face them even from a distance, but some fled, some reared, and others fell against one another. The chariots too charged on the right and left at the same time, and many fleeing from them were caught by them.

Abradatas, shouting, "Friends, follow!", drove in without sparing his horses; and the other charioteers set off with him. Abradatas charged into the phalanx of the Egyptians, and those posted nearest charged in with him, but the others turned aside after the fleeing chariots. Abradatas' men overturned some with the rush of their horses and crushed those who fell; whatever the scythes caught, arms and bodies alike, was cut through by force. In this indescribable confusion, as the wheels leapt over heaps of every kind, Abradatas was thrown out, and others of those who had charged in with him. They were cut down; but the Persians, following up, killed the Egyptians who were thrown into disorder, while those who held together advanced against the Persians, and there was a terrible battle. The Egyptians had the advantage both in numbers and in arms. The Persians could not hold out, but drew back step by step, striking and being struck, until they were under the engines. When they came there, the Egyptians in turn were struck from the towers. There was great slaughter of men, great clash of arms and great shouting.

Meanwhile Cyrus, pursuing those opposite him, arrived. Seeing the Persians being pushed from their position, he was grieved, and ordering those with him to follow, he rode round to the rear; and falling on them they killed many. When the Egyptians saw this, they turned about. Foot and horse fought in confusion; and a man who had fallen under Cyrus's horse and was being trampled struck the horse in the belly with a sword, and the horse, writhing from the blow, threw Cyrus off. At once all shouted and fell on and fought. One of Cyrus's attendants leapt down and put him on his own horse. When he had mounted, he saw the Egyptians already being struck on all sides. Riding along by the engines, he climbed one of the towers and saw the plain full of horses, men and chariots, fleeing, pursuing, conquering, conquered; and he could see nothing standing firm anywhere any longer except the Egyptians. These, surrounded on all sides, sat under their shields, no longer doing anything, but suffering much and terribly. Cyrus admired them and pitied them because, being brave men, they were perishing; he let no one fight them any longer, and sent a herald to them asking whether they wished all to perish or to be saved. They said: "How could we be saved and still act honourably?" Cyrus said: "If you hand over your arms to us, who choose to save you when we could destroy you." Hearing this, the Egyptians gave pledges and received them.

Having done this, Cyrus led his men back, when it was already dark, and camped. Cyrus's men dined and slept; but Croesus fled at once to Sardis with his army, and the other peoples went away in the night wherever they could. At dawn Cyrus too led on to Sardis, and when he reached the city he set up his engines and prepared ladders. The next night he sent Chaldaeans and Persians up the parts of the Sardian fortress that seemed most precipitous. A Persian, the slave of one of the guards on the acropolis, guided them on the way. When the heights were taken, the Lydians fled from the walls. At daybreak Cyrus entered the city, ordering that no one move from his post. Croesus, shut up in the palace, called out for Cyrus. Cyrus left guards over Croesus, and when he had camped his men he went to Croesus. Seeing him, Croesus said: "Greetings, master." "And to you, Croesus," said Cyrus. "But would you be willing to give me some advice?" "I would, Cyrus," he said. "Then," he said, "Croesus, seeing that the soldiers have laboured and run very many risks, and now think they have a city that is the richest in Asia after Babylon, I think the soldiers should be rewarded; but I do not wish to let them plunder the city." Croesus said: "Then let me tell whichever Lydians I wish that I have arranged with you that there shall be no plundering, and know that from the Lydians, willingly, you will have everything of value in Sardis. But first," he said, "send to my treasures and take as much as you wish."

Cyrus agreed to do so, and they spent that day on these matters. The next day Cyrus called some of his friends and ordered some to take over the treasures, and others whatever money Croesus handed over. Then he asked whether anyone had seen Abradatas; and when one of the attendants said he had died in the battle, he struck his thigh, leapt onto his horse and rode to him. When he saw Panthea sitting on the ground and the body lying there, he wept and said: "Alas, good and faithful soul." The woman said: "I know that he suffered this because of me, and perhaps, Cyrus, because of you too. For I, fool that I was, urged him much to show himself a friend to you worth speaking of, and he himself was thinking what he could do to please you. He has died blameless, and I who urged him sit here alive." Cyrus wept in silence for some time, then said much to console her in her grief, and then went away. The woman ordered the eunuchs to withdraw, and told her nurse to stay and, when she was dead, to cover her and her husband with one cloak. The nurse sat weeping; and she cut her own throat, and laying her head on her husband's breast she died. The nurse wailed and covered them both. The eunuchs, three in number, learning what had happened, drew their short swords and cut their own throats. When Cyrus learned of the woman's deed, he admired her and lamented, and he heaped up a very great mound for them, burying them magnificently.

## 3.24

The Carians, who were in civil strife and at war with one another, both sides gave themselves up to Cyrus; and those of Phrygia on the Hellespont gave Cyrus very many gifts, so as not to receive barbarians into their walls, but to pay him tribute and serve in war wherever Cyrus ordered. The king of the Phrygians prepared as if he would not make terms; but when his officers deserted him, he came into the hands of Hystaspas. Hystaspas, leaving garrisons of Persians on the heights, went off taking with his own men many Phrygian horsemen and targeteers.

Cyrus set out from Sardis, leaving a garrison in Sardis and having with him Croesus, who brought very many wagons full of much wealth of every kind, and had a written list of exactly what was in each wagon. He gave Cyrus the lists, and Cyrus said: "You have done well, Croesus; but those who have received the wealth will surely bring it to me, and if they steal anything, they will be stealing their own." Croesus also brought many Lydians, splendidly armed. Going on the road to Babylon, Cyrus subdued the Phrygians of Greater Phrygia, subdued the Cappadocians and made the Arabians subject; and from all these he equipped no fewer than forty thousand Persian horsemen, and distributed many of the captured horses to all the allies. He came to Babylon bringing many horsemen, many archers and javelin men, and slingers without number.

When he was at Babylon, he set the army round it, and going round himself with the commanders of the allies and his friends he viewed the walls. Then he brought up the army and camped round the city. Seeing the river there flowing into the city and passing through the middle of Babylon, he revealed his plan to no one, but ordered a ditch to be dug round the camp, broad and very deep, so that perhaps they would need very few guards in the camp. Those digging the ditch dug it a moderate distance from the river, and threw the earth up towards the camp. The Babylonians laughed at the siege, since they had provisions for more than twenty years.

When the ditch had been dug, Cyrus waited for a night on which he knew the Babylonians would keep a festival, drinking and revelling the whole night, and on it he gathered a crowd and opened the ditches to the river (this is the Euphrates, as Herodotus records); the water went into the ditches in the night, and the river's course through the city became passable for men, the water having been diverted. Then he sent attendants, foot and horse, down into the dry bed of the river and ordered them to see whether it was passable. Meanwhile he spoke with the leaders of the host and roused them to the deed, and said at last: "Come, take your arms, and I will lead; and you, Gadatas and Gobryas, show the roads, since you know them. When we are inside, lead the quickest way to the palace." On this they set off. Going as fast as they could, those with Gobryas and Gadatas reached the palace, found the gates shut, and fell on the guards as they drank. When a shout and uproar arose, those within noticed it, and when the king ordered them to see what the matter was, they opened the gates; the enemy burst in through them and reached the king. They found him already on his feet, with the short sword he had drawn. Those with Gadatas and Gobryas overpowered him, and those with him died, some defending themselves and some giving way. Cyrus sent the squadrons of horsemen along the streets, telling them beforehand to kill those they caught outside, and to proclaim to those in the houses who knew Syrian that they should stay indoors, and that anyone caught outside would die. Meanwhile Gadatas and Gobryas came, and because they had taken vengeance on the unholy king, they first bowed down to the gods and then kissed Cyrus's hands and feet.

Xenophon, telling the story of Cyrus, does not give the name of the king of the Assyrians who was captured; but Josephus, in the tenth book of the Antiquities, records that he was this Belshazzar, to whom the hand appeared coming out of the wall and writing the letters already described, whose meaning Daniel interpreted.

## 3.25

When day came and those holding the citadels learned that the city had been taken and their king killed, they surrendered the citadels too. Cyrus sent commandants and garrisons into them. Then he called the magi, and, since the city had been taken by the spear, ordered them to choose first-fruits for the gods; he distributed houses and public buildings to those who had shared in his deeds, and he decided to arrange for himself a way of life fitting for a king. So as not to be envied by his friends, he wished to do this with their consent, and speaking with them he persuaded them by what he said to ask him to do it. After that he went into the palace, set guards about himself, gelded the servants who attended his person and made them eunuchs, chose about ten thousand Persians as spearmen, and set sufficient garrisons in the city. Having done this, he trained the more honoured men of the army not to live without toil and in luxury. He set different overseers over different matters, I mean receivers of revenues and payers of expenses, overseers of works and keepers of property, stewards of provisions for daily life and keepers of horses and dogs; but those whom he thought must be kept as guardians of his good fortune, he took the care of himself.

When he came to have the means of doing good with money too, he decided that no kindness to men is more gratifying than sharing food and drink. So he ordered many dishes to be set before him like those he ate himself, enough for very many people, and what was set before him, except what he and his fellow diners used, he distributed. And he became the most generous of men. To surpass others in the size of gifts when one is very rich is not remarkable; but to excel, while being king, in attention to and care for one's friends is more noteworthy. A saying of his is recorded, that the work of a good herdsman and of a king are much alike: for he said that the herdsman must make his flocks thrive and then use them, and the king likewise must make cities and men prosperous and then use them.

It is said that once, when Croesus told him that he would be poor because he gave so much, Cyrus asked him: "How much money do you think I would have by now, if I had been collecting gold as you bid me since I came to power?" Croesus named some large sum. Cyrus said: "Come then, Croesus, send a man with Hystaspas here. And you, Hystaspas, go round my friends and tell them that I need gold for some undertaking, and bid them write down how much money each could provide me, seal it, and give the letters to Croesus's servant." He wrote all he had ordered Hystaspas, sealed it and gave it to him to take to his friends. He also wrote to all of them to receive Hystaspas as his friend. When he had gone round and Croesus's servant brought the letters, Hystaspas said: "King Cyrus, treat me too as a rich man now; for I have come with a great many gifts because of your letters." Cyrus said: "Here, Croesus, is one treasure for us; look at the others, and reckon how much money I have ready if I need to use any." It is said that when Croesus reckoned it up, he found it many times more than he had said Cyrus would have in his treasuries if he had been collecting; and at this Cyrus said: "Do you see, Croesus, that I too have treasuries?"

When he decided to hold a procession to the precincts set apart for the gods, he called those holding office among the Persians and the others and gave them Median robes; and then for the first time the Persians put on Median dress. When he had given the best robes to the best men, he brought out other Median robes too (for he had prepared a great many), sparing neither purple nor dark nor scarlet nor crimson garments; giving each of the commanders a share of them, he ordered them to adorn those under them with these. One of those present asked him: "And you, Cyrus, when will you adorn yourself?" He said: "Do I not seem to you to be adorning myself now, by adorning you? For if I can do good to my friends, in whatever robe I have I shall look fine." Having held the procession and sacrificed to the gods, he dined with his friends, seating those he honoured most on his left hand, as being more exposed to plots than the right, and the others on the right.

After dinner he sent home those who had fought on his side as volunteers, except those who wished to stay with him; to these he gave land and houses. To those leaving, both soldiers and officers, he gave many gifts. Then he distributed to the soldiers and commanders about him the money he had taken from Sardis, to each according to his worth. When they had received what was given, they said of Cyrus: "Surely he must have a great deal himself, since he has given each of us so much." Others said: "It is not Cyrus's way to make money; he takes pleasure in giving rather than getting." When Cyrus heard of this, he gathered his friends and all the important men and said: "It seems to me the simplest course for a ruler to make his power plain and from it to compete in nobility. So I wish to show you all of mine that can be seen, and tell you of what cannot be seen." With these words he showed them much fine wealth, and told them of what was stored so as not to be easy to see. And he said: "Men, you must think these no more mine than yours. For I gather them so that I may have something to give to any of you who does something fine, and so that, if any of you needs anything, he may come to me and take it."

## 3.26

When he had settled affairs in Babylon well, he prepared to go away to the Persians. When on the journey he came into Media, Cyrus turned aside to Cyaxares, and greeting him told him that a house had been set apart for him in Babylon, and gave him many fine gifts. Cyaxares sent his daughter to him bearing a golden crown, bracelets, a necklace and a very beautiful Median robe. The girl crowned Cyrus, and Cyaxares said: "I give you, Cyrus, this girl herself as wife, being my daughter, and with her I give all Media as dowry; for I have no lawful male child." Cyrus said: "I praise the family, Cyaxares, and the girl and the gifts; but I wish to marry her with the consent of my father and mother." With these words he went on to the Persians. When he came to the borders of the Persians he left the army there, and went into the city with his friends, bringing victims enough for all the Persians to sacrifice and feast. He brought his father and mother such gifts as were fitting, and to his friends such as befitted officials and elders; and he gave to all the Persians, men and women too. After spending some time with his parents he went away. Coming into Media, with his father's consent he married the daughter of Cyaxares; and having married her he set off at once with her.

When he came to Babylon, he sent satraps to the subdued nations: to Arabia, Cappadocia, Greater Phrygia, Lycia and Ionia, Caria, Phrygia on the Hellespont and Aeolis. To the Cilicians, the Cypriots and the Paphlagonians he did not send satraps, because they seemed to campaign with him willingly; but they too paid tribute. So that, his empire being great, news might be brought to him quickly even from far away, he worked out how far a horse ridden hard could go, and built posting stations that distance apart, placed horses in them and men to care for them, and set over each a suitable man to receive the letters brought, pass them on, take in the tired horses and send others fresh.

When a year had passed he made a campaign, in which he is said to have subdued all the nations that live from the entry into Syria to the Red Sea. After this he marched against Egypt and subdued it too; so that from then on his empire was bounded on the east by the Red Sea, on the north by the Euxine Sea, on the west by Cyprus and Egypt, and on the south by Ethiopia. He made his home in the middle of these: he spent seven months, the winter season, in Babylon, for that land is warm; three months, those of spring, at Susa; and two months, the height of summer, at Ecbatana.

Having lived so and grown very old, Cyrus came to the Persians for the seventh time, his parents having long since died. As he slept in the palace, someone greater than a man seemed to come to him in a dream, saying: "Make ready, Cyrus; for the gods are already sending for you." From this he guessed that the end of his life was at hand, and on the third day he called his sons, for they had accompanied him, and his friends and the Persian officials, and said: "The end of my life is now at hand, and I must make clear about the kingdom who shall have it after me, so that it may not cause you trouble by being disputed. You, Cambyses, being the elder, have the kingdom, by the gift of the gods and of me; and to you, Tanaoxares, I give to be satrap of the Medes, the Armenians and the Cadusians." Having given these charges to his sons and much advice, and having addressed those present and taken all by the right hand, he covered himself and so died. When he had gone, at once his sons quarrelled, cities and nations revolted, and everything turned for the worse.

These are the things Xenophon has recorded about Cyrus. But Herodotus of Halicarnassus says other things about Cyrus's upbringing, his death and the rest of his life, which it would be long to tell. Since I have made an epitome of history, it does not suit me to make the work run to many lines; I have written what is more plausible, and whoever wishes to know what Herodotus wrote about him too will find it by taking up his book, in the first book, which he titled with the first of the Muses, Clio.

## 4.1

So Cyrus ended the rule of the Assyrians. In the first year of his reign, which was the seventieth since the Hebrews were carried from Jerusalem to Babylon, he allowed the Jews in Babylon to go to Jerusalem and rebuild the city and the temple, as the prophet Jeremiah had proclaimed, and Isaiah before him had foretold. For Jeremiah lived when the city was taken and the temple razed, while Isaiah lived a hundred and forty years before the city was taken and the sanctuary razed. He not only granted the captives their return and the raising of the city and temple, but also sent with them the vessels of the temple which Nebuchadnezzar had brought to Babylon, handing them over to be carried by Mithridates, his treasurer, and Zerubbabel. He also wrote to the satraps in Syria to help them in building the works and to supply all the expense from the royal funds. When Cyrus had decreed this, the leaders of the two tribes, Judah and Benjamin, set out, with the Levites and the priests; but many stayed in Babylon, not choosing to leave their possessions. When they reached Jerusalem, all Cyrus's men worked with them on the buildings and helped them. Those who returned were forty-two thousand six hundred.

While they were hurrying on with the building of the city and the temple, the surrounding nations, and above all that of the Cuthaeans, whom Shalmaneser had moved to Samaria when he carried the Israelites captive, out of envy first won over the satraps not to help the Jews with the building. Then, when Cyrus had ended his life and his son Cambyses succeeded to the rule, they wrote to Cambyses slandering the Hebrews as a rebellious and insubordinate nation, saying, "If they complete the city and the temple, they will neither pay tribute nor obey." By writing this and much more of the kind they persuaded him to stop the rebuilding of the city and the temple; and the works were held up for nine years, until the second year of the reign of Darius the son of Hystaspes.

For Cambyses reigned seven years, subdued Egypt in that time, and on his way back died at Damascus, when the magi had gained control of the kingdom. It is well to tell their story in brief too. Cambyses had a brother called, according to Xenophon, Tanaoxares, and according to Herodotus, Smerdis. When Cambyses was in Egypt, someone seemed to stand over him in his sleep and say that Smerdis, sitting on the royal throne, touched the sky with his head. Afraid for his kingdom, he at once sent Prexaspes to Susa to kill his brother secretly. So he was destroyed; but Cambyses did not escape his fate. For there were two brothers, Medes, called magi, whose names were Patizeithes and Smerdis. This Smerdis shared not only the name of Smerdis the son of Cyrus but also his looks. Cambyses had one of them as steward of his affairs. While he stayed long in Egypt, Patizeithes, learning of the murder of Smerdis the son of Cyrus, and that his death was known to few, planned to seize the kingdom; and he set his brother, who had the same name as Smerdis and looked very much like him, on the royal throne, not as his own brother but as the son of Cyrus and the brother of Cambyses; and he sent heralds everywhere announcing that Smerdis the son of Cyrus was king.

When Cambyses learned this too, he questioned Prexaspes for not having carried out his order. He insisted that it was not his brother who had risen against him. "For I," he said, "killed and buried him, as you commanded." So Cambyses ordered the man who was proclaiming in the army that Smerdis the son of Cyrus was king of the Persians to be seized. Seized, he was asked whether he had seen Smerdis himself, and whether Smerdis had ordered him to say what he was saying; he answered that he had not seen Smerdis, but had been ordered to say this by the magus who was steward of the palace. Learning the truth from this, Cambyses lamented that he had killed his brother for nothing, and at once set out to march against the magi. But as he leapt onto his horse, the cap of his sword's scabbard fell off, and the bared sword struck his thigh with a mortal wound. He asked what the town was called, and learning that it was Ecbatana, he said: "Here I am fated to die." For it had been foretold him that he would die at Ecbatana; he had thought before that the end would take him at Ecbatana in Media, but the oracle meant not that one, but Ecbatana in Syria. So after some days he died childless,

## 4.2

having reigned seven years and five months; and the magus, now that Cambyses was dead, reigned without fear, calling himself Smerdis the son of Cyrus. Sending to every nation he ruled, he proclaimed freedom from taxes for all for three years. When he had ruled seven months, it was discovered who he was; it was discovered in this way. Otanes was equal to the first of the Persians in birth and wealth. Cambyses had had a daughter of his, called Phaedymia. The magus too had her, as he had the others he found in the palace. Her father sent and asked her secretly whether she was sleeping with Smerdis the son of Cyrus or with someone else. She answered that she had never seen Smerdis the son of Cyrus and did not know whom she was sleeping with. He sent to her again, bidding her run the risk if need be, to fulfil her father's command, and advising her, when she was lying with the king and knew him to be asleep, to feel his ears; and if she found he had ears, to know that she was with Smerdis the son of Cyrus; but if he had none, to take him for Smerdis the magus. Phaedymia obeyed her father, and finding that the man who lay with her had no ears, she let her father know. He had no ears because Cyrus had once cut them off for some offence.

Otanes shared the secret with Aspathines and Gobryas, leading Persians and his friends. They, who had suspected the matter before, at once accepted his words. They decided to take others as companions too. Otanes brought in Intaphernes, Gobryas Megabyzus, and Aspathines Hydarnes; and they shared the secret with Darius the son of Hystaspes, who had just come to Susa from Persis. He accepted the proposal, and advised them to set about the deed at once; or, if the present day went by in vain, he said he would himself become the informer of the plot to the magus. The rest were persuaded by Darius's advice and set to the work.

Something else happened too which spurred them to hasten the attempt. The magi, knowing that Prexaspes had carried out the murder of Smerdis the son of Cyrus, and that he was blamed by the Persians for it, called him and made him their friend, and bound him beforehand with oaths and splendid promises not to reveal the trick to anyone. When he agreed to do so, they went on to say that they would call the people together below the wall of the palace, and he should go up on a tower and cry out in the hearing of all that they were ruled by Smerdis the son of Cyrus and by no one else; for he had spared him and not killed him as Cambyses had ordered. When he agreed to do this too, the people were gathered, and Prexaspes went up on the tower and spoke of all the good the Persians had had from Cyrus, and added that he himself had killed his son Smerdis, under compulsion from Cambyses. He assured them that the magi held the kingdom, and called curses down on the Persians if they did not punish them. With these words he threw himself down and died.

The seven men, learning on the way what had happened with Prexaspes, made at once for the palace and went in, not stopped by the guards; for they respected the men's eminence. Entering the courtyard, they met the eunuchs who carried messages, who asked what they had come for and tried to stop them going further. They killed them there, and themselves ran in. The magi, who were inside and heard the uproar of the eunuchs, turned to defend themselves, one taking up a spear and the other a bow. One struck Aspathines in the thigh with the spear and Intaphernes in the face, so that he lost an eye; but the bow was useless as they were at close quarters. The one who held it ran into a bedchamber, and Darius and Gobryas burst in with him. Gobryas grappled with the magus, but Darius, at a loss because of the darkness and afraid of striking his companion, stood doing nothing. Gobryas asked why he stood idle; he told him the reason. And Gobryas said: "Thrust the sword through both of us." Darius thrust in the dagger and hit the magus. When the others had killed the second too, they cut off the heads of both; they left the two who were wounded in the citadel, because of their wounds and to guard the citadel, and the rest ran out carrying the heads of the magi, showed them to the Persians and explained what had happened. They, learning of the deceit, killed whatever magi they met.

## 4.3

When the uproar had died down and some days had passed, they took counsel about the government of affairs. Otanes urged them to choose democracy, Megabyzus aristocracy, but Darius advised them to choose kingship again. The four others agreed with his opinion; and they decided to mount their horses, and that the one whose horse neighed first after sunrise, when they were in the outskirts, should have the kingdom. Darius told the plan to his groom. The man was clever, and said: "Take heart, master; the kingdom will be yours." When night came, he took a mare which Darius's horse loved, tied her in the outskirts, brought the horse there, led him round close to the mare, and at last let him mount her. At dawn, when the seven came to the outskirts and rode through them, as they came to the place where the mare had been tied, Darius's horse, remembering her, went forward and neighed; and from this the kingdom came to Darius. Some say that Oebares (for this was the name of Darius's groom) contrived this; others say his contrivance was different: that he touched the mare that Darius's horse loved on her privates with his hand, and kept the hand hidden in his trousers; and when the sun was rising and the seven men were mounting their horses, he put his hand to the nostrils of Darius's horse, and it at once caught the scent, snorted and neighed. Whether so or the other way, Darius gained the kingdom.

While still a private man, he had vowed to God that if he became king he would send all the vessels of God that were still in Babylon to the temple in Jerusalem.
