He had long had a friendship with Zerubbabel, because of which he had been thought worthy to be one of his bodyguards, with two others, having lately come to him from Jerusalem. In the first year of his reign Darius prepared a drinking party for his nobles; when they had feasted and broken up, he too went to sleep in his bed. After sleeping a little he woke and talked with his bodyguards. He set each of them a contest of argument: the first, whether wine is strongest of all; the second, whether kings are; and the last, who was Zerubbabel, whether women are, and truth stronger than they; and he promised to give the winner as a prize to wear purple, a turban of fine linen and a gold collar, to drink from golden cups, sleep on gold, have a chariot with golden bridles, sit next after him and be called his kinsman.

At daybreak he sent for the nobles and ordered each of the bodyguards to give his views on the question. The first extolled the strength of wine, since it deceives the minds of those who indulge in it, alters the dispositions of their souls and makes men drunk with wine different people from what they were. The second argued that the king is stronger than all, since men rule all other things by wisdom, and kings are masters of these men to whom everything is subject. But Zerubbabel declared that women surpass in strength, since both kings and those who plant vineyards are born of them, and everyone values them above parents, homelands and their dearest; and women rule even kings, who flatter them and humble themselves if they see them displeased in anything; but truth is more powerful than these, since God too is true and is so called, and other things are mortal, but truth is something immortal and everlasting.

When all agreed with his opinion, that he had argued best about truth, Darius allowed him to ask whatever he wished besides what had been promised. He reminded him of the vow he had made if he gained the kingdom: this was to build the temple of God in Jerusalem, and to restore the vessels still left in Babylon. "And this," he said, "is my request." The king, pleased at this, wrote to the satraps to supply cedar wood for the building of the temple and to help Zerubbabel build the city, that all the Israelite captives who had gone to Judaea should be free and exempt from taxes, and that fifty talents should be given for the raising of the temple. He also sent all the vessels he still had; and all that Cyrus had decreed about the Jews, Darius ordained too.

## 4.4

When this had happened, Zerubbabel came to Babylon and told his countrymen the good news from the king; and all who were eager to return to Jerusalem set out. The total of those who went was four million six hundred and twenty-eight thousand, apart from the Levites, gatekeepers, temple slaves and servants who followed those going up. Their leader was Zerubbabel, the son of Shealtiel, of the tribe of Judah, a descendant of David, and Jeshua, the son of Jozadak the high priest; besides these, Mordecai and Serebias were rulers anointed from the people. Going to Jerusalem, they sacrificed and began the building of the temple. The temple was soon completed; and when the sanctuary had been finished, the priests, the Levites and the sons of Asaph sang hymns to God. But the elders of the clans, recalling the former temple in their memories and seeing that the one just made fell short of the old one, were moved to tears and lamentation, and their wailing drowned the sound of the trumpets.

While the temple was still being raised, the Samaritans came to Zerubbabel asking to share with them in building the temple. He did not accept them as partners in the building, but said he would allow them to worship in the temple. The Samaritans were annoyed at this, and persuaded the nations in Syria to ask the satraps to stop the raising of the temple. The governors of Syria and Phoenicia came up to Jerusalem and asked by whose permission they were raising the temple like a fortress and surrounding the city with very strong walls. Zerubbabel and Jeshua the high priest said that the building had been permitted first by Cyrus, and then again by Darius. The governors of the lands of Syria and Phoenicia decided not to stop the building of the works, but wrote at once to Darius. When the Jews were afraid that the king might change his mind about what he had arranged, the prophets Haggai and Zechariah urged them to take heart and fear nothing unwelcome. So, trusting the prophets, they kept on vigorously with the building.

Darius received the letters and the charges of the Samaritans, that the Jews were fortifying the city and building the temple as a fortress, and that Cambyses had stopped their building, and ordered a search to be made in the royal records about this. And it was found written at Ecbatana, in the citadel in Media, that in the first year of his reign Cyrus had ordered the temple in Jerusalem and the altar to be built, had ordained that the expense of them be met from the royal funds and the vessels of the temple returned, and had ordered the rulers there to help with the work and pay the Jews bulls, rams, lambs and kids for sacrifice, and fine flour, wine and oil. Finding this in the records, Darius wrote back to those who had written: "I have found the copy of the letter in the records of Cyrus and sent it to you, and I wish everything to be done as it contains. Farewell." Learning the king's intention, the governors of those lands worked with the Jews. The temple was built in seven years, and the Israelites offered sacrifices renewing their former blessings. When the feast of unleavened bread came, all the people flocked from the villages into the city, kept the feast and offered the sacrifice called the Passover.

They lived in Jerusalem under an aristocratic constitution combined with oligarchy; for the priests were at the head of affairs until the descendants of Hasmonaeus came to be kings. For before the captivity they had been ruled by kings for five hundred and thirty-two years, six months and ten days; and before the kings, rulers called judges and monarchs governed them. Living under this form of government, they spent more than five hundred years after Moses and Joshua.

The Samaritans, being hostile to them, did the Jews much harm. So envoys were sent from Jerusalem to Darius, Zerubbabel and four other rulers. The king, learning the charges against the Samaritans, wrote to the cavalry commanders of Samaria and the council not to trouble the Jews at all, but to supply them from the royal treasury, out of the tribute of Samaria, with everything useful for their sacrifices.

## 4.5

When Darius died, his son Xerxes took over the kingdom, and succeeded to his piety towards God too, and was most generous to the Jews. At that time there was in Babylon Ezra, a good man and most exact in his knowledge of the laws of Moses. He became a friend of Xerxes, and wishing to go up to Jerusalem and take with him some of the Hebrews in Babylon, he asked the king to make him known to the satraps by his letters. He wrote this: "Thinking it a work of my own kindness that those of the Israelites who wish should set out together for Jerusalem, I have ordered this to Ezra, priest and reader of the divine law, and whoever wishes, let him go. And all the silver and gold that may be found in the land of the Babylonians dedicated to God, I order to be taken to Jerusalem. Whatever you have in mind, Ezra, do that too, meeting the expense from the royal treasury. And I order that neither tribute nor any other burden be laid on the priests, the Levites and all the servants of the temple. And you, Ezra, by the wisdom of God, appoint judges who know the law, to judge in all Syria and Phoenicia; and you shall have those who do not know it learn it."

Ezra took this letter and read it in Babylon to the Jews there, and sent a copy of it to all his countrymen; and they were all overjoyed. Many of them took their possessions and came to Babylon, so as to return to Jerusalem; but the whole people of the Israelites stayed where they were. That is why Josephus says that two tribes are subject to the Romans in Asia and Europe, but the ten tribes are beyond the Euphrates, countless myriads who cannot be known by number. Many came to Ezra and went with him to Jerusalem. Ezra handed over to the treasurers what Xerxes, his counsellors and the Israelites in Babylon had given to God, amounting to many talents of silver and bronze, and gave the king's letters to the governors of the lands. They honoured the nation and helped them in every need.

Learning afterwards that some of the people, the priests and the Levites had married foreign wives, he persuaded them with many tears and constant exhortations to put away the foreign women and the children born of them. When the feast of tabernacles came and almost the whole multitude of the people had gathered for it, they asked Ezra to have the laws of Moses read. Hearing them they were moved to tears, distressed about the past. But Ezra said they must not lament, since it was a feast, but keep the feast, make merry and keep the laws in future.

He died an old man. One of the captives, called Nehemiah, the cupbearer of Xerxes, walking before Susa, saw some strangers entering the city, and hearing them speaking in Hebrew, he went up and asked where they came from. When they said they had come from Jerusalem, he asked further how the people fared and their mother city Jerusalem. When they answered that things were going badly, for the surrounding nations were bringing many terrible things on them, Nehemiah wept; and when the hour came, he went in just as he was to serve the king. The king, seeing him gloomy, asked why he was downcast. He said the misfortunes of his country were the cause of his grief, and begged to be allowed to go and raise the wall and complete what was lacking in the temple. The king granted the request and the next day gave him a letter to the governor of Phoenicia, Syria and Samaria, ordering him to honour Nehemiah and supply the expenses of the building.

So he came to Jerusalem, with many of his countrymen following him, in the twenty-fifth year of the reign of Xerxes, and gave the letter to the governor of the lands mentioned. He divided the building of the wall among the people by villages and towns, so that it might be finished as quickly as possible. The Jews prepared for the work (they were so called from the day they came up from Babylon; for since the tribe of Judah came first to those places, both they themselves and the country were renamed Jews and Judaea after them); but the Ammonites, Moabites and Samaritans were annoyed at the building of the walls, attacked them and killed very many, and plotted against Nehemiah himself. The multitude, alarmed by this, would almost have given up the building. But Nehemiah, setting a band of men about him to guard his person, went on tirelessly going round the circuit of the city night and day; and he endured this hardship for two years and four months. For in that time the wall of Jerusalem was finished, in the twenty-eighth year of Xerxes' reign, in the ninth month. Nehemiah, seeing that the city was short of men, urged the priests and Levites to leave the countryside and move into the city. Nehemiah died an old man, a man of good character, just and most generous to his countrymen.

## 4.6

When Xerxes died, the kingdom passed to his son Cyrus, whom they also call Artaxerxes. In the third year of his reign he called the commanders and satraps of his nations and feasted them lavishly for many days; and Queen Vashti likewise held a banquet for the women. Since she surpassed all in beauty, the king wished to show her to his fellow banqueters, and sent ordering her to come to the banquet; but she would not obey, and though he sent many times she would not give way. So the king was angered at her disobedience. He shared the woman's disobedience with the seven counsellors of the Persians, and they judged that she must be put away. She was put away; but he was grieved, being in love with her. While he was in this state his friends advised him to gather fair virgins from everywhere and take the one preferred as his wife. He followed this advice, and when many virgins had been gathered, there was found in Babylon a girl, a Jew by race, of the tribe of Benjamin, an orphan who had lost both parents, brought up by her uncle Mordecai, one of the leading men among the Jews; the girl's name was Esther. The king, pleased with her, took her as his wife and put the diadem on her. Her uncle moved from Babylon to Susa, spending his time about the palace and asking after his niece. For neither did she make known the nation she came from, nor did Mordecai acknowledge his kinship with her.

The king made a law that no one should approach him uninvited when he sat on the throne, or the one who so approached would die at the hands of the axe-bearers standing by him, unless he stretched out the rod he held in his hands towards him; for whoever touched the rod was safe. When two of his eunuchs plotted against King Artaxerxes, Barnabazus, the servant of one of them, a Jew by race, learned of the plot and told Mordecai, and he, through Esther, informed the king of the plotters. The king crucified the eunuchs, and ordered Mordecai's name to be written in the records and that he should stay at the palace.

All bowed down to Haman, who was the king's friend above all. When Mordecai would not bow down to him, because of the law, Haman, learning that he was a Jew, was indignant and said to him that the free Persians bowed down to him, while he, a slave, refused to do it. So he went to the king and accused the whole nation, and asked him to order it to be destroyed root and branch and no remnant of it to be left; and so that the king might not lose their tribute, he himself promised to give forty thousand talents of silver. So Haman asked this; and the king made him a present both of the silver and of the people, to treat as he wished. At once an edict was sent by Haman to all the nations as from the king, containing this: "I order all those indicated by my second father Haman to be destroyed with their wives and children, and I wish this to be done on the fourteenth day of the twelfth month of the present year." When this letter was brought to the cities and lands, the destruction of the Jews was prepared; and those in Susa were in the same state.

Mordecai, learning what had happened, put on sackcloth, poured ashes over himself, and cried out that a nation that had done no wrong was being destroyed. He came to the palace and stood outside, for it was not allowed to enter in such a guise. Queen Esther, learning this, sent a eunuch to Mordecai to find out why he was mourning. He told the eunuch what had happened, and bade Esther entreat the king about it. When she learned this, she let him know that, not having been called by the king, she feared she would die if she went to him uninvited. Mordecai let her know that she should not consider her own safety but that of the nation; if not, help would surely come to it from God, but she and her father's house would be destroyed. She bade him go into Susa and have the Jews there fast three days for her. Mordecai did so; and Esther threw herself on the ground in mourning dress, stayed without food for three days and prayed to God.

After the three days she put on royal attire, and with two maidservants, on one of whom she leant lightly while the other held up with her fingertips the long train of her robe that flowed to the ground, she came to the ruler and went in with fear. When she came before him as he sat royally on the throne, and he looked at her grimly, she was seized with faintness from fear and fell, speechless, against the maid at her side. The king, afraid for his wife (God had provided this), leapt from the throne, took her in his arms and revived her, kissing her and telling her to take heart, stretching out his golden rod and putting the sceptre in her hands. Taking heart from this, she asked him to come to her for a feast with Haman. When he consented and they came, the king bade her say what she wished, for she would not fail to obtain anything. She said she would tell him her wish the next day, if he came to her for a feast again with Haman. When the king promised, Haman went out overjoyed; and seeing Mordecai in the court, he was indignant that he did not bow down to him. Going home he told his wife and friends what honour he enjoyed from the queen too, and how he was vexed to see Mordecai in the court. When his wife told him to have a stake of fifty cubits cut, and in the morning to ask the king and crucify Mordecai, he accepted the advice and ordered his servants to prepare the stake and set it up in the court for Mordecai's punishment.

It was ready; but that night God took sleep from the king. Lying awake, he ordered the scribe to bring the records of his own deeds and read them. As they were being read, it was found that Mordecai had informed the king of the eunuchs' plot. When the scribe had told only this and passed to another deed, the king stopped him and asked whether any reward given to him was recorded. When he heard there was none, he asked what hour of the night it was; learning that it was already dawn, he ordered them to call in whichever of his friends they found in front of the court. Haman was found, having hurried so as to ask for Mordecai. When he was called the king said: "Advise me how I might honour someone I love." Haman, reckoning that whatever advice he gave he would be giving for himself, since he was loved by the king more than the others, said: "If you wish to glorify this man, make him go on horseback wearing the same robe as you and having a gold collar, and let one of your close friends go before him proclaiming through the whole city that this honour is given to whomever the king honours." The king, pleased with the advice, said: "Seek out Mordecai the Jew, and giving him the horse, the robe and the collar, go before him and proclaim it; for you are my close friend. He shall have these things from me for having saved my life." Hearing this, he was confounded, and, struck helpless, nevertheless went out bringing the horse, the purple and the collar. Finding Mordecai in sackcloth in front of the court, he told him to put on the purple. He said: "Vilest of men, do you mock our misfortunes like this?" But learning that the king gave him these as a reward for his safety, for having revealed the eunuchs' plot, he put on the purple and the collar, mounted the horse and went round the city, with Haman going before and proclaiming that so the king honours whomever he loves.

When he had gone round the city, he went back to the king,

## 4.7

and Haman returned home and, weeping, told his wife what had happened. Meanwhile eunuchs came from Esther hurrying Haman to the dinner. One of them saw the cross in his house, and learning from one of the servants that they had prepared it for Mordecai, who was to be asked for to be punished, he went away for the time being. When the feast was near its end, the king bade the queen say what she asked; and she lamented the people's danger, said that she too had been given over to destruction with the nation, and that Haman had brought this about by his request. The king, troubled at this, leapt up from the banquet, and Haman begged Esther; and as he fell on the couch entreating the queen, the king came back in and said: "Vilest of men, are you trying to force my wife too?" At this the eunuch who was present told of the cross he had seen prepared for Mordecai. The king at once ordered Haman to be hanged on that cross. So he perished, and the queen received his property as a gift. Learning of Mordecai's kinship with Esther, the king gave him the ring he had given to Haman before. The queen gave the property to Mordecai, and begged the king to free the nation of the Hebrews from danger. He allowed her to write what she wished about the nation in his name. What was written was this. They accused Haman of being wicked and ungrateful and of having asked for the destruction of a nation that had done no wrong; they called Mordecai a saviour, and the Jews the best citizens and worshippers of God; and they not only freed them from the punishment to which Haman had demanded them, but held them worthy of honour, allowed them to live in peace using their own laws, and granted them to defend themselves on the thirteenth day of the twelfth month, which is Adar, against those who had wronged them, and ordered the satraps of the lands to help them, so that they might pursue their enemies.

The Jews in Susa, learning of Mordecai's fortune, thought the good fortune shared by all, and on the thirteenth of the month Adar, which among the Macedonians is called Dystrus, the Jews in Susa killed about five hundred of their enemies, and those in the other cities and lands destroyed seventy-five thousand. Those in Susa killed three hundred on the following day too, at Esther's request; and the ten sons of Haman were crucified, the king granting her this too. The fourteenth of the month the whole nation made a festival. And from then on they keep both days as festivals, calling them Phrouraia. Mordecai was great and shared the government with the king.

When the high priesthood passed to John, the son of Joiada, his brother Jeshua, a friend of Bagoses, the general of Artaxerxes' force, had a promise of receiving the high priesthood. For this reason John, provoked, killed his brother in the temple. Bagoses came at once and went into the temple, saying: "If those who have dared murder in the temple go into it, I too will go in, being purer than they"; and for seven years he afflicted the Jews. When John ended his life, his son Jaddua succeeded to the high priesthood. He had a brother, Manasseh, to whom Sanballat, the satrap of Darius, the last king of the Persians, gave his own daughter in marriage, called Nicaso. The leading men of Jerusalem were in uproar because the brother of the high priest had married a foreigner, and ordered him either to divorce her or keep away from the altar. He went to his father-in-law and told him this. Sanballat promised to preserve the priesthood for him, to make him high priest and ruler of the places he himself governed, if he did not give up his daughter, and to build a temple on Mount Gerizim, which is the highest of the mountains about Samaria, greater than the one in Jerusalem, with the consent of King Darius; and Manasseh, elated by this, stayed with Sanballat. Since many other priests and Israelites were entangled in such marriages, all came over to Manasseh. Sanballat was ready to put the request about building the temple on Gerizim to King Darius, but Darius engaged Alexander at Issus in Cilicia and was defeated and fled. So Sanballat, giving up on Darius, came to Alexander with eight thousand of his men, and said he surrendered himself and the places he ruled to him. When Alexander received him, being now confident about his son-in-law and the raising of the temple on Gerizim, he made his request. Permitted to build, he built the temple with all zeal, and appointed his son-in-law Manasseh high priest.

## 4.8

Since the course of the history has mentioned Alexander, it is well to tell in passing his deeds, his character and from where and from whom he was born, and then to bring the account back to its sequence; all the more because this king visited Jerusalem, and something worthy of wonder happened between him and the high priest and the people, and he himself told of a divine vision, as Josephus records; which the account, as it proceeds, will tell after the history of him.

He was the son of Philip, king of the Macedonians, born to him by Olympias; but the myth is told that he was the son of Ammon, who lay with Olympias in the form of a serpent; and they say Ammon is Zeus. But this is a myth. It is said that his mother, before the night on which she lay with Philip, dreamed that a thunderbolt fell on her belly, that a great fire was kindled from it, and that this spread everywhere and died away. After the marriage Philip seemed in his sleep to put a seal on his wife's belly, and on the seal a lion was engraved. The other seers interpreted the dream in other ways, but Aristander said that the woman who had been sealed was pregnant, for empty things are not sealed, and that the child to be born would prove high-spirited and lion-like. He was born on the day on which the temple of Artemis at Ephesus was burned; and some of the magi who were there ran about saying that that day had given birth to ruin and heavy calamity for Asia.

Alexander was fair in colour, but the fairness was flushed with red, especially about the chest and face, and a sweet fragrance breathed from his skin and from his mouth. The cause of this would be the heat of his body; for being fiery and very hot it consumed the moisture and let nothing putrid stay on the surface of his body. From boyhood he showed the weight of his spirit. Being swift of foot, when people asked him whether he would compete in the stade race at Olympia, he said: "If I were to compete with kings." If the capture of some famous city by his father was reported to him, or a brilliant victory in battle, he would say to his companions as if vexed: "My father will leave nothing splendid for me and you to do."

When the horse Bucephalas was brought to Philip, at a price of thirteen talents, and would neither let anyone mount it, as was shown to Philip, nor bear a voice, and so seemed altogether unmanageable, Alexander said: "What a horse they are sending away because they do not know how to handle it!" His father, rebuking him, said: "And will you be able to handle it?" He answered: "I would handle this one better than anyone else." Allowed to try the horse, he took the rein and turned it towards the sun, understanding perhaps that it was frightened by its shadow moving in front of it. Stroking it a little, he leapt up and mounted it, and letting it go a little he gave it its head to run; those watching were in anguish. When he had turned back with pride and joy, Philip's people cried out, and he shed tears of joy, and kissing his son said: "Seek, child, a kingdom equal to yourself; for Macedonia cannot hold you." His nature was hard to move when he was ordered, but if someone approached him with reason and persuasion, he was obedient.

He studied with Aristotle, sharing not only in ethical and political teaching but also in the more secret teachings, which they did not share with many, calling them esoteric and acroamatic. For a little letter of his to Aristotle is preserved, running thus: "You did not do right in publishing the acroamatic teachings; for in what shall we differ from the rest, if the teachings in which we were educated are to be common to all?" Aristotle wrote back that "they are published and not published", showing by this their obscurity and difficulty. In medicine too he pursued not only the theory but also the practice. When he was sixteen and already giving proof of his spirit and courage, he was loved dearly by his father; but because of Philip's loves unfitting his age and his mother's jealousy and sullenness, Alexander had quarrels with his father.

## 4.9

When Pausanias killed Philip, he received the kingdom at twenty years old. Though affairs were in turmoil everywhere at the start of his rule, he quickly settled them by daring and weight of spirit, taking and razing Thebes and selling the captured Thebans, but making peace with the Athenians. At that time a Theban woman of the notable families, Timocleia, was violated by force by one of the commanders, a Thracian, who then questioned her about money. She took him aside alone and led him to a well, saying she had hidden the finest of her treasures in it. When the commander leant over the mouth of the well, the woman pushed him down into it and killed him by throwing stones on him from above. When she was brought to him bound, Alexander asked who she was. She answered fearlessly: "I am the sister of Theagenes, who was chosen general against Philip and fell fighting for the freedom of the Greeks." Alexander, admiring the woman's greatness of mind and the firmness of her soul, set her free with her children.

Going to Diogenes of Sinope, who was living near Corinth, and finding him warming himself in the sun, he greeted the man and asked whether he needed anything. He said he needed the warmth of the sun, and asked him to move aside. As Alexander went away, those about him laughed at Diogenes; but he said to them, admiring the man's disdain: "If I were not Alexander, I would be Diogenes."

Setting out on his campaign into Asia, he did not board ship before he had given to one of his companions a field, to another a village, to another some other revenue. When Perdiccas said, "And what, king, are you leaving for yourself?", he answered: "My hopes." When he had crossed the Hellespont, Darius's satraps met him with a heavy force at the crossing of the Granicus. While those about him feared the depth of the river, the force of the current and the attack of the enemy besides, he plunged into the stream with thirteen squadrons of horsemen, risking himself against it, against the enemy and against muddy and broken landing places. For this reason he seemed then to have made the crossing madly rather than like a general. Having crossed in this way, he was forced to fight in confusion. Many charged at him, since he was conspicuous by his arms; he was shot at but not wounded, but when someone struck his helmet with a cleaver, the blow went so far into the helmet that the cleaver touched his hair. While the battle was going on in this way, the Macedonian forces crossed the Granicus; and Darius's men no longer held out, but turned to flight. From this battle a great tide of good fortune came to Alexander; for Sardis came over to him and the rest joined him. From there he turned to the coast, and took Pamphylia, Cilicia and Phoenicia, Pisidia and Phrygia; then he subdued the Paphlagonians and Cappadocians.

Darius set out from Susa with six hundred thousand men, elated by the size of his army and emboldened as if Alexander were afraid, because he stayed long in Cilicia. That was because of an illness, when he had been given up by the other physicians, and Philip the Acarnanian alone dared to treat him with a drug. Parmenion had written to him first that Philip had been bribed by Darius with great gifts and promises if he killed him; he read the letter, revealed it to no one, and kept it by him. Just as Philip brought him the cup of the drug, he took it confidently himself and handed him the letter. He drank and Philip read, looking at each other, Alexander with a gracious and cheerful face, Philip with a frightened and troubled one. As Darius hurried against Alexander, so that, as he said, the enemy might not run away, Amyntas, a Macedonian deserter with him, said: "Take heart, king; Alexander will not run away, but will soon be marching against you."

When battle was joined at Issus in Cilicia, the place, because of its narrowness, gave Alexander a great advantage, and he gave himself more by his skilful generalship. Then too he was wounded in the thigh by a sword, fighting among the foremost. Winning a brilliant victory, he did not catch Darius, who fled, but took his chariot and bow. Darius's camp was plundered by the Macedonians, but Darius's tent was set aside for Alexander, with the servants, the wealth and the rest of the equipment. Then someone told him that Darius's mother, wife and two daughters were being brought in captive, wailing as if Darius were dead, since they had seen his chariot and bow captured. Moved at the news because of the instability of fortune, he sent someone to tell the women that Darius was alive and that nothing unpleasant would befall them; for he did not think Darius an enemy, but was contending with him for the kingdom. He took away nothing of the service they had, did not come into their presence, and did not allow them to suffer or expect anything unworthy from anyone.

## 4.10

Having won at Issus, he became master of Damascus too, where the Persians and Darius himself had left their money and most of their baggage. After this Cyprus and Phoenicia, except Tyre, were handed over to him. Tyre was taken by siege; while he was besieging it, Alexander saw two dreams: Heracles seemed to call him from the wall and greet him; and the other dream showed him a satyr sporting with him, escaping when he wished to catch it, but at last coming into his hands. This dream was interpreted, by dividing the word, as showing him that "Tyre will be yours" (*sa Tyros*). Then, besieging Gaza, a great city of Syria, he was struck on the shoulder by a stone, but took the city.

Having become master of Egypt, he wished to found a Greek city in it named after himself, and had already marked out a site for the city. As he slept, a man seemed to stand over him, grey-haired and venerable, the one who appeared being Homer, and to speak these lines:

"Then there is an island in the surging sea, off Egypt; they call it Pharos."

Leaping up, he went at once to Pharos, and pleased by the fine nature of the place he founded the city there, joining it to the mainland by a mole, though it had been an island before. It is said that, delighted with the natural fitness of the place, he said that Homer was great in other things and a wise architect too.

Going to the temple of Ammon through a waterless and sandy region, he found there too the good fortune that attended him in everything easing the hardship of that journey. For rains fell and averted the danger from lack of water, and made the air temperate, the sand being wetted and packed down by the moisture. When the landmarks of the road were confounded and its guides went astray, ravens flying overhead led the way for those with him, and if any who travelled slowly were left behind, they brought them back at night by their croaking. When he had come through the hardship of the journey in this way and reached Ammon, the priest there, wishing to address him in Greek, made a barbarism at the end of the greeting, saying *o paidios* instead of *o paidion*. And the barbarism gave many the belief that Alexander's birth was from the gods, as the story spread that the priest had said to him "son of Zeus" (*pai Dios*). He boasted among the barbarians of his birth from the god, so that Olympias even said: "Will Alexander not stop slandering me to Hera?" But with the Greeks he was sparing of the claim. Once, when wounded by an arrow and the blood was running from the wound, he said: "This is blood that flows, and not ichor, such as flows in the blessed gods."

When Darius wrote to him offering ten thousand talents as ransom for his dearest, giving up to him all the land this side of the Euphrates and making him his friend by marrying one of his daughters, Parmenion said: "If I were Alexander, I would accept this." "And so would I, by Zeus," he said, "if I were Parmenion." He wrote back to Darius that if he came to him he would not fail to meet with every kindness.

One of the eunuchs of Darius's wife ran away and, coming safe to Darius, reported the death of the queen; for she had died first, in childbirth. Darius lamented, saying that the queen had not only become a captive but had not received royal burial either. The eunuch said: "Take heart, master. Neither while she lived did my mistress, your mother and your children suffer any harm, nor in death did she lack fitting burial, but she was honoured even with the tears of the enemy." At this Darius fell into strange suspicions, and taking the eunuch aside he bound him first with oaths to say whether Alexander had felt any passion for his wife. "For how," he said, "has the wife of an enemy, a young man at war with me, been so honoured, as you say?" The eunuch cut him short and said: "Speak no ill, master, and do not dishonour your wife or slander Alexander, who has shown more self-control to the Persian women than courage to the Persians"; and he confirmed his words with oaths, telling of Alexander's restraint and his other virtue. And Darius prayed that he might be able to repay Alexander for the kindness he had shown to his dearest, or else that no one other than Alexander might sit on the throne of Cyrus.

## 4.11

Having taken the land this side of the Euphrates, Alexander went against Darius, who was bringing an army of a million men. When the armies were in sight of each other, and it was night, and the plain between was lit with the fires of the barbarians, and a great uproar came from their camp, Parmenion and some others of the companions, struck by the numbers, advised Alexander to attack the enemy by night; for they could not stand against so great an army in close combat. He said: "I do not steal victory." The next day, when battle was joined, according to some at Arbela, according to others at Gaugamela, the barbarians gave way, and there was a pursuit of them. Alexander, seeing Darius from a distance on a high chariot, surrounded by very many splendidly armed horsemen, drove the fugitives together there, so as to throw those who stood into confusion with them and scatter many of them. When many had fallen in front of Darius and the chariot could not be driven out, being hemmed in by the bodies, Darius got down from it, mounted a mare and fled. He would have been caught, had not Parmenion sent for Alexander to help the wing he commanded, which was hard pressed.

So the victory came to Alexander brilliantly, the kingdom of the Persians was overthrown from that time, Alexander was proclaimed king of Asia, and the whole of Babylonia came under him. Becoming master of Susa, he found in the palace forty thousand talents of coin and a wealth of other riches beyond telling. Mastering Persis too, he found as much coin as at Susa. They record that the rest of the wealth was carried away by ten thousand pairs of mules and five thousand camels. He set fire to the palace, but quickly repented and ordered the fire put out. Being generous, he gave still more as his affairs grew. Seeing those about him living luxuriously and lavishly, he gently rebuked them, saying he wondered whether they had not learned, by comparing the life of the Persians with their own, that luxury is most slavish and toil most kingly. He himself did not slacken, toiling at campaigns and hunts. His companions, wishing to live in luxury because of their wealth but forced to follow him, went so far as to abuse him. At first he bore the abuse mildly, saying that it is kingly to be ill spoken of while doing good. Later, however, the many slanders made him harsh and rendered him severe and implacable towards those who abused him. Yet before, when he heard capital cases, he used to put his hand over one of his ears, as though keeping it untouched for the accused and free from the accusation.

Setting out against Darius with three thousand men, he pursued for many days, so that most gave up because of the hardness of the road and the lack of water. Then some Macedonians bringing water in skins met him, filled a helmet and offered it to him as he suffered badly from thirst. He took the helmet, and seeing those about him looking at him, he did not drink, but gave it back to those who had given it, saying: "If I drink alone, these men will lose heart." Falling on the enemy, he passed by their camp and hastened to overtake Darius. He lay in a covered carriage, full of wounds and already dying. Asking for water and drinking, he said to Polystratus, who gave it: "This is the limit of all my misfortune, that I have been well treated and cannot repay you; but may Alexander repay you the favour, and the gods repay Alexander for his kindness to my mother, my wife and my children." With these words he died. Alexander, standing over him dead, wrapped his body in his own cloak and sent it to his mother, royally adorned. Bessus, who had killed him, he tore apart, ordering him to be tied to two trees bent down by force, and then the trees to be let go, so that the man was torn apart as each tree sprang back violently to its natural upright position.

Going into Hyrcania by the Hyrcanian Sea, which is also called the Caspian, when some barbarians took his horse Bucephalas, he was furious, and sent threatening to kill them all unless they sent the horse back; and they brought the horse and surrendered themselves to him.

## 4.12

From there he went off into Parthia, where he first put on barbarian dress, less pompous than the Median but grander than the Persian; but the sight seemed offensive to the Macedonians. Choosing thirty thousand boys from the captives, he ordered them to be taught Greek letters and armed in the Macedonian way. He fell in love with Roxane, a beautiful woman, but did not approach her except lawfully. Of his friends, Hephaestion, who praised the dress he had put on and changed his clothes like him, he called Alexander's friend, and Craterus, who did not depart from ancestral custom, the king's friend; and he used the one for dealing with the barbarians, and did business with the Macedonians and Greeks through Craterus.

Philotas, the son of Parmenion, who boasted that the successes came from him and his father and called Alexander a stripling, and then, as he learned, was plotting against him, he put to death. Sending to Media, he killed his father too, for he was there. Cleitus also, one of the companions: at a drinking party, when verses were being sung that had been made about some generals recently defeated by the barbarians, and Alexander was listening to them with pleasure, Cleitus grew harsh and indignant and boastful through insolence and drink and abused him. Alexander first threw an apple at him; and when he went on being shameless and bold and speaking the iambics of Euripides, "Alas, how ill things are ordered in Greece," and the rest, Alexander, boiling over with rage, ran him through with a spear and killed him. As soon as his anger had passed, he repented and set out to kill himself, but was held back by his bodyguards and carried into his chamber, where he spent the night and the following day in lamentation and lay exhausted, speechless, only groaning very heavily. When his friends came in to him, he would not accept their words. But when Aristander the seer reminded him of a vision he had seen about Cleitus and of some signs that had happened to him, and said that what had happened was fated, he began to give way.

About to invade India, when the wagons had been packed, he set fire first to his own, then to those of his friends, and after that he ordered those of the Macedonians to be burned. This grieved a few, but the rest set to the deed with shouting and enthusiasm and filled Alexander with eagerness for the campaign. He was now fearsome and implacable in punishing wrongdoers. Many dangers befell him in the battles and he met with serious wounds; but most of the losses of the army were caused by shortage of necessities and harsh climate. He himself, through daring, thought nothing unconquerable for the bold and nothing secure for the timid. Attacking a sheer rock with the younger Macedonians, he addressed one of them called Alexander and said: "You at least should be brave because of your name too." When the Macedonians hesitated to cross a deep river, he said: "Why, wretch that I am, did I not learn to swim?" When envoys came to him from the besieged cities, the eldest of them, named Acouphis, asked what they should do to be counted his friends; Alexander said: "If they choose you as ruler, and send me the hundred best men." Acouphis laughed at this and said: "But I shall rule better, king, if I send you the worst and not the best."

## 4.13

Taxiles, who ruled a part of India fertile and prosperous, not inferior to Egypt, and was a wise man, sent to greet Alexander and said: "What need is there of wars between us, if you have come to take from us neither water nor needed food? In other things, if I am the stronger I am ready to do good; if the weaker, I do not shrink from being grateful when well treated." Alexander, pleased at this, said: "I will contend with you and fight it out in kindnesses, so that you may not get the better of me in generosity." Receiving many gifts and giving more, he at last gave him a thousand talents of coin. He made a truce with a city of India, and when the mercenaries in it, the most warlike of the Indians, were leaving, he killed them all; which is like a stain on his deeds of war.

Then he fought Porus, who too was king of a land of India, and whose body was four cubits and a span in height. Having defeated him and taken him alive, Alexander asked how he should treat him; he answered, "Like a king." When Alexander asked further whether he said anything else, Porus said: "Everything is in 'like a king'." So he let him go and gave him rule, with the name of satrap, not only over what he had ruled before but over many others too. In this battle Bucephalas too was wounded and died some time later, being already very old. Alexander grieved as though he had lost a friend.

But the Macedonians, worn out by the battle against Porus, would not obey Alexander when he pressed them to cross the river Ganges, which was thirty-two stades broad and a hundred fathoms deep. He, in despondency, stayed in his tent without coming out, reckoning not crossing the Ganges an admission of defeat. But when his friends urged him and the soldiers entreated him with weeping, he gave in and turned back. He built many ferries and rafts and travelled down the rivers, wishing to see the outer sea; and sailing along he landed and subdued cities.

Among the Malli, the most warlike of the Indians, he came near to disaster. For he was the first to climb the wall by a ladder; when the ladder broke and the enemy attacked and shot at him, he gathered himself and let himself drop from above into the midst of the enemy; and by chance he landed on his feet. At first the barbarians scattered, but seeing him with only two shield-bearers, they turned back and fought, and wounded him with close-fighting weapons. One, standing off, shot him with an arrow from a bow and drove the arrow into the bones about his breast, and the blow went home so that he gave way and bent; at which the man who had shot him ran at him with a sword. Peucestas and Limnaeus shielded him and were both wounded; Limnaeus died, but Peucestas still fought on, and Alexander killed the barbarian who had wounded him. Wounded in many places, and then struck on the neck by a club, he leant against the wall and looked towards the barbarians. Meanwhile the Macedonians gathered, snatched him up and carried him unconscious into the tent. Since the arrowhead was fixed in one of the bones, when the shaft was drawn out sharp pains and fainting fits came on, so that it was expected he would die. Yet he escaped the danger, and being treated for a long time he threw the Macedonians, who longed for him, into uproar; and he came out.

## 4.14

When he had recovered, he went on down the rivers again, subduing much land and many cities. He seized some of the gymnosophists, set them hard questions, gave them gifts and let them go. Taxiles persuaded one of those of repute among them, called Calanus, to come to Alexander. He threw a dry hide down in front of the king and went round treading on its edges. The hide, pressed down at the edges in turn by the treading, rose up in the other parts. Then, when he trod on the middle and held it down, the whole hide lay still and did not move. By the example he urged Alexander not to linger at the edges of his empire, but to hold the middle, so that the parts round about too might be at rest.

After being brought down the rivers to the sea in seven months, he is said to have sailed with his ships out into the Ocean. Then, turning back, he ordered the ships to sail along the coast with India on their right, and he himself, going by land, came into the utmost hardship and lost so great a number that not even a quarter of the army was brought back from India, because of diseases, bad food, heat and hunger. For he passed through a barren country with few sheep, and those fed on sea fish and having poor, foul-smelling flesh. Having crossed that country in sixty days and reached Gedrosia, he was in plenty, restored his force and revelled himself. Coming down to the sea, he punished the wicked among his generals. For the campaign inland, the wound among the Malli and the loss of so many men had made people doubt his survival, and had stirred the generals and satraps to insolence and injustice; and they moved the tributaries to revolt. When one of the satraps, Abulites, prepared no provisions but brought him three thousand talents of coin, he ordered them to be set before the horses, and when they did not eat, he said: "What use are these to us?"

In Persia, seeing the tomb of Cyrus dug open, he put to death the man who had dug it, a man of Pella and not of the obscure. The tomb of Cyrus bore this inscription: "O man, whoever you are and wherever you come from, for I know that you will come, I am Cyrus who won the empire for the Persians. Do not then grudge me this little earth that covers my body." When this was read, it moved Alexander, as he reflected on the instability of affairs.

Betrothing himself to Stateira, the daughter of Darius, and celebrating the wedding, he gave each of the nine thousand guests a golden bowl, made other splendid displays, and paid the debts of those who owed money, nearly ten thousand talents. Then Antigenes, one of the commanders, who pretended to owe and brought forward someone falsely as his creditor and took the money, was found out. The king, angered, drove him from the court and took away his command. When it was suspected that he would kill himself out of grief, Alexander, afraid, let go his anger against him and let him keep the money.

When the thirty thousand boys he had ordered to be trained turned out noble, handsome and not without talent in their exercises, Alexander was pleased, but the Macedonians were stung. When he was sending the weak and maimed down to the sea, they said they thought this an outrage and an insult, and told him to send them all away and be content with his young war-dancers. Angered at this, Alexander handed over the guard duties to the Persians. But when the Macedonians were humbled, he accepted them again, dismissed the unfit with magnificent gifts, and wrote to Antipater that they should have front seats at the games and theatres and sit there crowned.

When he was at Ecbatana, Hephaestion had a fever, and as the illness grew worse from undisciplined living, he died. Alexander did not bear the grief with reason, but crucified the physician, ordered the horses and mules to be shorn, pulled down the battlements of the cities, and stopped music and flutes in the camp for a long time.

As he was going to Babylon, Nearchus came back, having sailed again into the Euphrates from the great sea, saying that some Chaldaeans had met him who advised that Alexander keep away from Babylon. Paying no heed to the warning, he went on. When he was at the walls, ravens fighting one another fell in front of him. It was reported to him that Apollodorus, the general of Babylon, had sacrificed about him; and when the seer who had sacrificed, named Pythagoras, did not deny it, he asked how the sacrifice had turned out. When he said the liver had no lobe, Alexander said, "Alas, a strong sign," and let the seer go unharmed. Other signs happened to him too, and this one: when he had stripped for anointing and ball play, the young men playing ball, when they were about to dress, saw someone sitting silent on the king's throne, wearing the royal robe and with the diadem on his head. Questioned who he was, he was speechless for a long time, and at last with difficulty said he was called Dionysius, a Messenian by birth, that he had been brought there in chains on a charge, that Sarapis had loosed his chains, brought him here and told him to put on the robe and the diadem and sit in silence. Alexander made away with the man, as the seers advised, but he himself was despondent, and becoming fearful and troubled he took everything for a portent.

Having bathed, and gone to Medius's to revel, and spent the night there in revelry and the following day, he began to have a fever. Feverish and very thirsty, he drank wine, became delirious and died. This is one account of his death; another has it that he was feverish for several days and bathed, and the fever grew so strong that he lay speechless; that the Macedonians were in uproar, thinking he was dead, and shouted at the commanders, until the doors were opened to them and they saw Alexander on his bed; and after this he died. Some say that afterwards a story arose that he had been killed by poison, that Aristotle had advised Antipater in the deed, and that the poison was brought by him. It was water, cold and icy, from a rock at Nonacris, which they gather like a fine dew and put into an ass's hoof; for no other vessel holds it, but it breaks through from its coldness and sharpness. But most say it is invented that Alexander died of poison, and they take as proof that his body lay for many days uncared for in hot places, while the commanders quarrelled with one another, and showed no sign of such corruption, but stayed clean and seemed fresh.

It is said that when he knew that his life was failing him, he wished to throw himself secretly into the Euphrates, so that by disappearing he might give the impression that he had gone to the gods, having been born of them; but Roxane, learning this, prevented his attempt, and he said with a groan: "So you grudged me, wife, the glory of being made a god instead of dying." So Alexander, having been carried to great

## 4.15

fortune, died. When he was besieging Tyre, he wrote to the high priest of the Jews asking him to send him help and a market for the army, and to give him what they paid in tribute to Darius. When the high priest said he had given Darius oaths not to take up arms against him, and would not break them while Darius lived, Alexander was angry and threatened to march against Judaea. As soon as he had taken Tyre, he set out for Jerusalem. The high priest Jaddua was in anguish because of the king's anger, and prayed to God to protect the nation. God told him in his sleep to take heart, to adorn the city and open the gates, and himself with the priests to meet the king in their customary robes, and the people in white garments. He did as he had been told; and when Alexander was approaching the city, he went out with the priests and the people of the city to a place from which the city and the temple could be seen. Those following the king reckoned that he would order them to plunder the city and kill the high priest; but Alexander, seeing from afar the people and the priests adorned as has been said, and the high priest wearing the robe of hyacinth and gold with the mitre on his head and on it the gold plate on which the name of God was inscribed, came forward alone, bowed down and greeted the high priest.

At this all the others were astonished, and Parmenion even asked why he had bowed down to the high priest of the Jews. He said: "It is not he I bowed down to, but the God with whose high priesthood he has been honoured. For I saw him in my sleep in this very robe while I was still near Macedonia; and when I was pondering how I might master Asia, he urged me not to delay, and promised to lead my army himself and hand over to me the empire of the Persians. Since I have seen no one else in such a robe but him, I at once remembered the vision of the dream, and I think that, having made the campaign with God, I shall defeat Darius and win the empire of the Persians." With these words he took the high priest by the hand and entered the city; and going up to the temple he sacrificed to God as the high priest directed. He saw the book of Daniel, in which it is written that one of the Greeks would destroy the kingdom of the Persians, and was pleased by it; and he granted all that the Jews asked.

Seeing this, the Samaritans too met Alexander not far from Jerusalem, splendidly arrayed, and begged him to honour them and their temple with his presence. He said: "I will come when I return." When they asked him to grant them what he had granted the Jews, he asked who they were. They said they were Hebrews, but were called the Sidonians of Shechem. He asked again whether they were Jews. When they admitted they were not, he said: "I granted what you ask to the Jews." The temple on Mount Gerizim became a refuge for lawbreakers; for if anyone was accused of eating unclean food or of some other breach of the law, he fled to the Shechemites. When the high priest Jaddua died, his son Onias received the high priesthood.

## 4.16

When Alexander died, his kingdom was divided into four realms: Antigonus ruled Asia, and those already mentioned the rest. As they fought among themselves there were continual wars, and the cities suffered. Ptolemy the son of Lagus, who reigned over Egypt and was also called Saviour, proved the opposite of his name to Syria, and took Jerusalem by a trick. For entering the city on the sabbath as if to sacrifice, he mastered it without effort; for the Jews, not knowing his intention and being at rest because of the day, did not resist. Having mastered the nation in this way, he ruled it most harshly, and taking many captives he carried them off to Egypt and settled them there. The people of Jerusalem had disputes with the Samaritans, those of Jerusalem calling their own temple holy and saying the sacrifices must be made in it, and the Shechemites exalting the temple on Mount Gerizim.

When Ptolemy the son of Lagus had reigned over Egypt for forty years and died, his son Ptolemy Philadelphus succeeded to the rule. He was eager to have the Hebrew Scriptures translated from their native tongue into the Greek language, and freed those of the people of Jerusalem who were slaves in Egypt. For wishing to make a collection of books and being ambitious about it, he had Demetrius of Phalerum in charge of the libraries. Once, when Ptolemy asked Demetrius how many tens of thousands of books he now had, he said about two hundred thousand had been collected; but that among the Hebrews too there were writings of their laws worth attention, and these too ought to be acquired. So the king wrote to the high priest about them. Aristeas, who was a friend of the king, said: "Since we have decided not only to copy the laws of the Jews but also to translate them, how could we do this while many Jews are slaves in your kingdom? Release them, then, king, from slavery, and so you will make the nation eager both to copy the Scriptures and to translate them." The king, persuaded by Aristeas's words, ordered all the Jews who were slaves in Egypt to be set free, buying each from their masters for a hundred and twenty drachmas. The number of those freed came to more than a hundred thousand, and what was paid for their price came to four hundred and sixty talents.

So Ptolemy wrote to Eleazar the high priest. For when the high priest Onias died, his son Simon succeeded him, who was surnamed the Just; and when he died, leaving an infant son called Onias, Simon's brother Eleazar held the high priesthood. To him Ptolemy wrote, announcing the freeing of the enslaved Jews and his goodwill towards the nation, and asking that the laws themselves be sent, and six men from each tribe, trained to exactness both in their native language and in Greek, so that the Scriptures they had might be translated by them into the Greek tongue. He also sent offerings to the temple: a hundred talents of silver, twenty golden bowls and thirty silver ones, five mixing-bowls and a golden table. Eleazar, receiving Ptolemy's letter and the offerings, chose six men from each tribe and sent them bringing the law. When they reached Alexandria and brought the king the skins with the law written on them in golden letters and the gifts sent him by the high priest, the king was exceedingly pleased; he greeted the men, feasted with them, gave each three talents and prepared the finest lodgings for them.

Then Demetrius took them, led them to a quiet house and urged them to set to work. They made the translation exact with all possible zeal. When the work was finished in seventy-two days and what had been translated was read to the king, he rejoiced that his wish had been carried out, and greatly admired the mind and wisdom of the lawgiver. He wondered how none of the historians or of the other wise men had mentioned it. Demetrius told him that no one had dared to touch the recording of these laws because they were divine, and that some who had attempted them had been harmed by God. For Theopompus, wishing to write about them, was deranged in mind for more than thirty days; and understanding where his madness had come from, he appeased God in the intervals, and saw a dream that the affliction had befallen him because he had meddled with divine things and wished to publish them to ordinary men. So he gave up the work and came to his senses. "And Theodectes too," he said, "the tragic poet, wishing to mention in one of his plays things written in the sacred book, was afflicted in his eyes and blinded; and knowing the cause, he was freed from the misfortune after praying to God." The king took the books and bowed down to them, gave the men most generous gifts, sent offerings to the temple and the finest gifts to the high priest, and let them return to Jerusalem.

So Josephus has recorded that the translation of the Hebrew Scriptures was made; but others say that the translators of the Scriptures did not make the version together, but were divided two by two and set out the translation living in separate quarters, and after finishing came together, and when each one's writings were compared with the others they were found to differ neither in sense nor even in words, but to agree in everything. So the translation under Ptolemy Philadelphus

## 4.17

happened in one way or the other. The Jews, while Antiochus the Great was reigning over Asia and fighting against Ptolemy Eupator and his son Ptolemy Epiphanes, suffered severely. Antiochus won and brought Judaea over to himself. When Ptolemy Epiphanes, after his father's death, sent a great force against Coele Syria and took many cities, the nation of the Jews, under attack, went over to Epiphanes. When Antiochus won again, the Jews went over to him of their own accord, received him into the city, and helped him besiege the soldiers of Ptolemy guarding the citadel; and from then the nation was friendly to him. Then Antiochus made peace with Ptolemy and made a marriage alliance with him, giving him his daughter Cleopatra, and gave as dowry Coele Syria, Phoenicia and Judaea, and Samaria too. This happened under the high priest Onias. For when Eleazar died his uncle Manasseh succeeded him, and after him Onias was granted the high priesthood, being the son of Simon the Just. Simon was the brother of Eleazar, as has been told.

This Onias, through love of money and weakness of mind, did not pay Ptolemy the yearly tribute which those before him had paid the kings, and so roused him to anger. Ptolemy sent and threatened to treat the nation and the city badly; but the high priest, enslaved to money, was not to be moved. Joseph, the son of Tobias and the high priest's nephew, asked his uncle whether he would let him go as envoy to Ptolemy. When he allowed it, he entertained the man sent by Ptolemy most generously, gave him costly gifts and sent him on ahead, promising to follow himself. The man went back to Ptolemy, reported what had happened, told of Joseph's kindness, said that the man would come as envoy, and spoke much in his praise to the king and the queen. Joseph prepared himself and afterwards came to Alexandria. Hearing that Ptolemy was at Memphis, he went to meet him. The king happened to be sitting in a carriage with his wife, and the man whom Joseph had entertained was there too. Seeing him he said to the king: "This is the one of whom I told you that he is a good and generous young man." Hearing this, Ptolemy greeted him first and urged him to get up into the carriage. When the king blamed the high priest, he said: "Forgive him because of his age; for old men and infants have the same mind; but we young men will pay you all that is owed." From this the king's goodwill towards him grew further.

When the king was in Alexandria, the leading men of Syria, who happened to have come to buy the taxes of the cities (for the king sold them every year to the powerful men of the cities), were vexed to see Joseph sitting with him. When the powerful men of the cities were bidding eight thousand talents for the taxes of Coele Syria, Phoenicia, Judaea and Samaria, Joseph promised to give twice as much. The king consented, and asked whether he had men to stand surety for him. "I will give men," he said, "whom you will not distrust." And when he asked further who they were, he said: "I give you, king, and your wife, to stand surety." Ptolemy laughed and granted him the collection of the tributes. Taking about two thousand soldiers, he set out for Syria. At Ascalon, when they gave him nothing but even insulted him, he seized the leading men and killed them, confiscated their property and sent about a thousand talents to the king. He praised what had been done and allowed him to do whatever he wished. This struck fear into the Syrians, and they received Joseph eagerly and paid the tributes. Having made great profit from this, he sent great gifts to the king and Cleopatra and to all about them, and so enjoyed this good fortune for twenty-two years.

He married two wives, and had seven sons by one and one by the other. The latter was his niece; he married her in this way.

## 4.18

He fell in love with a dancing girl and was sick with love for her. He revealed his passion to his brother. His brother promised to serve him in his love, and at night he brought his own daughter to his brother and put her to bed with him. When this had happened many times, he loved her more violently. When his brother revealed to him what had been hidden, he married his niece, and had a son by her, whom he called Hyrcanus. When he was thirteen, he showed his understanding and his resourcefulness. His father gave him three hundred yoke of oxen and sent him out to a district two days' journey away to work the land, keeping back the straps of the yokes. When he got there and had no straps, and the ox drivers said they must send men to his father to ask for them, he paid no heed to their advice, but slaughtered ten yoke himself, distributed the meat to the workers, cut up the hides and tied the yokes on with them, and so did the work and came back. His father, learning of his ingenuity, admired him, loved him and preferred him to his other sons; and they were vexed.

When a son was born to Ptolemy, the leading men of the lands under him went to Alexandria to celebrate the birthday; and Joseph, being difficult to transport because of his age, urged one of his sons to go there. When the elder ones declined, Hyrcanus agreed to go. He advised his father not to send gifts to the king from home, but to write to his steward in Alexandria to supply him with gold, so that he might buy gifts with it. His father, praising his son for the advice, wrote to the steward of his money in Alexandria, which was not less than three thousand talents, to give his son as much money as he needed, expecting that the expense for the king's gift would not be more than ten talents. Taking the letter, Hyrcanus went off to Alexandria. Joseph's other sons wrote to their father's friends to plot against Hyrcanus and destroy him.

When he was in Alexandria he gave his father's letter to the steward. He asked how many talents he needed; and he said, "A thousand." The steward would give no more than ten. The boy, angered, put the steward in chains. Ptolemy sent to Hyrcanus, saying he wondered how he had neither shown himself to him and had chained his father's steward. He said he had not come because he was waiting to prepare the gifts, and had punished the slave for disobeying his orders. At this the king laughed and admired the boy's high spirit. Arion the steward, knowing there was no help for him, gave the thousand talents and was released from his chains. Hyrcanus bought a hundred boys in their prime who knew letters, paying a talent for each, and as many maidens for a talent each. While all the others brought the king ten talents each, and those who gave more did not go beyond twenty, Hyrcanus presented the boys and maidens to the king and Cleopatra, each of them carrying a talent too; and to the king's friends and his attendants he gave gifts of many talents. Ptolemy, admiring the boy, urged him to ask what he wished. He asked nothing but that he should write to his father and brothers about him. The king did so, gave him royal gifts and sent him off.

His brothers, learning that he was returning with honour, went out to meet him and kill him, and their father did not stop them, out of anger at the spending of the money. When his brothers attacked him, many of those with them fell, and two of them. Afraid because of this, he went off across the Jordan and made war on the Arabs, built a costly fortress there and held those parts for seven years, as long as Seleucus, the son of Antiochus the Great, ruled Syria. When he died and his brother Antiochus, called Epiphanes, succeeded to the rule, Hyrcanus, afraid of him, killed himself with his own hand; and Antiochus took his money.

## 4.19

Ptolemy Epiphanes, who ruled Egypt, also died, leaving two very young sons, one called Philometor and the other Physcon. Despising them, Antiochus marched against Egypt; but he was driven back from it, the Romans ordering him to keep away from the country. Returning from there he set out for Jerusalem, and took the city without a battle, those of his party opening the gates to him. For when Onias the high priest died, Antiochus gave the office to his brother Jeshua. Then, angry with him, he gave the high priesthood to the younger brother, called Onias. For Simon had three sons, and the high priesthood came round to all three. Jeshua renamed himself Jason, and Onias Menelaus. When Jeshua quarrelled with Onias, also called Menelaus, the people were divided too; most sided with Jason, the others with Menelaus, whom the sons of Tobias also helped. Hard pressed by the majority, these and Menelaus withdrew to Antiochus, abandoned their ancestral laws and took up Greek ways, and covered over the circumcision of their members by drawing the skin forward. When those of their party opened the gates of the city to Antiochus Epiphanes, he became master of the city, killed many, plundered much money and returned to Antioch.

Two years later Antiochus came up to Jerusalem again, and mastering the city by deceit he did not spare even those who had received him; he plundered the temple, took away the hidden treasures, stopped the lawful sacrifices, sacked the city, killed some of the people and carried others off as captives, burned the finest parts of the city, sacrificed swine in the temple, and finally pulled down the walls, built the citadel in the lower city and put a garrison of Macedonians in it. He forced the people to worship the gods he believed in and not to circumcise their children, and left men to compel them to do these things. Then Eleazar the priest and his disciples, that is, the Maccabees, with their mother, contended to the end for the keeping of the divine laws. Those overcome by force obeyed the orders; but all whose souls were noble, not yielding to the lawless commands, died bitterly under torture, or were crucified while still breathing a little; and the tyrant's servants strangled their wives and the children they had circumcised by hanging them from their necks. And if a book of the Hebrew Scripture was found anywhere, they destroyed it and put those who had it to a pitiful death. Because of this the Samaritans no longer acknowledged themselves kin to the Jews, but called themselves Sidonians, and named their temple on Gerizim the temple of Zeus Hellenius.

There was then a priest named Mattathias, the son of John, the son of Simeon, the son of Hasmonaeus, who had five sons: John called Gaddis, Simon called Thassis, Judas called Maccabaeus, Eleazar called Auran and Jonathan called Apphus. When the king's men came to their village and ordered Mattathias first, as the foremost of the others, to sacrifice in the Greek way, he refused. When another sacrificed, Mattathias, filled with zeal, with his sons killed both the man who had sacrificed and the general Apelles, who was compelling them to sacrifice, pulled down the altar and set off with his sons into the desert. Many others, emulating them, fled with their wives and children into the desert and lived in caves. Antiochus's generals gathered a force and came against them, attacked on the sabbath, and burned many in the caves who did not even defend themselves because of the day's rest. Those who escaped joined Mattathias; and he urged them to fight even on sabbaths, so that the enemy might not destroy them without effort by attacking on the sabbaths. Gathering a force, Mattathias pulled down the altars, killed those who had been impious, and ordered the uncircumcised among the children to be circumcised. After ruling for a year he fell ill, urged his sons to imitate his zeal, to die for the laws if need be and to be of one mind with one another, and having given them these charges he died.

## 4.20

His son Judas, also called Maccabaeus, took on the leadership of affairs, and with the help of his brothers and others he drove the enemy out of the country and punished those of his countrymen who had broken the law. Apollonius, the general of Samaria, marched against Judas, and he and very many of those with him fell. Learning this, the general of Cilicia came against Maccabaeus, engaged him, was routed and died, and those under him fled; and Judas, pursuing, killed many of them. Angered at this, Antiochus ordered Lysias, the steward of his affairs, to subdue Judaea, enslave the nation and raze Jerusalem. He chose three generals and sent them with a heavy force. Judas, seeing the number of the enemy, urged those with him to put their hopes in God. After dining in the evening and leaving many fires in the camp, he marched through the night and fell on Antiochus's men about dawn; he killed many in battle and pursued the rest, so that more than three thousand fell. After the enemy's defeat Judas came back and stripped their camp, and taking much booty and abundant wealth he returned home rejoicing.

The next year Lysias gathered a larger army and invaded Judaea. Judas engaged his advance guard and won. So Lysias took the rest of his force back to Antioch, and prepared to invade Judaea with a greater army. Judas went up to Jerusalem to cleanse the temple, and finding it deserted and burned in places, he lamented. Having cleansed it, he brought in new vessels, a lampstand, a table and an altar of gold, built a new altar of stones not cut with iron, burned incense, offered whole burnt offerings and set loaves on the table. This happened on the day on which the temple had been defiled and the worship profaned, three years having passed, according to the prediction of Daniel made four hundred and eight years before. And from then on the Jews celebrate the recovery of their worship, calling it Lights. He walled the city and set guards in it. When the surrounding nations destroyed many of the Jews, lying in wait for them out of spite, Judas defended them in war and held them back.

Antiochus, learning that there was a city in Persis rich in wealth, called Elymais, and in it a temple of Artemis full of costly offerings, set out against it and besieged it. Beaten off by those in the city when they came out, he fled to Babylon and lost most of his army. While Antiochus was grieved at this, the defeat of his generals by the Jews was reported to him. With this grief added, he fell ill, and knowing that he would die he called together those under him and said he suffered so because he had afflicted the nation of the Jews and plundered the temple. With these words he died, leaving Philip, one of his friends, as regent of the kingdom, and giving him the diadem and the ring to take to his son Antiochus. Lysias, learning that Antiochus was dead, declared his son king, calling him Eupator.

## 4.21

The Macedonians in the citadel of Jerusalem harmed the Jews going up to the temple to sacrifice. So Judas was eager to remove the garrison and besieged it hard. The boy Antiochus, learning this, was angry and set out with a great force against Judaea and Jerusalem. Judas met him and killed about six hundred of his advance guard. Eleazar, Judas's brother, also called Auran, seeing the tallest of the elephants armed with royal breastplates and thinking the king was riding on it, charged it with violent onset, and getting under the beast's belly struck and killed it. It fell on Eleazar, crushed him with the weight of its body and killed him. Judas, seeing the enemy's strength, went back to Jerusalem and prepared for a siege. Antiochus came to Jerusalem and besieged the sanctuary, those within defending themselves stoutly; but when their food failed them, many slipped away. But when Philip, whom the dying Antiochus had left as regent of the kingdom, was claiming the kingdom for himself, Lysias the general and Antiochus, learning this, made peace with the besieged on terms that they might keep their ancestral laws. Antiochus was received into the city, and seeing the temple very strongly fortified, he broke his oaths and razed the wall; and so he went back, taking with him the high priest Onias, who was also called Menelaus, since he had persuaded his father to force the Jews to break their ancestral customs and had been the cause of many evils. He sent him to Beroea in Syria and put him to death, after he had held the high priesthood for ten years and proved impious. Alcimus, also called Jakeim, became high priest. Antiochus made war on Philip, captured him and killed him.

Onias, the son of the high priest Simon, whom his father on dying left a child, as has been told, seeing that Antiochus had given the high priesthood to Alcimus, who did not belong to the family of the high priests, fled to Ptolemy in Egypt; being honoured, he received a place in the nome of Heliopolis, and built there a temple like the one in Jerusalem.

Demetrius, the son of Seleucus, escaping from Rome and coming to Tripolis in Syria, seized both Antiochus and Lysias and put them both to death, after Antiochus had reigned two years. The high priest Alcimus came to this Demetrius, and many wicked Jewish exiles, and accused Judas Maccabaeus and the whole nation of having killed his friends. Provoked, he sent Bacchides, his closest friend, with a very great force, ordering him to kill Judas and those with him. Coming into Judaea, he sent to Judas and his brothers inviting them to peace.
